# Known traps — "passes in the emulator, breaks on real hardware" (and other silent killers)

A catalog of the Atari 2600 traps mined from AtariAge that **do not show up as a failure in a naive
emulator run** but corrupt the result on real hardware (or in a stricter emulator). Past Pong attempts died
on exactly this class of bug ("unverified timing / positioning"). This is the **pre-flight checklist** the
authoring loop runs before shipping a kernel, and the **spec for `scripts/check_traps.py`** (the future
trap linter, [[knowledge-activation-architecture]]).

Detection column: **static** = a source-text linter can flag it · **runtime** = needs a sim assert /
`breakif` / `trace_clocks` · **manual** = judgment / pixel compare.

> **★coverage, measured 2026-07-30 — "static" says a linter COULD flag it, not that one does.** 10 rows below
> are marked static; `scripts/check_traps.py` implements **7 detectors** covering: unstable illegal opcodes,
> `LAX #imm`, `NOP $00`/`BIT $00`, variables in the `$F8-$FF` stack-collision zone, missing `CLD`/`CLEAN_START`
> (which is also the post-reset-undefined row), and **new today**, reads of a write-only TIA register and
> **`STA` to ROM**. Page-cross `+1` is handled by `cyclebound`'s abstract interpreter rather than here. Still **unimplemented**:
> bank-move page-cross misalignment. **F8/F6/F4 random boot bank is now checked at ROM level** (see below). **Read of a write-only cartridge
> hotspot / SuperChip write port is NOT statically decidable** and its row is re-marked below.
> The parenthetical below calling `check_traps.py` "the future trap linter" is stale — it exists and runs
> in CI on every push; what is future is that one row.

**New static trap (2026-07-30): reading a write-only TIA register.** The TIA answers reads only at
`$00-$0D` (`CXM0P`..`INPT5`; verified in Gopher2600 `cpubus.go TIAReadRegisters`). Everything from `$0E` (PF1)
to `$2C` (CXCLR) is write-only, so `lda GRP0` returns bus residue — which an emulator can make look stable and
repeatable while real hardware does not. Measured false positives: **0 across 123 known-good ROMs**
(31 techniques + 92 litmus), and the detector is not merely silent — the same scan matches 509 read-operand
pairs in that corpus, so it is looking at something. (That count is dated 2026-07-30; litmus has since grown to 172. `scripts/check_traps.py` now
walks `roms/techniques` plus the sibling works instead, and on 2026-09-28 found 0 across 417 files on this machine — 31 on a
harness-only checkout. Re-run on the old corpus, techniques + litmus, it reports 2, both deliberate write-only reads in
`litmus_floatbits.asm`.) Bait-tested in `--selftest`; negative control: disabling
the rule fails the selftest by name.

**A setup database can patch our ROMs, and its path is relative — measured 2026-09-05.**
`internal/emu` attaches every cartridge through `setup.AttachCartridge`, and the engine's
`setup/doc.go` says what that is for: *"Toggling of panel switches / **Apply patches to cartridge** /
Television specification ... `<DB Key>, patch, <SHA-1 Hash>, <patch file>, <notes>`"*. An entry keyed
by a ROM's SHA-1 can flip the console switches, **rewrite the cartridge's bytes**, or change the TV
standard, and `setup.go` will *"silently ignore absence of setup database"*.

★The path is not one well-known location. `resources/dev_path.go` is `//go:build !release` and
returns the **relative** string `.gopher2600`; this repository builds without the `release` tag. So
which database is consulted depends on the directory the command ran from — and `CLAUDE.md` requires
that variation: *"Run commands from each repo's root."* **Two mandated working directories, two
possible databases.**

Measured: four `.gopher2600` directories exist (umbrella root, `roms/`, `harness/`, `sandbox/`),
**all four empty**; `~/.gopher2600` does not exist. `resources.JoinPath` creates the folder just for
being asked the path, which is why they are there at all. **The mechanism is live and the data is
absent** — and absence is the only reason this has never mattered.

★★`internal/emu/setupdb_test.go` asserts the absence. The `.gitignore` files already carry
`/.gopher2600/`, which says "do not commit this" and says nothing about what it can do. Negative
control: touching one file in any of them fails the test. Found by the mailing-list distillation
(helper-2), who closed the population of engine defaults and then followed `AttachCartridge` into
this.

**`VSYNCsyncedOnStart` hides the first frames' vertical instability, and 42% of our measurement
points are inside that window — measured 2026-09-05, and it hides almost nothing.** The engine will
not move the picture's vertical origin until the frame is Stable (`stabilityThreshold = 6`), and
`VSYNCsyncedOnStart` — **default true, never set here** — is the gate. The distillation (helper-2)
closed the population of engine defaults, found five preferences named nowhere in this repository,
and counted the exposure: **722 time references across 122 scenarios, 303 of them at frame < 6**.

Measured across 192 ROMs rendered twice at six read points:

| after | differ | | after | differ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 frames | 0 | | 5 frames | **1** — `cart_f4sc` |
| 3 frames | 0 | | 6 frames | **1** — `pf_wraps` |
| 4 frames | 0 | | 8 frames | 0 |

**At most one ROM differs at any point, which one depends on the point, and by frame 8 everything
agrees.** The effect is real, transient and self-correcting; the 42% is exposure, not damage.

★The first version of this measurement read at six frames only and said "one ROM differs:
`pf_wraps`". That was **one sample of a moving quantity**, and helper-2 caught it — six is the
threshold itself, so reading there can miss what the flag does. `internal/emu/vsyncstart_test.go`
now sweeps all six points and pins the witness list at each. ★★They also predicted `cb_roll` would
differ; it does not, at any point.

★★★Family: this is the fifth entry of *"TRUE OF HARDWARE, AND NOT OF WHAT WE MEASURE WITH"* —
after `RandomState`, the random boot bank, the VSYNC threshold and SuperChip SARA. **All of them,
and nine more, are now gathered in "The configuration surface" below**, which is where to look
first; the prose entries keep the per-trap advice, the table keeps the roster.

**The 3F hotspot is a 74LS173, and that explains two things the condition alone does not, 2026-09-05.**
The engine states the condition (`addr&0x10c0 == 0x0000`, quoting alex_79) but not the circuit. Adam
Wozniak posted the wiring in 200506 — *"`/G1`→A7, `/G1`→A6, `CLK`→enable/A12 ... When A7 and A6 are
low, followed by a high transition on A12, the 74LS173 loads its flip-flops."* Two consequences follow
that the address condition does not state on its own:

- **On a 3F cartridge the TIA has to be addressed at `$40`-`$7F`, not `$00`-`$3F`.** The same
  sentence says so directly — *"This means we can't access the TIA at 00-3F, **we must use 40-7F**"* —
  and it is a stronger statement than the trap rows above. Those say *do not accidentally touch
  `$00`-`$3F`*; this says the whole low mirror is **gone** on such a cartridge, so every TIA access in
  the program has to use the higher one. `STA WSYNC` at `$02` is a bank switch; at `$42` it is a
  WSYNC. ★A ROM written for any other mapper and then rebuilt as 3F does not fail at the one clever
  line — it fails at **every** line, which is a different and much louder kind of broken, and worth
  knowing before choosing the mapper rather than after. DASM's common `vcs.h` makes the choice one
  definition: its header says `TIA_BASE_ADDRESS` *"should (externally, before including this file) be
  set to $40 when creating 3F-bankswitched (and other?) cartridges"* (version 1.01, at Eckhard
  Stolberg's suggestion), and 1.04 added `TIA_BASE_READ_ADDRESS` / `TIA_BASE_WRITE_ADDRESS` to move the
  read and the write equates separately, *"for convenient disassembly/reassembly compatibility"* —
  Manuel Rotschkar pointed a 2004 Donkey Kong rebuild at those two 〔`200404/msg00028`〕. The switch is not
  always the whole change: Dennis Debro, who had posted that disassembly, answered a post quoting Paul Slocum's report
  of a rebuild with the base at `$30`: *"If you change the base address then you might also have to change the
  ClearGameRAM routine. Right now it stores #$00 in PF1+64,x."* 〔`200404/msg00074`〕 On our reading an
  address written as arithmetic on a register name moves with the base while the memory it meant does not
  (Cited only, not verified). No ROM under
  `roms/` here includes `vcs.h` (`rg -i 'include\s+"?vcs\.h' roms` → 0), so this repository's sources
  have neither switch.
- **You cannot bankswitch from code running in RAM.** The load needs a LOW-to-HIGH transition on A12,
  and code executing out of RAM never drives A12 high. *"This also means we can't switch banks from
  code running in RAM."* — which is the constraint behind the separate `running-code-in-ram` idea of
  putting the switch routine in RAM: it works for other mappers and cannot work for this one.
- **The scheme scales by paralleling the latch**: one gives *"16 2K segments = 32K"*, two in parallel
  *"256 2K segments = 512K"*. That is where the 2003 request for *"3F images that are greater than 8K
  (up to 512 K)"* comes from — the size is not part of "3F", so a 3F ROM's size tells you nothing.

Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-1). ★Not measured here: this repository has no 3F
fixture to run it against, so the circuit is recorded as the source's, not as ours.

★★**And that last sentence has a casualty, 2026-09-07.** If the ROM's size tells you nothing, then a
bank number the cartridge cannot honour is not a compile error, not a link error, and not an emulator
failure — it is a crash on the machine and nowhere else. Eckhard Stolberg diagnosing a 2003 release on
a Cuttle Cart: *"The new version starts with `lda #$1e/sta $3f`. This would switch the CC to the 31st
(30th when counting from 0) 2K bank. But **in your 32K binary there are only 16 2K banks**. Since the
CC can address 64K in this mode, your game is **jumping into an empty bank and therefore crashes**.
After patching the `#$1e` to `#$0e` in the binary the game came up fine"* 〔`200301/msg00250`〕.
★★★**One byte, and only real hardware could say which byte.** An emulator sized to the image wraps the
high bits and survives; the cartridge, which addresses more than the image fills, does not.

★★★★**Why this is a note and not a `check_traps` rule.** The check would be "is the value written to
`$3F` within the cartridge's bank count", and **the `.asm` does not carry the cartridge's bank count** —
only the image's, which is precisely the number the source above says is meaningless. A detector built
on the image size would pass Stolberg's crash. Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2), who
cited `200301/msg00256`; that is Andrew Davie quoting it back, and the words are Stolberg's six
messages earlier.

**"Bus residue" names three different models, and this engine picks one, 2026-09-05.** The row above
says a read of a write-only register "returns bus residue", which is true and does not say *what*. The
engine's own source names two candidates and B. Watson's 200508 post names the third:

| model | what the floating bits become | who |
|---|---|---|
| the ADDRESS | bits 0-6 read as the address being read from | **Stella and z26, 2005** — *"in both emulators, the disconnected bits will read as the address being read from, so bits 0-6 will be `$2B`, which has bit 0 set"* |
| the LAST BUS BYTE | `data \|= mem.LastCPUData & ^mem.DataBusDriven` | **this engine, by default** — its comment says the pattern matches a PlusCart, and *"a different bit pattern can be seen on the Harmony"* |
| a fixed pseudo-random pattern | `data \|= Random.Rewindable(0xff) & ^mem.DataBusDriven` | this engine with `RandomPins` |

**Measured, not read off the source**: `litmus_floatbits` reads register `$0B` twice, once where the
last bus byte is the address (`lda $2B`) and once where it is not (`lda $00,X`, X=`$2B`), and the two
answers differ — bit 0 set then clear. Under the address model they would agree. So **this engine is
the last-bus-byte model and is NOT doing what Stella and z26 did.**

That matters because Eckhard Stolberg (200110) names three commercial ROMs whose behaviour turns on
it: Video Pinball's ball *"won't bounce off of the paddles properly"*, Dodge'em gets *"a reversed score
display"* if bit 0 comes back 1, and Berzerk enables a missile at `$F093`. **None of the three is in
this tree**, so the model is pinned here and the consequences are not.

**Does the difference reach a game? On the one vector we have, no.** Nicolas Olhaberry posted the
exact CPU state Haunted House reaches, because his own emulator disagreed there (200107/msg00044):
`A=$02`, carry set, `SBC $0F`. *"Both PCAE and Z26, after executing this opcode, leave the
accumulator with `$F3`... In my emu, since is subtracting zero, the carry remains set."* Measured
here: **`$F3`, carry clear** — PCAE's and z26's answer. `$0F` happens to be an address where the
two models agree (the operand byte and the address are the same value), so this is not a second
discriminator; it is the separate and more useful statement that **the model difference does not
reach this game.** Whether it reaches Video Pinball, Dodge'em or Berzerk is still unmeasured, and
will stay that way while their ROMs are not in this tree. Stolberg named a fourth in 2005, to someone whose FPGA
TIA drove the undriven bits to zero: Mountain King *"(accidently) interprets data from the TIA read
registers as pointers for the level data. If the lower bits in the read registers are set to zero, the
level data for the secret kingdom is created in such a way that there always will be some platforms in
the way when you try to jump to the entry point of the secret kingdom."* Of where the bits come from:
*"Usually the TIA collision registers are read with zeropage addressing mode, so the last data on the
bus usually is the low byte of the address"* — the case in which, as at Haunted House's `$0F`, the two
models agree, derived, **Not verified** (AtariAge `topic/67091`; Cited only, not verified). It is not in this tree either.

★And `RandomPins` does not mean unpredictable: it draws from `Random.Rewindable`, so it produces a
*different fixed* pattern, reproducible run to run. Measured — the default gives (1, 0) every time and
`RandomPins` gives (0, 1) every time. A test written expecting noise there would be wrong.

**`$00-$0D` is right about the TIA and wrong about the machine, 2026-09-04.** Those addresses answer
because the TIA decodes only the low bits — but the *page* they sit in is not exclusively the TIA's,
and something else in the address space can win. Darrell Spice Jr., 2003-08, tested **135 titles on a
Supercharger and found three broken** — Air-Sea Battle (*"shots either don't register"*), Canyon
Bomber and Code Breaker (*"screen rolls"*, differently) — and noted the same collision fault in
Haunted House, Space War and Ghost Manor. **Air-Sea Battle reads the collision registers at
`$00/$01`, not `$30/$31`.**

**Corrected 2026-09-04, hours after this row first landed, and the correction matters.** The proposed
fix — *"patching the code to use the images at `$3x` makes it work on the supercharger"*, with byte
offsets — is from **2000-08** (`200008/msg00038`), not 2003. The 2003 post quotes it in full **and
then reports that it does not work**: *"**I tried this, but no luck.** Running Air-Sea battle thru
Distella it looks like the `004a` should have been `024a`, but changing that didn't help either."*
This file cited the refutation as if it were the confirmation.

**Read collisions at `$30/$31` anyway** — but for reasons that survive the retraction, because the
"it fixes Air-Sea Battle" claim does not. The engine folds TIA reads to the low nibble
(`maskReadTIA = 0x000f`) and `$00-$2F` overlaps the write side; neither depends on a Supercharger
being present, and the two mirrors are identical on a bare console. **So the rule stands and the
evidence for it does not include a working repair.** What is actually established: a shipped game
reads through `$0x`, and it is one of three in 135 that a Supercharger breaks. Why remains open —
the 2000 reporter says "maybe", the 2003 reporter could not reproduce the cure, and nobody has
checked whether ROM revision, cartridge individual or modification accounts for the difference.
★**A 1998 post answers the "why", and it is the bus-residue row further up this same file.**
Bradford Mott — Stella's author — diagnosing Haunted House three years before either row was written:
*"it seems to be because Haunted House is reading and using the values from undefined locations in the
TIA. The values read from these locations change depending on 'something' … So when Haunted House is
loaded into a SC the values the cartridge reads isn't the same as if it was a real cartridge and thus
you get the strange behavior. Something similar happens with **Warlords** I believe"* 〔`199801/msg00272`,
1998-01〕. Two consequences. **Warlords is a seventh name**, not among the six the 2003 survey lists.
And **the mechanism is not specific to the Supercharger** — it is that the undriven bits are an
electrical property of whatever hardware is answering, so ANY substitute cartridge can differ.
★**That is also why an emulator cannot see it.** All three residue models below are DETERMINISTIC
given the ROM; real bus capacitance is not. The false green is structural, not a missing feature.
★★**Not yet separated here:** a probe reading `$00`/`$0100`/`$0200`/`$0D00` returns `$00`/`$01`/`$02`/
`$0D`, which fits the ADDRESS model and the LAST-BUS-BYTE model equally, because in absolute addressing
the last byte fetched IS the address's high byte. Telling the two apart needs a read whose preceding
bus byte is not part of its own address. Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-1).
**One of those three now has a name.** The "modification" is not hypothetical: *"Bob's hardware
modification disables all bankswitches. It is meant to be used only with ROM dumps of actual carts.
Some of those accidently access the address which triggers the bankswitching and therefore crash. It
is limited to 2k and 4k games"* 〔`199706/msg00047`, Eckhard Stolberg, 1997-06 — the same post gives
the control byte at `$FFF8`, write-only, and the read-then-compare idiom that drives it〕. So a
Supercharger in the field can be running with bankswitching disabled, and a 2K or 4K ROM that brushes
the hotspot behaves differently on two machines that are both "a Supercharger".
★**This is a variable, not an explanation.** Air-Sea Battle's symptom is a collision read through
`$00`/`$01`, not a `$FFF8` access, so the modification is not shown to be its cause — what changes is
that "modification" is no longer a word standing in for something nobody has looked at. Found by the
mailing-list distillation (helper-1). **This is the false-green
direction** — the emulator passes it and a real configuration does not — the same side as the F8
hotspot decoding elsewhere in this file. Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2).

**New static trap (2026-07-30): `STA` into cartridge ROM.** The write is discarded, so a program that treats a
ROM address as storage reads back whatever was assembled there. Bank-switch hotspots and the SuperChip write
port are the legitimate exceptions, and both are deliberate — so intent is **declared, not inferred**: a line
carrying `@rom-write-ok` is allowed, anything else is an ERROR. Measured over 123 ROMs: exactly **2** stores
into cartridge space exist, both in `litmus_6502`, both aiming at ROM on purpose to time `STA abs,X` across a
page boundary (its own comment already said so). Those two lines now carry the declaration — comment-only, ROM
hash `7c16b7f…` identical before and after — and the corpus is clean. Negative control: removing one
declaration brings the ERROR back.

**Re-marked 2026-07-30: read of a write-only hotspot / SuperChip write port is not static.** What makes a
read of `$F000-$F07F` a trap is the cartridge MAPPING RAM there, and the mapper is not in the `.asm`
text — the same source line is ordinary code on a plain 4K image. Measured over the 123 ROMs the trap
linter scans: exactly **one** maps cartridge RAM (`litmus_superchip`), and the only source line reading
into that range (`litmus_6502.asm:52`, `lda $F010,x`) belongs to a plain 4K ROM where it is legitimate.
A naive static rule would score **1 false positive and 0 true positives**. The check belongs at ROM level,
where `emu.MapsCartridgeRAM` answers first; filed, not built. `internal/emu.TestCartridgeRAMIsRareAndNamed`
guards the premise — it fails if a second ROM starts mapping RAM (the trap becomes reachable and this row
needs re-deciding) or if `litmus_superchip` stops (cyclebound's SD-8c decline loses its witness).

**Checked at ROM level 2026-07-30: F8/F6/F4 random boot bank.** The console's power-on bank is undefined, so a
bank-switched cartridge must end up in the same place whichever bank it wakes in. This is decidable from the
IMAGE (unlike the write-port row above): read each bank's reset vector at `$FFFC` and look for a bank-select
hotspot in the stub. `internal/emu.TestEveryBankCanBeBootedInto` does it for every multi-bank ROM.
**The obvious rule is wrong, and measuring caught it before the check shipped.** "Every bank's stub must
select" flags `litmus_superchip`, which is correct — its bank 1 does `lda $FFF8` and jumps, and bank 0, the
destination, has no reason to select itself. `banked_game` selects in both banks, which is merely redundant.
So the rule is **at most ONE bank may omit the select**, and byte-identical banks are exempt outright.
Measured: 10 multi-bank ROMs, all pass (9 select in every bank, `litmus_superchip` in 1 of 2). Negative
controls: the too-strict rule fails `litmus_superchip` by name; blinding the hotspot scan fails all 10.

> Provenance: every row cites the mined thread(s) it came from. Raw notes in `reference/atariage/<id>-*/notes.ja.md`.

## The configuration surface — what our answer depends on besides the ROM

Every row here is TRUE OF HARDWARE AND NOT OF WHAT WE MEASURE WITH, or the reverse. They are not
bugs in Gopher2600; they are choices, and the choice is ours because nothing in this repository
sets any of them. This table exists because the family kept being discovered one member at a time
— `RandomState`, the random boot bank, the VSYNC threshold, SuperChip SARA, `VSYNCsyncedOnStart` —
each in a different section, each read as a one-off. **They are one population, and it is closed.**

Counted 2026-09-05 (`rg 'func .*SetDefaults' Gopher2600 --include='*.go' | rg -v _test`): `SetDefaults`
exists in **12** functions and all twelve have been opened. Four are hardware (`preferences.go`,
`cartridge_preferences.go`, `television.go`, `revision_preferences.go`) and hold **17** values; a
fifth file, `television/colourgen/colourgen.go`, holds **14** more that every pixel passes through
— **31** in all. The 17 are accounted for exactly: 2 + 2 + 5 + 8, and rows 1–7 and 9 below name all
of them. Of the remaining seven `SetDefaults`, **two reach outside this machine** and are rows 13–14;
the other five are the ARM coprocessor, the disassembly listing, rewind granularity, and two for the
GUI, which a headless run does not build.

**Three kinds, and they need different answers:**

| kind | what it is | what to do |
|---|---|---|
| **A — a switch that is off** | the engine models the behaviour and is not asked to | flip it and MEASURE the difference; the size of the difference is the finding |
| **B — no model at all** | the engine cannot show it however it is configured | document it; a litmus here can only measure our own settings |
| **C — something outside the repository** | a file, or a network, changes the answer | assert its absence, or read it and record what it said |

| # | Setting / mechanism | Default | Kind | Measured? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `RandomState` — INTIM and all 128 bytes of RAM | **false** (read in 18 places) | A | partly — the consequence is recorded (§C), the difference is not |
| 2 | F8/F6/F4 boot bank (`Random.Intn`, gated by 1) | fixed `Loader.Bank` | A | no — a litmus would measure our setting |
| 3 | `VSYNCscanlines` — the "frame is synced" threshold | **2** | A | no |
| 4 | `EmulateSARA` — SuperChip phantom reads | **false** | A | no |
| 5 | `VSYNCsyncedOnStart` — hides vertical drift before `stabilityThreshold` (**6**) frames | **true** | A | **yes, 2026-09-05** — 192 ROMs × 6 read points: at most **1** differs at any point, and by frame 8 all agree. Transient and self-correcting. 42% of our measurement points sit inside the window: exposure, not damage |
| 6 | `RandomPins` — floating-bus model | **false** | A | yes — used as a negative control in `internal/emu/floatbits_test.go` |
| 7 | The eight TIA-revision flags | **all false** | A | **yes** — 7 of 31 technique ROMs change under them; the catalogue table listed only 18 of the 31, now gated by `check_wiring.py` |
| 8 | `colourgen`: `LegacyEnabled` **true** plus 13 adjustment values (Brightness 1.196, Saturation 0.963, NTSCPhase 0.0; the non-legacy set differs on all three) | legacy generator | A | **no — and the existing guard cannot catch it**: `PaletteFor` and `HarvestPalette` BOTH go through `Spec.GetColor`, so a generator change moves both twins and `TestHarvestedPaletteEqualsDerivedPalette` stays green. So does C1==0 on the five playfield-only ROMs. **Closed 2026-09-05**: `internal/ceiling/palettegolden_test.go` pins the 128 measured RGB triples and checks BOTH paths against them instead of against each other. Negative control: moving `Saturation` from 0.963 to 0.500 shifts `$46` from `[236 51 51]` to `[157 69 69]` and fails the golden while the twin test still reports `ok` (probe applied, measured, reverted) |
| 9 | `VSYNCimmedateSync` / `HaltChangedVBLANK` / `HaltChangedVSYNC` / `UnwrapACE` | false/false/false/true | A | **no — not named anywhere in this repository before this table** |
| 10 | **PAL colour-loss** (odd scanline count → the set goes black and white) | — | **B** | Gopher2600 has **no implementation at all** — not a preference that is off, absent. Stella has the setting; it was off by default in 2011 and its maintainer said a developer should always have it on. Our own trap row for this warns the author about something our instrument cannot show 〔atariage 190917〕 |
| 11 | The composite waveform — sync separation, DC reference. **A missing layer, not a setting** | — | **B** | our television takes a digital sync flag, so no VBLANK-interferes-with-sync effect can appear here 〔stella-list "262 scanline kernel is rolling"; atariage 113732, 126450, 254640, 189414〕 |
| 12 | `setupDB` + `patches/` — a SHA-1-keyed database that can toggle the panel, PATCH THE CARTRIDGE BYTES, or change the TV spec | absent here | **C** | `setup.AttachCartridge` consults it on every load and the engine's own comment says it will "silently ignore absence of setup database". In a dev build the path is **relative**, so which database is consulted depends on the directory the command ran from. Measured 2026-09-05: **38** `.gopher2600` directories exist under the umbrella, **all empty** — 32 of them in Go package directories, because `go test` runs each package with the package directory as its working directory and `resources.JoinPath` mints the folder just for being asked the path. Asserted by `internal/emu/setupdb_test.go`, which **walks** rather than listing: an earlier version listed four roots and reported "four" |
| 13 | **PlusROM outbound HTTP** | no off switch | **C** | `plusrom/network.go` POSTs to a URL carried in the ROM itself (`http.NewRequest("POST", …)`), and no preference disables it — the gate is a **fingerprint**. `cartridge/fingerprint.go`'s `fingerprintPlusROM` searches the WHOLE cartridge image for the bytes of `STA $1FF1` (`8d f1 1f`); the comment says an earlier version searched only the first 1024 bytes and missed a real PlusROM after it. A false positive is caught one step later — `NewPlusROM()` validates the URL — so the byte match alone does not transmit. Measured 2026-09-05: **0 of 1157 `.bin` files** under the umbrella carry the sequence, so the second check is never even reached. Re-run the three-byte scan if the corpus grows |
| 14 | `FestivalEnabled` (AtariVox speech) — can launch an **external binary** | **true** | **C** | **not measured.** Same family as 13: an engine default that reaches outside the process. Whether any path in our headless use ever reaches it is unknown |

**The general rule.** An emulator implements the specification; what is not in the specification
cannot be modelled, only lost. **Kind A is not that** — kind A is in the specification and switched
off, so it is measurable today and the measurement is cheap. Do not write "hardware does X" from a
run that never asked the engine to model X.

**Provenance.** Rows 1–4 and 10–12 were found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2), who
closed the population of `SetDefaults` and then followed `AttachCartridge` and `fingerprint.go` out
of it; rows 5, 7 and the counts in 12 and 13 were measured here. Rows 13 and 14 are the two that
leave the machine, and only 13 has been counted.

## A. Timing / sync (the Pong killers)

| trap | what happens | fix | detect | source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **the TIA manual's "one line after" for VDELx** | It reads as a mechanism and is a consequence. The manual says the second graphics bit is loaded *"one line after the first was loaded from the data bus"*; the registers actually **watch for writes to GRPx and never look at the scanline counter**. Under the era's idiom — write GRP0 and GRP1 every other line, as Combat does — the two are the same thing, which is why the wording survived. Do anything else and the delay tracks your writes, not your lines. | Think in writes, not lines: a VDELx object changes when GRP1 is next written. Ours agrees with the writes reading: `ball.go`'s `setEnableDelay` is called from exactly two places, both `case cpubus.GRP1`, and nothing in that file reads a scanline counter (its two mentions of the word are drawing comments). | No fixture yet — write GRPx at an irregular cadence and show the delay follows the writes. Source semantics are not a measurement. | 〔stella `200506/msg00063`, `msg00070`〕; engine read 2026-09-03 |
| **a subroutine called mid-line** | Whichever branch it takes decides whether it spills into the next line, so the frame grows by the case rather than by the code. Only two things make a path change a frame's length: **crossing a line boundary** or **exceeding 76 cycles** — path length alone does not, and `litmus_pf_async` proves it, holding 262 with bands 7 cycles apart (`cyc 40` vs `cyc 33`). | Give the call a line of its own: `sta WSYNC` before it as well as after. **Bracketing is not always enough** — `litmus_flicker_attrib` went 260/261 mixed, then 263 in three groups and 264 in the fourth *with* brackets, because one routine's group-2 path was longer and still spilled. A routine whose length varies by case needs its own line, so it was split in two. | `frame_lines_stable` (∀ over frames). **`ntsc_frame_lines` samples ONE frame and passed both broken versions.** | measured 2026-09-03 |
| **RIOT timer wraparound write** | writing `TIM64T`/`TIM1024T` on the exact wraparound cycle silently drops the divider to **1T** → frame length collapses → rolls on real HW, Stella passes. ★The thread `202736` in the source column is a cycle table of the whole timer, and parts of it are measured here in `docs/fundamentals-audit.md` (the RIOT timers entry: `litmus_timer`, `litmus_timint_pa7`) — the 1-per-cycle count after the wrap, `INTIM` reads clearing `TIMINT`, and a `TIMINT` read clearing D6 (`roms/litmus/scenarios/timint_pa7.json`, *"reading TIMINT clears the PA7 flag"*). Three points are not measured here, as the distilled notes record the thread: the timer holding 0 for one interval before it wraps (documented-only in that entry); reading `TIMINT` leaving D7 set, which only an `INTIM` read or a timer write clears; and D6 sometimes found set at power-up on a console, where this engine sets it on every reset (that entry's `$40` at boot) (AtariAge `topic/202736`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified) | **double-write** the timer; or never write on the wrap cycle | runtime (`breakif` timer-write@wrap) | 303277, 316035, 327383, 202736, 323670 |
| **`TIM1024T` then `TIM64T` does not add up: there is one timer** | Piero Cavina, 1997, proposed timing the picture the way VBLANK is timed — *"start a timer for 192*76 cycles"* — and, since *"TIM64 isn't enough for so many cycles"*, to *"use TIM1024 followed by TIM64"*. Nick Bensema: *"I don't know if TIM64 could conceivably "add on" to TIM1024"*. Neither 1997 message answers it; Erik Mooney, to a different asker in 1999, does: *"There's only one timer; how fast it counts depends on which register you write to."* So the second write starts a new count at the new rate rather than adding to the first — our reading of his answer, Cited only, not verified. The premise does not hold either: 192 × 76 = 14,592 cycles is 228 intervals of 64, inside the 255 a timer write can hold — arithmetic, Not verified. | one write of the interval that covers the span (about 228 to `TIM64T` for 192 lines, by the arithmetic above) | manual | stella-list `199703/msg00225` (Piero Cavina), `199703/msg00226` (Nick Bensema), `199908/msg00083` (Erik Mooney) |
| **a wait for `INTIM` = 0 exits one whole interval early, and `T1024T` makes the interval visible** | Dennis Debro, 2002, timed a 192-line kernel with `TIM64T` set to 228 (*"192 scan lines * 76 cycles / 64 ~ 228"*) and counted 262 lines in Z26; with `T1024T` set to 14 he counted 246. Eckhard Stolberg: *"I suppose you are checking if INTIM has reached zero, when you want to know if the timer has expired, right? The T1024T timer will be in this state for 1024 cycles, so your "waiting to expire" loop might catch it way too early. This would explain the 16 lines difference. You just need to increase your timer value by one, and the timer loop should work as you expected."* No reply in the thread says whether that fixed it. The early exit is one interval long: 64 cycles is less than one 76-cycle line, which on our reading is why the `TIM64T` count came out right, while 1024 cycles is about 13.5 lines. The 14 is also short by itself (14 × 1024 = 14,336 against 192 × 76 = 14,592, about 3.4 lines), and the two together come near the 16 — arithmetic, Not verified. The hold at 0 is the "timer holding 0 for one interval before it wraps" in the wraparound row above, documented-only here (Cited only, not verified). | add one to the count, as Eckhard says; the larger the interval, the more the 0 costs | manual | stella-list `200209/msg00149` (Dennis Debro), `200210/msg00003` (Eckhard Stolberg) |
| **HMOVE then HMxx within 24 cy** | writing any `HMxx` within 24 CPU cycles after an `HMOVE` strobe → unpredictable motion on HW. ★The reason for a window at all, as alex_79 put it in 2017: *"The effect of strobing HMOVE is not immediate; instead, a series of "motion pulses" are generated (one every 4 TIA clocks) and each of them moves the sprite by one pixel. Not only that, but those motion pulses only cause motion if they occurr during the horizontal blank (the first 68 TIA clocks on each scanline, which correspond to the first 22,66 CPU cycles)"*, so the `HMxx` registers *"must not be modifyed … before HMOVE finishes its operastions"* (his spellings). The largest move, 15 pulses (§B), is 60 colour clocks = 20 CPU cycles by that arithmetic — Not verified. ★**Both halves of the rule are also broken on purpose.** SpiceWare, answering a thread where it had just been broken by accident (`sta HMOVE / lda #0 / sta HMP1 ;TOO SOON!!!`): *"Hitting the HMxx registers at the wrong time leads to stars"*, *"while hitting HMOVE at the wrong time can eliminate the black bars that normally occur when repositioning objects mid-screen"* (AtariAge `topic/274546`; Cited only, not verified). The second is the cycle-73/74 strobe in the next row. The first is Cosmic Ark's starfield — Eckhard Stolberg, 1997: *"The trick is to do a HMOVE command, then wait 18 cycles and finally write a different value into HMM0 again"* 〔`199705/msg00024`〕. ★**The window is not only for `HMxx`.** Thomas Jentzsch, 1999, asked why River Raid's routine for a player at the far left strobes `RESP1` at cycle 25 and corrects it with an extra `HMOVE` of 6 pixels; Eckhard Stolberg: *"That's because of the HMOVE command right after the fist WSYNC. If you access RESxx or HMxx within 24 cycles after a HMOVE command, you might get different positions for the objects than normal."* (his spelling). Jentzsch had not found that in the big-file or the archives, and Stolberg widened it: *"It happens with all registers that affect the internal positioning counters, like RESxx, HMxx and HMCLR."* 〔stella-list `199908/msg00090`, `199908/msg00095`, `199908/msg00104`, `199908/msg00105`〕 (Cited only, not verified). His 2002 answer on how z26 handles a `RESxx` in the window is in §D's row on emulator agreement. `cmd/timinglint`'s static check looks for `HMxx`/`HMCLR` writes in the window and not for `RESxx` (`internal/cyclebound/timinglint.go`, read from the source). ★**Writes on both sides of a late `HMOVE` were reported to go wrong too.** In a 2005 thread started by cd-w, a kernel stored `HMM0` at cycle 72, strobed `HMOVE` at 75, and wrote `ENAM0` at 2 and `NUSIZ0` at 9 of the next line; the missile was misplaced, for some values the notes record the TIA as caught in an endless loop, the same happened on a console through a Supercharger, and moving the `HMOVE` to cycle 76 did not cure it. The notes name no single write as the cause; their advice is to keep `HMxx`, `ENAxx` and `NUSIZx` writes from crowding the cycles around `HMOVE` (AtariAge `topic/67525`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). ★**Smooth in one emulator, jittery on the console.** Dennis Debro, 2002, after running his game from a Cuttle Cart: *"My horizontal movement is not smooth on the real console. When I run this in Z26 the motion is smooth and just like I want it. On the real console I'm getting jittery motion from the obstacles and the main player jumps back and forth horizontally."* Next day, from z26's log: *"I was setting HMP1 to #$00 on the 20th cycle. The Stella Programming Guide mentions that these registers should not be modified for at least 24 cycles after HMOVE. I repositioned the call until later and now the sprites move smoothly"* 〔`200210/msg00122`, `200210/msg00143`〕. So that emulator's smooth picture did not show this trap (Cited only, not verified). | keep ≥24 cy between HMOVE and the next HMxx write — by Stolberg's account the next `RESxx` too; by one 2005 report, do not crowd `ENAMx`/`NUSIZx` writes around a late `HMOVE` either | runtime (trace_clocks) | 307641; AtariAge `topic/271313` (alex_79), `topic/274546` (SpiceWare), `topic/67525` (cd-w); stella-list `199705/msg00024`; `199908/msg00090`, `msg00095`, `msg00104`, `msg00105` (Thomas Jentzsch, Eckhard Stolberg); `200210/msg00122`, `200210/msg00143` (Dennis Debro) |
| **mid-line HMOVE / cycle-73-74 strobe** | the "no black comb bar" trick (HMOVE at cy73/74) only moves **left**, and `WSYNC`-then-`HMOVE` becomes unusable; behaviour differs by TIA revision. **The strobe cycle is a whole axis, not a point** (`199804/msg00198`, Bradford Mott's 1998 full-line sweep, reproduced here 2026-09-07): **cycles 21..54 are a dead band** where no nibble moves anything, the HM=0 column reaches −5 at cycles 69–70, **cycles 73 and 74 are identical rows** so the window is two cycles wide, and **cycle 75 re-raises the comb and flips $8..$F positive** — overshooting by one costs twice. ★**An emulator version can pass a late strobe that a newer one does not.** eshu, 2010, on upgrading Stella: *"last time I upgraded I realised I had a bunch of mucked up HMOVEs that were doing what I wanted (but wouldn't have worked on a real machine) on the old version"* — the cause, *"hitting HMOVE one cycle late"*; stephena: *"Handling of 'illegal' HMOVEs was reworked for the 3.0 release onwards, making the emulation more accurate as compared to real hardware"* (AtariAge `topic/170901`; Cited only, not verified — no console run is reported, "a real machine" is eshu's judgement). ★**Two HMOVEs in a row, the first at cycle 74, worked in Z26 and moved nothing on a console.** Paul Slocum, 2003, hiding the HMOVE bars by striking `HMOVE` on every line, with the cycle-74 strobe on lines that had to shift players left by 8: *"Since a 74th cycle HMOVE doesn't generate the bars, I thought maybe I could do two HMOVEs in a row to also get the bars. This worked great on Z26, but unfortunately it doesn't work on the real thing. On a real Atari, the second HMOVE cancels the first one and the players don't move at all."* The source he posted later does not contain the pair (no `@74` HMOVE in it is followed by a second `HMOVE`), so at which cycle the second strobe landed is not known, and no reply takes it up (stella-list `200304/msg00211`, `200304/msg00216`; Cited only, not verified). ★**On one Jr the five objects did not move together.** John Saeger — a post sent to the list in 1999 whose inner header is dated 24 April 1998, the day of Mott's request 〔`199804/msg00198`〕 — changed Bradford Mott's measuring program to show P0, M1, P1, BL and M0 at once: *"On my classic VCS, they do. But on my JR, they don't. Although I didn't count pixels, the ball and missile 0 seem to work like the classic. But the players and missile 1 seem to be marching to a different drummer..."* 〔stella-list `199906/msg00028`〕 Mott had asked others to build the missile and ball tables, *"(hopefully it'll be the same)"*. One console of each kind, judged by eye (Cited only, not verified) | use only if you accept left-only; pin TIA revision when verifying, and name the engine version with the result | runtime + manual | 183219, 210361, 162520, 133205, 280660, 319456, 170901; stella-list `200304/msg00211`, `200304/msg00216` (Paul Slocum); `199906/msg00028` (John Saeger), `199804/msg00198` (Bradford Mott) |
| **the `HMxx` table shifts by eight under a cycle-73/74 `HMOVE` — `$00` moves, `$80` holds still** | Omegamatrix, 2015: *"just know the constants for hmxx are different for early hmoves"*, *"With early hmoves hmclr will not be no motion. You need to use $80 for no motion."* He posted both tables. The ordinary one runs `LEFT_7 = $70` … `NO_MOTION = $00` … `RIGHT_8 = $80`; the *"73/74 cycle HMxx"* one runs `LEFT74_15 = $70` … `LEFT74_8 = $00` … `NO_MO_74 = $80` — the same sixteen nibbles, each eight colour clocks further left. So `HMCLR`, which writes zeros, leaves every object set to move 8 colour clocks left at such a strobe. Bradford Mott's 1998 sweep agrees at the zero nibble: the HM=0 column that `internal/emu/hmovemid_cycles_test.go` carries reads −8 at cycles 73 and 74. That is the table's figure; the test measures only the strobes its ROM lands at, cycles 27, 51 and 70. What the engine does measure at the end of the line is the same eight: `internal/emu/hmoveside_test.go`'s `TestHmoveSideLateHmoveAddsEight` strobes `HMOVE` at about cycle 74 with `HMP0 = $10` and gets nine colour clocks left per strobe, the nibble's one plus eight. `$00` and `$80` themselves are not measured here. Omegamatrix again: *"Just be aware that some consoles don't like early HMOVE's. I found this out while doing the Dr. Who Berzerk hack"* — which consoles is not said (AtariAge `topic/246239`; Cited only, not verified). | at a cycle-73/74 strobe write `$80` for no motion and read every other value eight colour clocks further left; do not `HMCLR` before it expecting nothing to move. `design-principles.md` gives the same `$80` rule for this idiom | runtime: `read_tia`'s `HmovedPixel` after the strobe | AtariAge `topic/246239` (Omegamatrix); stella-list `199804/msg00198` (Bradford Mott, as carried in `hmovemid_cycles_test.go`) |

Andrew Davie: *"your $80 value is rapidly incremented' (there is no top limit) over multiple frames"*, and the sprite loop counted on it *"draws (say) 200+ scanlines"*. The author traced it to Y = 0 instead — *"the loop to draw the sprite cannot handle if the y position is 0"* — and stopped Y going below 1; Davie: *"It will ALSO fail when Y is (say) 255"* — *"You appear to have fixed the symptom, not the problem"* (AtariAge `topic/265967`; Cited only, not verified). Thomas Jentzsch, 2018, on a game that jittered on a live stream although *"the scanline count is perfectly stable in Stella"*: *"your VSYNC code. It doesn't last full three scanlines. … Add a WSYNC before the first VSYNC."* Five years later another author, the same stream, the same fix: *"I had tested it on Stella and on my Atari Jr. with the Harmony cartridge … The problem was that my VSYNC was not synchronized on every frame; I added a WSYNC before the VSYNC"* — **so one console passing is not enough either**. marcoj, 2023, after Kovi Kovi rolled on the same show, measured it with Stella's `trap VSYNC`: *"between frames the Scan Cycle varied in a sequence of 66, 51, 63, 49, 63, 51 ... etc."* and *"With the fix, the Scan cycle became 3, 3, 3, 3, 3...etc which is synchronised"*, the fix being *"a "STA WSYNC" command directly before the VSYNC write"* (AtariAge `topic/349506`; Cited only, not verified). A shipped game has it: SpiceWare traced Battlezone misbehaving on a video upscaler to a `VSYNC` raised at scan cycle 64 and held just over two lines (Cited only, not verified). ★**The same fault as a typo.** A beginner's frame wrote `sta VSYNC` four times where `sta VSYNC` and three `sta WSYNC` were meant; RevEng: *"so you turn on VSYNC, draw 3 lines with it on, and then turn it off"*. Both forms assemble, and VSYNC stayed on for a few cycles instead of three lines. The report was rolling and flicker in Stella *"whenever I introduce anything horizontal"*, while *"A plain background with no horizontal images works fine"* (AtariAge `topic/187990`; Cited only, not verified). ★**And the same shortfall after a careful reading.** svolli, 2022, who had written his first 2600 program from scratch out of the original Stella programming manual: *"My first code just didn't work, and frustrated after hours of not figuring out why, I switched to modifying example code to finally get some sort of result. Weeks later it turned out, that I missed a line the docs stating that I needed three lines for a sync, I just did one."* (AtariAge `topic/331794`; Cited only, not verified). ★**Named in 2003, and not tested then.** Thomas Jentzsch, on three homebrews that misbehaved on a 2600 Jr (and, later in the thread, a 4-switch), of the frame `lda #2 / sta VSYNC / sta WSYNC / sta WSYNC / sta WSYNC / lda #0 / sta VSYNC`: *"doesn't work 100%, because the first line may be hit much to late"* (his spelling) 〔stella-list `200302/msg00267`〕. Dennis Debro: *"The VSYNCs in Skeleton don't always happen immediately after a WSYNC and the last VSYNC in 'Qb' doesn't happen immediately after WSYNC but I wouldn't think it would matter that much"* 〔`200302/msg00269`〕; the thread turned to the consoles' TIA and never tried it (Cited only, not verified). ★**A VCR can be stricter than the television.** Carl Forhan, 1999, taping games from a TV/VCR combo: *"Edtris and TPS would not tape properly. Oystron did, and so did Submarine Commander."* — *"they kept scrolling like they're out of sync"*. Eckhard Stolberg: *"In VCS games doing the sync signal properly is left entirely to the programmer. Oystron does a WSYNC right before it turns on the VSYNC signal and then does three more WSYNCs before it turns VSYNC off again. This way it makes sure that the signal is left on for three full scanlines. TPS doesn't do the first WSYNC. Therefore it might actually generate the vertical sync signal only for two and a half scanlines or so. This might be good enough for your TV, but not good enough for the record feature of your VCR."* 〔stella-list `199907/msg00149`, `199907/msg00162`〕 That is his reading; Edtris's code is not discussed and no retaping is reported (Cited only, not verified). ★**A PAL 7800 showed it as colour coming and going.** Eckhard Stolberg, 2001, running Andrew Davie's Qb v0.03: frames were occasionally a line too long or too short, and on his PAL 7800 *"the colours disappear for a while occasionally and then come back for a couple of frames"*; z26's `-o` switch showed the same effect, but on the 7800 *"the (dis)appearance of colours is just the opposite of what it is on the emulator. This usually means that VSYNC gets turned on in the middle of a scanline, which z26 dosn't emulate properly. You can fix it by doing the accesses to VSYNC directly after a WSYNC."* 〔stella-list `200102/msg00171`〕 The `VSYNC` sentence is his reading of why the 7800 looked opposite to z26, on a 7800 and not a 2600. Two days later, in the v0.04 thread, he reported the cause he found: *"BTW, I figured out why Qb wouldn't sync on my 7800. The timer for the display part of the screen is too small, so that it is already expired before your display routine ends. A timer value of 233 gave me a stable and colourfull display"* 〔stella-list `200102/msg00217`〕 — the fix he reports is the timer, not a `WSYNC` before `VSYNC` (Cited only, not verified). ★**This repository's own litmus ROMs are written in the 2003 shape above.** Of the 172 `roms/litmus/*.asm`, 169 write `VSYNC`, and in none of them is the source line before a `sta VSYNC` a `sta WSYNC`, except `sta VSYNC,x` in `litmus_jsr_display.asm`'s `PeriCall`, a deliberate indexed store and not the frame's sync: `awk 'FNR==1{p=""} {l=tolower($0); sub(/;.*/,"",l); sub(/^[ \t]+/,"",l); sub(/[ \t]+$/,"",l); if(l=="")next; if(l~/^sta[ \t]+vsync/){n++; if(p~/^sta[ \t]+wsync/)w++} p=l} END{print n, w+0}' roms/litmus/*.asm` → `339 1` (2026-10-02). All 169 hold `sta WSYNC` three times and `lda #0` inside the window, the next row's shape too: `rg -U -l -i 'sta\s+VSYNC\b[^\n]*\n(\s*sta\s+WSYNC\b[^\n]*\n){3}\s*lda\s+#0\b[^\n]*\n\s*sta\s+VSYNC\b' roms/litmus/*.asm` → 169 files. What runs before the raising write in a frame was not traced, the engine runs these ROMs as tests, and what the shape does on a console is not measured here (Not verified). | `sta WSYNC` immediately before `sta VSYNC`, then three whole lines before clearing it. That is the shape of Manuel Polik's `VERTICAL_SYNC` macro, 2003 — `LDA #$02` / `STA WSYNC` (*"Finish current line"*) / `STA VSYNC` / two `STA WSYNC` / `LSR` / `STA WSYNC` / `STA VSYNC` — which Andrew Davie put into the DASM distribution's 2600 companion files 〔stella-list `200306/msg00086`, `200306/msg00091`〕. Its `LSR` sits inside the window, which the advice in the row "anything but `WSYNC` between the two `VSYNC` writes" avoids, and by 2005 `macro.h` held a different sequence (the row "Where on the line `VBLANK` or `VSYNC` is written can decide whether a real set rolls" in §D) (Cited only, not verified) | runtime: `beamtrace` gives the beam clock of both `VSYNC` writes. **The engine will not fail it by itself**: it counts a frame synced at `VSYNCscanlines` = **2** (§E), below the three the fix restores — Not verified against a fixture | AtariAge `topic/209800` (Thomas Jentzsch), `topic/352788`, `topic/320280` and `topic/325492` (SpiceWare, Battlezone); `topic/187990` (RevEng); `topic/331794` (svolli); stella-list `200302/msg00267`, `msg00269` (Thomas Jentzsch, Dennis Debro); AtariAge `topic/349506` (marcoj); stella-list `199907/msg00149`, `msg00162` (Carl Forhan, Eckhard Stolberg); `200102/msg00171`, `msg00217` (Eckhard Stolberg); `200306/msg00086` (Manuel Polik), `msg00091` (Andrew Davie) |
| **`WSYNC` where `VSYNC` was meant: the same typo the other way round** | The row above has `sta VSYNC` written where `sta WSYNC` was meant. arwanderer, 2023, on the first demo in a book, once `vcs.h` was included: *"the code compiled, and I received a flashing pink screen"*, and then *"I had a "WSYNC" where a "VSYNC" should have been. After fixing that little mistake, I got a blue screen which is what the "demo" exercise in the book was supposed to do."* The two names differ by one letter, both are TIA write addresses, and the swapped source assembled (AtariAge `topic/354662`; Cited only, not verified). | Read each `VSYNC` and `WSYNC` line for what it is meant to do: `VSYNC` set for three lines, `WSYNC` to end one. | Nothing here flags the swap; `ntsc_frame_lines` and `frame_lines_stable` see the frame it makes. | AtariAge `topic/354662` (arwanderer) |
| **anything but `WSYNC` between the two `VSYNC` writes — reported to roll some televisions** | Three whole lines of `VSYNC` were not enough on one PAL set when the program did other work inside them. Eckhard Stolberg, 1997, to Erik Mooney about an RPG kernel: *"it produces a rolling screen on my PAL TV. It seems as if my old TV can't sync to anything but"* `STA Wsync` / `STA Vsync` / `STA Wsync` / `STA Wsync` / `STA Wsync` *"with nothing inbetween theese lines."* Mooney: *"I'm just doing WSYNC / VSYNC / WSYNC / WSYNC / set timer for vblank interval / WSYNC / VSYNC... that should appear the same to the TV as the lines you listed."* Stolberg: *"Unfortunately it's not. I'm not sure what it is, that causes the problem, and if it is only my TV or all PAL TVs, but RESCUE.BIN got a pretty messed up screen that way. When Greg took the STA Tim64T out of the three lines of Vsync, the screen syncronised much better on my TV."* 〔stella-list `199708/msg00102`, `199708/msg00105`, `199708/msg00107`〕 In 1998, to another author: *"It seems, that the sync signal gets weaker, if the TIA has anything else to do than creating it. Some TVs, like mine, can't syncronize correctly to that and produce a rolling picture."* His routine loads both values before the window — `LDA #$02` / `LDX #$00` / `STA VBLANK` / `STA WSYNC` / `STA VSYNC` / three `STA WSYNC` / `STX VSYNC` — and *"Don't do anything between the two accesses to VSYNC, or the signal might get too weak."* Told to get a set with a vertical hold control: *"My TV has a vert hold knob. I'm just too lazy to get off the couch and turn it, everytime I want to play a different game."* 〔`199803/msg00061`, `199803/msg00062`, `199803/msg00073`〕 He had given Ruffin Bailey the same routine two months before, for a program that set `Tim64T` in the three lines after `STA Vsync`: *"Some TV sets can not sync to that and will produce a rolling picture"*, ending *"with nothing between the three STA WSYNC lines"* 〔`199801/msg00279`〕. One witness, his own sets, and an explanation he says he is not sure of (Cited only, not verified). ★**In 2002 the same author hedged it again and pointed it at NTSC sets.** Paul Slocum asked how to avoid the PAL colour-loss problem and recalled *"not doing anything with the TIA during VSYNC too"*; Stolberg: *"The thing with the uninterrrupted VSYNC is something I'm not sure about. But the problems that arise from this would normally affect badly tuned NTSC TVs rather than cause colour loss on PAL TVs."* And: *"If you hear someone on RGVC or AtariAge complain that his game rolls on his NTSC TV while he is sure that it is a NTSC game and not a PAL game, and then look at the game in question, you will notice that they are doing something during the VSYNC period. Mostly this seems to affect games that set up a TIM8T timer for the VSYNC period instead of doing 3 WSYNCs. But I'm not sure if that really is the problem, or if other things done during VSYNC might also cause problems."* He suggested the same shape with `sta WSYNC` first, *"since I've never heard of problems with a game that does it this way"* 〔`200206/msg00111`, `200206/msg00114`〕 (Cited only, not verified: a recollection, with no game or television named). ★**Earlier in 2002, Stolberg's requirement and a different one from Roger Williams.** Stolberg, in February: *"A game need to do three complete uninterrupted lines of VSYNC to work properly on all TVs. Some games try to use this time to do some other setup work (Solaris and Desert Falcon are known offenders in this respect) and this seems to to cause problems on some TVs. I don't have an electronical explanation for this though."* 〔`200202/msg00087`〕 Roger Williams, answering his later note that Gas Hog sets up a timer and keeps polling it 〔`200202/msg00097`〕: *"The point of writing to WSYNC is that the end of the pulse is timed exactly to within one cycle. When you set up a loop you get at least a 15-cycle, and sometimes more, variation in the length of the pulse. It should not matter if you do logic within the vertical sync, but you must end it by writing WSYNC and waiting for the TIA to wake up the CPU."* And: *"Neither the NTSC nor PAL specification addresses whether hardware should trigger from the leading or trailing edge of VSYNC. Hardware which triggers from the leading edge will not care about sloppy trailing edge timing. Emulators too will vary in response depending on how they trigger their background video timing."* 〔`200202/msg00101`〕 One asks for nothing inside the window, the other for an end timed by `WSYNC`, and the thread does not settle which a set needs (Cited only, not verified). The litmus shape counted in the row "`VSYNC` raised mid-line, or held for less than three full lines" ends the window with `lda #0` / `sta VSYNC` straight after a `WSYNC`, which meets the second and not the first — derived from that pattern, Not verified. Meeting the second is not shown to be enough: Thomas Jentzsch, answering Williams, *"Gas Hog does an WSYNC before turning of VSYNC too (see my previous post). So that can't be the reason here."* Williams: *"They must have messed up the cycle count then. I really don't believe that processing during VSYNC could mess up the pulse, since it is essentially a digital signal."* Stolberg: *"At the time I was having the problems I was mainly using the Atari 2600 Jr, that also has problems with Kool Aide Man. I'm not sure if I ever tried out the problematical games on a different console, so I can't say if it is a model specific problem."* 〔`200202/msg00105`, `msg00106`, `msg00107`〕 So, on Jentzsch's reading of its code, Gas Hog met the second condition, and it is one of the two games whose copies were reported to *"flip like PAL carts"* at the start of this exchange 〔`200202/msg00087`〕 (Cited only, not verified). ★**In November 2002 he tied the rolling to his set's tuning.** Stolberg: *"My old TV has a VHOLD knob. When I had set it a little off-tune, all PAL games and most NTSC games would play fine on the TV. Some NTSC games would roll the screen though. The only thing that all those games had in common was that they were doing something during the three lines of VSYNC. Most of them were setting the VBLANK period timer there."* Retuned, the set took every game *"except for Artillery Duel NTSC, which is just doing a too high frequency"*, and so *"I'm sure that the problem is not affecting too many TVs out there, since most of them don't have a VHOLD knob anymore"* — while still advising to leave the VCS alone during `VSYNC` *"just to be safe"* 〔`200211/msg00141`〕 (Cited only, not verified). ★**A month later he put the 2600 Jr of February down to a TIA version that, he said, every PAL 7800 he had seen shares — and the case in front of him had another cause.** Fabrizio Zavagli's Space Treat would not sync on his PAL 7800. Stolberg, from a trace log, blamed a timer set and a collision reset inside the three `VSYNC` lines, and: *"Thinking about it now, I was using a 2600 Jr. console, that has one of the TIAs that have the positioning problem in Kool Aid Man, back then. All PAL 7800s, that I have seen, have this version of the TIA chip too. If the synchonizing problem only happens on these TIA chips, it would explain, why Space Treat would have problems on your 7800 but works fine for almost everyone else."* 〔`200212/msg00315`〕 Zavagli then reported what had been wrong: *"It turned out I've been doing some silly stuff (memory initialization) and now I've finally been able to fix it"*, adding that he would keep his TIA accesses out of the three `VSYNC` lines from then on 〔`200212/msg00333`〕. So the thread neither shows that the work inside `VSYNC` was the cause nor tests the link to a TIA version (Cited only, not verified). | Set up the clearing value before the window, as his routine does, and put only `sta WSYNC` between the two `VSYNC` stores. | Nothing here flags it. The work inside the window does not change the line count, so `ntsc_frame_lines` and `frame_lines_stable` see an ordinary frame — derived, Not verified. | stella-list `199708/msg00102`, `msg00105`, `msg00107` (Eckhard Stolberg, Erik Mooney); `199803/msg00061`, `msg00062`, `msg00073` (Eckhard Stolberg, Chris Wilkson); `200206/msg00111`, `msg00114` (Paul Slocum, Eckhard Stolberg); `199801/msg00279` (Eckhard Stolberg); `200202/msg00087`, `msg00097` (Eckhard Stolberg), `msg00101` (Roger Williams), `msg00105` (Thomas Jentzsch), `msg00106` (Roger Williams), `msg00107` (Eckhard Stolberg); `200211/msg00141` (Eckhard Stolberg); `200212/msg00315` (Eckhard Stolberg), `msg00333` (Fabrizio Zavagli) |
| **`VSYNC` does not reset the line — a `RESxx` after it is timed from the last `WSYNC`** | Writing `VSYNC` starts no new line and waits for nothing, so the beam is wherever the instructions since the last `WSYNC` put it. An emulator author, with Battlezone's radar 15 pixels out: *"I knew that immediately after a WSYNC the color clock is at 0. … However, immediately after a VSYNC you're at color clock 15. As Battlezone sets RESP0 in the first row after a VSYNC I was off by 15 pixels."* The code was `STY WSYNC / LDA #00 / STA VSYNC`: *"I made the mistake of thinking that VSYNC reset the pixel clock to zero as it starts a new frame. But it doesn't. And the LDA and STA here add up to 15 pixel clocks after the WSYNC."* (Cited only, not verified) | count every cycle from the last `WSYNC` to the strobe — here 2 + 3 = 5 CPU cycles = 15 colour clocks | runtime: `beamtrace` gives the beam clock of each write | AtariAge `topic/260569` (dan-piponi) |
| **a frame far too short does not roll — it TILES** | the television is not ready to sync again and keeps drawing, so the picture repeats down the screen and each repeat's VBLANK shows as a black band. Chris Cracknell, 1998, on a console: *"Three copies of each sprite spread out vertically across the screen and black lines dividing the screen into 3rds … The image is very stable"* — and *"When I run the programme on an emulator, everything works fine."* Eckhard Stolberg found the line loop: `ldx #$ab / blow sta WSYNC / dex / bpl blow`. `$AB` already has bit 7 set, so after the first `dex` (`$AA`) `bpl` falls through — one line where many were meant (the signed-test trap in §D; Stolberg's own wording, *"When X is decreased to $7f the branch isn't taken anymore"*, describes a `bmi` loop, but the effect he names is the one that happened): *"you are doing a lot less lines than you wanted to. Your TV isn't ready to sync again after such a small number of lines and just goes on displaying. The black stipes in your display is the VBLANK routine"* (`stipes` is his spelling). **The signature points away from the cause**: the first reply asked whether the players were set to three copies. ★**Too many lines are the other end of the same question.** Stolberg, the same month, asked by Bradford Mott for the minimum and maximum line counts Stella should accept: *"There are no definite minimum and maximum numbers. It depends on your TV with how many scanlines it still can sync. If you do a sync signal before the the TV is ready again, it will just go on displaying. If you do to many lines, the TV will force a sync signal on it's own and return to the top of the screen."* 〔`199804/msg00044`〕 (Cited only, not verified: no set was named). | fix the line count; read "stable vertical copies + black bars" as a frame-length symptom before touching NUSIZ. A line loop whose count starts at 128 or more has to close on `bne`; Omegamatrix, listing pitfalls he met when he began: *"Changing BNE to BPL on a kernel loop that is large. If you have values 128-255 it is a negative value, and BPL is not taken. A kernel might have 192+ lines, so you have to use BNE to loop or do a compare. The compare eats up extra cycles though, and when you get a busy kernel you might not have that."* (AtariAge `topic/27010`; Cited only, not verified) | runtime (`ntsc_frame_lines`, `frame_lines_stable`) — the count is what this harness measures; the tiled picture is the television's and cannot appear here | stella-list `199804/msg00108` (Cracknell), `199804/msg00110` (Chris Wilkson, the three-copies guess), `199804/msg00113` (Stolberg), `199804/msg00044` (Stolberg, to Bradford Mott); AtariAge `topic/27010` (Omegamatrix) |
| **a jump back to `VSYNC` before the frame is finished — once was a blink; a held Reset that jumped to the start lost sync** | Chris Larkin, 2003, wanted to start the next frame as soon as a deal of unpredictable length finished, with VBLANK never ended: *"This works in Z26 and Stella the screen blinks for a frame and then resumes... however PCAE takes a bomb on it and just freezes the game"*. Roger Williams: *"I am 90% sure that this will cause the TV to lose vertical sync and roll until it re-syncs. Not damaging, but very annoying in play mode."* Larkin, a day later, from testers (he had offered the ROM to anyone with a Cuttle Cart or a modded Supercharger): *"On all reports the software caused a momentary blink in the picture and then it returned to normal. Much like when reset is pressed on most games (Which I believe does cause an early VSync)"*, with *"no data as to how a PAL TV will react"*. B. Watson, 2001, listing what had failed on a real VCS in a program developed on an emulator: *"holding down 'game reset' caused it to lose sync with the TV, because I was just jumping to the beginning of the cart whenever it was pressed"* (his backquote shown as a quote). That the held switch meant a jump on every frame, against the testers' single jump, is our reading of the two reports. Cited only, not verified. ★**The jump can also be the main loop's own, every frame, with no switch held.** Andrew Davie, 2003, of a clock demo: *"Found my major bug in the system (branching back to cart reset code every frame - go figure!) and now things make much more sense."* 〔stella-list `200301/msg00368`〕 He names no symptom and does not say the jump came before the frame was finished (Cited only, not verified). A reset routine that clears RAM would undo each frame's state, and `watch_ram`, which names the PC that writes a RAM change, would show that routine as the writer — our reading, Not verified against a fixture | Blank instead of cutting the frame short. Dennis Debro: *"Why not suppress the TIA output for a frame instead?"*; Erik Eid blanked eight frames in Euchre to shuffle, *"and it's not at all disruptive"*; Thomas Jentzsch, decoding a puzzle in Jammed, *"blanked the screen during decoding (no VSYNC) and then added 5 blank screens with correct VSYNC. Eckhard had helped me verifying this on real hardware."* | this harness counts the short frame's lines; what a set does with it is the television's, which is not modelled here (§D, the television row) | stella-list `200309/msg00294`, `msg00295` (Roger Williams), `msg00296` (Chris Larkin), `msg00299` (Dennis Debro), `msg00306` (Erik Eid), `msg00308` (Chris Larkin), `msg00309` (Thomas Jentzsch); `200109/msg00328` (B. Watson); `200301/msg00368` (Andrew Davie) |
| **GRP/COLUP written outside HBLANK — deadline = the object's leftmost pixel clock, not just "HBLANK end"** | `GRP*` must finish **before the sprite's first visible pixel**: a sprite parked at clock 11 (near the left wall) is corrupted by a `STA GRP0` that lands at clock 13 → its left 2 px show the *previous* line's GRP. Any **per-line prefix before the `STA GRP` eats that margin** — a parity branch (`TXA;AND#1;BNE`, +6cy≈+18px), a PF-store-before-sprite on a band's first line, or a slower address mode — pushes the write past the object. ★**The first line of a loop is one of these lines.** Chris Cracknell, 1998, with 19 sprites on screen: *"For some reason the top of each sprite gets cut off in the first few inches of the screen."* Eckhard Stolberg: *"The sprite gets cut off, because the first scanline uses more cycles than the rest. This is because you set up the pointers etc. in that scanline. You need to move the STA WSYNC in the first line of the loop."* 〔stella-list `199804/msg00163`, `199804/msg00166`〕 The author's reply took up Stolberg's other point, the line count, and does not say whether this one was tried (Cited only, not verified). ★**The same deadline holds for `ENABL`, and a late write reads as a thin object.** Eric Ball, 2002, drawing a direction pointer with the ball, found that when the left one showed at all it was *"thinner than the center & right balls"*, about 4 pixels where 8 were meant, and then answered himself: *"My kernal doesn't hit ENABL until 32 cycles in. So the left one gets half cut off."* A separate 4-pixel error on the right-hand one he could not explain 〔stella-list `200210/msg00079`, `200210/msg00080`〕 (Cited only, not verified: his diagnosis, and the engine was not run on his kernel). ★**What counts is when the store completes, not when it starts.** Chris Wilkson asked whether GRP timings depend on when the instruction starts or when it finishes; Andrew Davie, 2001: *"When the instruction completes, unfortunately."* Eckhard Stolberg confirmed in the same thread that `sta.w` is the absolute form 〔stella-list `200102/msg00159`, `200102/msg00185`, `200102/msg00219`〕 (Cited only, not verified). | keep the low-X sprite's `STA GRP0` as the **first thing after WSYNC**; on a band's first line write GRP *before* the PF stores; give the earliest-clock object the least-delayed slot | runtime/manual (`read_row` the drawn line's left edge) | 166749, 63229, 74225 + in-house: Combat band-boundary GRP0 & parity-prefix left-edge corruption 2026-07-18/19; stella-list `199804/msg00163`, `msg00166` (Chris Cracknell, Eckhard Stolberg); `200210/msg00079`, `msg00080` (Eric Ball); `200102/msg00159`, `msg00185`, `msg00219` (Andrew Davie, Chris Wilkson, Eckhard Stolberg) |
| **four objects on one line, with the writes spread across it** | Erik Mooney, 2013, recalling a 1997 paddle kernel built for *"four independent detailed player-controlled flickerless objects on the same scanline"*, two of them missiles: *"That part does work, as I remember. Not perfectly, since the register hits happen throughout the scanline so there'd be some graphical jitter."* The part that defeated it was elsewhere: *"The challenge is to reposition either sprite while still writing the other's graphics every scanline or two, and also reading the four paddles."* — *"I don't think that part ever worked."* He gives no measurement and does not say which writes moved (AtariAge `topic/205205`; Cited only, not verified). A write that lands after an object has begun drawing, the row above, is one way such jitter shows — derived, Not verified. | Know where on the line each object's write lands, not only that the line fits in 76 cycles. | `beam_intervals` gives each write's earliest and latest beam clock over all paths. The drift from repeated `HMOVE` strobes (the multiple-`HMOVE` row below) is a different cause. | AtariAge `topic/205205` (erikm) |
| **PF band's first row partial / the "eaten line"** | a full-width `PFx` band whose writes don't finish in its first row's HBLANK (e.g. PF set on the *previous* line's trailing, no leading `WSYNC`) renders that row partly black — the left edge is unfilled because PF fills as the beam crosses (same root as GRP-outside-HBLANK). And if an object's last row shares that line, the PF write eats it → you get the object's last row **xor** a full-width PF row, not both | set the band's PF early in the **HBLANK of its first line** (after that line's `WSYNC`); to keep BOTH an object's last row AND a full-width PF row, give the band **1 extra line** | manual / `read_row` the band's first row | in-house: PONG static-repro 2026-06-19 |
| **a banded playfield kernel that waits before it stores in one section and after it in another** | serguei2, 2004, wrote a playfield kernel after Andrew Davie's tutorial Session 15 and got a picture one band off. Thomas Jentzsch's diagnosis, as the distilled notes record it: in some sections the order was reversed — the `WSYNC` loop that waits out a band ran before the `PFx` store instead of after it — so those sections started on the wrong lines. The top sections happened to come out right and the middle one did not (AtariAge `topic/44284`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). | Use one order in every section: store the band's pattern, then wait until it is due to change. | `read_row` the first line of each band and compare it with the intended line number | AtariAge `topic/44284` (serguei2, Thomas Jentzsch) |

It is the early side of the windows already recorded here and in `docs/fundamentals-audit.md` (asymmetric-playfield rewrite windows; reflected mode: *"RPF2 must complete exactly at cycle 48"*) and in `docs/design-principles.md` (*"too early or too late and it breaks"*). `pf_deadlines` does not flag the early side: *"It checks the late edge only"* (`docs/scenarios.md`, where a right-half rewrite landing before the beam has finished the left half was measured passing on 2026-09-15, by the review, outside this repository). ★**An emulator put the same kind of rewrite on the exact cycle, and a console did not.** Roger Williams, 2001, switching a repeated-playfield example to a reflected one by writing `CTRLPF` = 1: *"it's on EXACTLY the right cycle with the code unmodified. Saunders gave, *"According to my math"*, that *"The STA PF2b had to start exactly on cycle 45, done by cycle 47"*, and warned that a cycle range has to say *"whether the cycle range refers to when either the STA can commence or when it needs to have finished, or both"*; his "done by cycle 47" and `fundamentals-audit.md`'s "complete exactly at cycle 48" may be one edge counted two ways (our reading). | **`RESP0` struck every frame resets the player's position every frame** | Manuel Polik, 2001, returning a beginner's program with his fixes marked in the source: *"You were in fact pretty close! On our reading, a `RESxx` sets an absolute position, so a strobe at the same cycle every frame puts the object back at that point each frame: the `HMOVE` of the current frame can shift it a little while the joystick is held, nothing carries over to the next frame, and it is back at the strobe point once the input stops; a routine that recomputes the strobe cycle from the object's X each frame is the deliberate form of the same act (Not verified). | strobe `RESxx` when the position should change, or from a routine that derives the cycle from X; move by `HMxx`/`HMOVE` otherwise | manual | stella-list `200104/msg00123`, `200104/msg00129` (Tempest), `200104/msg00125` (Manuel Polik) |
| **HMOVE comb on a visible line** | every `HMOVE` right after WSYNC blanks color clocks 0-7 of THAT line (the comb) — if a drawn band's first row shares the HMOVE line, its left 8px go black (e.g. a top-wall row-1 notch). **★It is an OVERSIGHT, not a specification, and that is why the cy73/74 workaround above is revision-dependent.** Glenn Saunders in 1998: *"The reason the 2600 positions objects as it does is because **poly-counter logic is cheaper to implement than digital logic**. Believe me, they did weigh the alternatives … they developed a software conversion routine and were anticipating that this routine, albeit CPU-hungry, **would only be necessary once at the top of the frame for each sprite (hence not knowing about the HMOVE comb bug)**"*. A second voice in the same thread: *"Refreshing to see someone refer to the HMOVE effect as a **bug** instead of a **feature**. My guess is that it was Activision who turned it into a feature. Too bad really, **it might have gotten fixed**."* So the comb lives in behaviour nobody specified, which is the reason our own measurement finds it moving under a TIA-revision flag (`LostMOTCK` changes `litmus_hmove_mid` and `litmus_hmove_side`, and nothing else in 145 litmus ROMs) rather than an unexplained coincidence. ★**Two `STA PF0` per line do not tell you which problem a kernel is solving.** Marc, 1997, read HERO, Decathlon and Enduro as skipping the first two bits of PF0 and using the last two on the right, *"so PF0 must be updated twice every scanline, right ?"*; Greg Troutman: *"I would guess that some or all of these games don't actually update PF0, but might instead just be doing an HMOVE on every single scanline to create the two bit margin you see."* In the same thread Erik Mooney's own kernel writes PF0, PF1 and PF2 twice per line for a non-reflected, non-duplicated playfield (`CTRLPF` = 0). So a second PF0 write in someone else's kernel may be an asymmetric playfield rather than the margin, and the thread does not settle what those three games do 〔`199703/msg00247`, `199703/msg00248`, `199703/msg00249`〕; Cited only, not verified. | land every HMOVE on a **black/blank line**; give the band's first row a line with no HMOVE (PF set in its own HBLANK). ★**Black is not blank, so a black line hides the comb only on a set adjusted to hide it.** SpiceWare, 2015: *"the color value for black(phase offset = 0, luma = 0) is not at blanking level. | **N-edge coincidence = the real worst path** | a per-line kernel with several `cpy <edge>` branches (ball top/bottom, paddle tops/bottoms…) gets budgeted on "typical" frames, but the true worst case is **all edges landing on the SAME Y** (+~5cy per extra hit) — a 1cy overrun that free-run testing may miss for hundreds of frames, then rolls once | hand-place the coincidence: poke every edge variable to one Y and `assert_line_budget`; add it as a standing fuzz case | poked-coincidence assert; `beamtrace` shows the stacked writes on one line | in-house: PONG PlayF 77cy (3 edges + INPT poll) 2026-07-02 |

Tjoppen: *"No, it entails processing 3 -> 6 bytes in ten scanlines (or however many times the lines are duplicated)..."*, having proposed earlier to *"compute the next block line while the current one is being drawn (6+6 byte buffer)"*. The author: *"Tjoppen you're right There are plenty of free cycles in the physical block of scanlines for each row of phat pixels to expand three bytes into six for the next row."* Counted per scanline, the cost looks as many times larger as the row is tall, and a design that fits is rejected on paper (AtariAge `topic/203463`; Cited only, not verified — no kernel was built here). | spread the preparation of the next row over the lines of the current one, into a second buffer | `prove_line_budget` still proves each line; nothing here proposes the split | AtariAge `topic/203463` (Mr SQL, Tjoppen) |

Erik Mooney, the same year, used the same idea to test a count: *"insert NOPs and other dummy instructions until you have exactly 76 cycles on a scanline _without_ WSYNC. Then if your screen gradually gets more corrupt going down, you definitely have your count wrong."* 〔`200109/msg00329`〕 A kernel built with no `WSYNC`, and what one late store cost it, is `docs/techniques/kernel-micro-idioms.md` §30.
