| **velocity-negate bounce without position clamp = sign-flip trap** | upgrading fixed-value reflection (`DY:=-1`) to angle-preserving `DY:=-DY` silently drops the *idempotence* the old code relied on: if the object can sit at/past the wall row for 2+ frames (re-hit, \|DY\|≥2 overshoot), the sign flips every frame → the object oscillates in the wall zone forever | clamp the POSITION back to the last legal row on every wall reflection (top→0, bottom→max−h) | free-run + watch the position variable near walls | in-house: PONG english 182↔184 trap 2026-07-02 |
| **`bpl`/`bmi` clamp on a coordinate that legitimately exceeds 127** | using bit7 (`bpl`→"non-negative") to detect underflow on a value whose valid range is 0..N with N>127 (e.g. a playfield Y of 0..182): every legit value ≥128 has bit7 set and is misread as "negative" → silently clamped to 0. A target of Y=150 becomes 0 = paddle snaps to top | detect underflow by the **subtraction borrow / wrap magnitude**, not bit7: after the signed add, `cmp #<wrap-threshold>` (e.g. `#200`) to separate a wrapped-underflow (≈247-255) from a legit high value (≤~180). **The threshold is range-dependent** — set it above the max legit value: a lead/extrapolation like `BallRow + 8·DY` reaches ~202, so it needs `cmp #220` (not `#200`, which would misread 202 as underflow). ★**Where the step is known, its sign names the edge and no second threshold is needed.** Andrew Davie, 2001, for a 0..159 coordinate: after `adc increment`, one unsigned `cmp #$A0` / `bcc` catches both overflows, *"because -1, -2, etc... are represented in binary as $FF, $FE, etc.... which as unsigned numbers are > 160"*, and then `ldx increment` / `bmi` picks the left edge, otherwise the right; without the step he separated the two with a second threshold (`sbc #$A0` then `cmp #$20` in his wrapping version), which allows *"a bullet to be traveling up to 32 pixels/step before it fails"*. Thomas Jentzsch's form of the first test: *"Add $5f and check if the carry flag is set."* Davie's own caveat: *"The above code snippets are probably buggy... it's the method you're looking at, not my code!!!"* 〔`200103/msg00100`, `200103/msg00101`, `200103/msg00103`〕 (Cited only, not verified) | poke the coord to a >127 value and read the derived target | in-house: PONG AI target Y clamp 2026-07-03 (threshold refinement: v3 lead AI 2026-07-06); stella-list `200103/msg00101` (Thomas Jentzsch), `200103/msg00103` (Andrew Davie) |
| **`cmp` clobbers Z between a load and a test-for-zero `bne`/`beq`** | to convert "0 → 1" you `lda v / … / bne keep / lda #1`, but if a `cmp #k` sits between the `lda v` and the `bne`, the branch tests `v==k`, **not** `v==0` (cmp overwrote Z). The clamp `lda v / cmp #hi / bcc / … / bne set` silently mis-handles `v==0` because the `bne` reads the cmp's result. Sibling of the immediate-`ld` clobber below; here it's `cmp` | do the **zero test before any `cmp`** that reuses the register (`lda v / beq zero / cmp #hi / …`), mirroring the working pattern in the serve-angle clamp. The same holds after the two-compare range test — `cmp #min / bcc less / cmp #max / bcs more`, *"#min <= a < #max, if we arrive here"*: on the path that arrives, `a < max`, so there Z is always clear and a `beq` placed after it never fires | poke the input to 0 and read the output; it comes out 0 instead of the intended 1 | in-house: PONG serve-angle continuation clamp 2026-07-04; the range idiom from AtariAge `topic/305866` (that thread does not raise the Z hazard) |
| **immediate `ldx`/`lda`/`ldy` clobbers N/Z between a flag-set and its branch** | a load-immediate sets N/Z from the loaded constant. Placing `ldx #80` after `lda dir` (to hold a value for the taken branch) destroys the sign flag the following `bpl`/`bmi` was meant to test → the branch reads the constant's sign, not `dir`'s. `sta` preserves flags (safe), but any `ld_` does not. ★The general form, bogax, 2011: after a `dey`, N and Z keep reflecting the `dey` until something else that affects them runs — an `ldx` after it, and they reflect the `ldx` — and some instructions change the flags without changing a register: the compares, `bit`, `plp` and `rti` (AtariAge `topic/180714`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). The `cmp` row above is one of those. | branch **before** any `ld_`, or avoid the branch entirely with arithmetic (`dir` ∈ {\$01,\$FF} → `X = dir + 79` gives 80/78 branchlessly, no flag dependence) | step through and read the branch's actual target | in-house: PONG serve-side select 2026-07-03; the general form AtariAge `topic/180714` (bogax) |
| **a subroutine called between `BIT`'s two branches can change `V`** | `BIT CXM0P` copies D7 to N and D6 to V, so one read answers two collisions — `bpl` for one, `bvc` for the other. Put a `jsr` on the first branch's path and the second branch tests whatever the routine left in `V`; `ADC`, `SBC`, `BIT`, `CLV` and `PLP` are among the instructions that write it. SpiceWare, under exactly that shape (`bit CXM0P / bpl .skip1 / jsr … / .skip1 bvc .skip2`): *"ProcessMissile0Player1Collision and ProcessMissile1Player0Collision need to preserve the state of V. They can do so by using PHP at the start, then PLP at the end, or by not doing anything that changes V. Another solution would be to just do another BIT test after .skip1 and .skip3."* Same family as the two rows above: a flag read after something else wrote it. ★The same two-flag read is used on purpose for state in RAM, and the same rule then applies to it: SpiceWare keeps the game state in bit 7 of one byte and, when he needs another, a second state in bit 6, so `BIT GameState` answers with `BMI`/`BPL` and `BVS`/`BVC` *"w/out having to mask out the lower bits, allowing them to be used for something else such as which game variation the player has selected"* (AtariAge `topic/292204`; Cited only, not verified) | `PHP`/`PLP` around the routine's body, or a second `BIT` before the second branch | read every routine on the first branch's path for an instruction that writes `V` | AtariAge `topic/215833` (SpiceWare), `topic/292204` (SpiceWare) |
| **`LDA` sets N and Z but not V: `lda CXP0FB` / `bvc` tests a V left by something else** | A load copies bit 7 into N and does nothing to V, so the two halves of a collision register cannot both be read after `LDA`. Andrew Davie, 1998, to a beginner testing `CXP0FB`: *"If you wanted to check D6, you couldn't do this... lda CXP0FB / bvc D6clear. The reason is that, whilst the sign flag is set correctly by the load, the V flag is not. One of the reasons I find V branching quite uncommonly used."* The D7 half works as written — *"lda CXP0FB / bmi joycheck ;(D7 = 1?)"*; the question he was answering was a beginners' book's warning, as the asker quoted COMPUTE!'s *Machine Language For Beginners*, against `BPL` and `BMI` themselves, to which he replied *"What a load of rubbish!!! They are two quite useful instructions."* His first D6 fix used `bit #0`, which the 6502 does not have; Chris Wilkson pointed that out, and Davie: *"simply... bit CXP0FB is all that is required. D6 and D7 now occupy the V and N flags respectively."* Same family as the row above: the branch tests whatever instruction last wrote V. Cited only, not verified | `BIT CXP0FB`, then `BVS`/`BVC` for D6 and `BMI`/`BPL` for D7; or Davie's other form, `lda CXP0FB` / `asl` / `bmi` for D6 | read back from the `BVC`/`BVS` to the last instruction that writes V — `ADC`, `SBC`, `BIT`, `CLV` and `PLP` among them, per the row above | stella-list `199806/msg00115`, `msg00116` (Andrew Davie), `msg00118` (Chris Wilkson), `msg00120` (Andrew Davie) |
| **a branch right after `clc` (or `sec`) goes one way only — delete the `lsr` that set the carry, and a `bcs` after `clc` is never taken** | atari2600land, 2020, in a two-line kernel: the ball stayed drawn after it landed, stretched past its height of 4, and writing 0 to `ENABL` did not remove it. karl-g found the code: `lda PlayerY+4` / `clc` / (`adc #1` and `lsr` commented out) / `sta Temp` / `bcs NoDelayBL` / `ldx #1` / `stx VDELBL`. With the `lsr` gone the carry is always clear at the `bcs`, so `VDELBL` was set every time; with `stx VDELBL` commented out the ball had the expected height. The `lsr` was the kernel's halving of Y, whose low bit, left in the carry, chooses `VDELxx` — the `VDELP0 = y & 1` of `docs/techniques/two-line-kernel.md` (AtariAge `topic/309990`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). The thread does not say why `ENABL` = 0 did not clear it; that a delayed object changes only when `GRP1` is written (the first row of this section) would explain it — our reading, Not verified. The author went round it by making the wall taller. | When deleting the instruction that sets a flag, delete or rewrite the branch that tests it. | runtime: `cmd/cover` lists the branches that took only one direction in a run as `one_sided_branches`, and a constant branch is one of them — among branches the run simply did not drive both ways (read from `cmd/cover/main.go`; Not verified on this case). | AtariAge `topic/309990` (atari2600land, karl-g) |
| **coarse-positioning region overruns the scanline at large X** | the `sta WSYNC`→`sta WSYNC` region wrapping the ÷15 divide loop + `eor`/ASL fine tail grows with the target X; at max X its **worst case can exceed 76cy** → RESP0 strobes on the *next* line → **roll**, while a lucky small-X playtest passes. The positioning line is the kernel's **tightest** region | prove over ∀ (`prove_line_budget`) with `; @amax N` on the **region-opening `sta WSYNC`** (not the `sbc` line); then **cap sprite travel** or replace the loop with a **÷15 lookup table** (constant-time). Bounding the loop alone isn't enough — the fine tail also counts toward the 76 | static (`prove_line_budget` `roll_free`) | in-house: density-ladder rung2 2026-07-24 |
| **one object passing another can shift the other by a scanline** | Lee Seitz, 1997, after loading Piero Cavina's demo — one player reused for up to 11 objects — through Makewav: *"As sprite 0 passes over the top line of each instance of sprite 1, that sprite 1 moves one (I think) scanline up or down, depending on which way sprite 0 is moving."* Cavina had seen lines *"floating"* in Stella and another emulator and guessed *"the code taking more time than what I've planned in some critical points"*; to Seitz he answered that it *"should happen on some emulators, as others have told me that on the real thing everything is ok"*, and Chris Pepin had reported the demo working on his NTSC console. Glenn Saunders saw *"some shakiness in the horizontal lines that separate the sprites"*, and to that Cavina replied: *"It was the code taking too much time than I thought in a critical point. I think I've fixed it (at least all the three emulators I'm using to test it work in the same way)."* That Seitz's shift had the same cause, and that Makewav means he ran it on a console through a Supercharger, are our reading. Cited only, not verified | Budget each line with the other object on it, at the same Y as well as apart — the `N-edge coincidence` row above | `assert_line_budget` halts a run on a line over 76 cycles; `prove_line_budget` bounds every path through a region | stella-list `199703/msg00305`, `msg00306` (Chris Pepin), `msg00309`, `msg00310` (Lee Seitz), `msg00312`, `msg00314` (Glenn Saunders), `199704/msg00003` (Piero Cavina) |
| **multiple `HMOVE` strobes in one frame (multi-object positioning)** | positioning each object with its own `sta HMOVE` (e.g. P0-block → P1-block → M0-block, each ending in HMOVE) **re-applies every current `HMxx` to ALL objects on every strobe** → the object positioned first gets its fine motion applied N times (looks like slope-Nx: +1 X-input moved the sprite ~3px) and a stale `HMxx` left in another object's register bleeds onto it → the sprite **never settles**: it drifts a few px every frame even with **zero input**, and wobbles / "loops left-right within a range while moving." Passes a single-frame screenshot and stays `roll_free`, so it hides until you watch it MOVE. RESxx re-strobing every frame does NOT save you — the extra HMOVEs act after the strobe. ★**The same property, used on purpose.** octavio-pinho-bokel, drawing two players' views of one game on alternate frames, shifts the whole picture: *"The way I shift everything is quite unusual, for the sake of simplicity I do 10 HMOVE with the value of 8, moving everything half screen, while keeping the relative position of everything intact."* (AtariAge `topic/331474`; Cited only, not verified.) An upper nibble of 8 is the −8 end of the range in §B, eight pixels right per strobe, and ten strobes make 80 of the 160 pixels — our reading; the post does not give the byte written. ★**Another jitter that went away by reordering alone.** Manuel Polik, 2001, listing a new Gunfight build: *"Removed the jumping of the bullets (To my surprise this was possible with just some simple rearrangements inside the kernel :-))"* (his emphasis on *jumping* dropped). He does not say which writes moved or what made the bullets jump, so it is not known to be this trap; what it shows is that rearranging the kernel was the fix 〔`200108/msg00151`〕 (Cited only, not verified). | strobe all coarse positions (`RESxx`) first, set all `HMxx`, then **one** `sta HMOVE` for the whole frame — deferring HMOVE across the intervening `sta WSYNC` cut lines is fine (HMxx persist). The order of one object's two writes is not the constraint; Eckhard Stolberg, 1999: *"You can write to HMxx before you write to it's corresponding RESxx register, but you have to do the RESPxx before you apply the fine movement from the HMxx register with a WSYNC/HMOVE."* 〔stella-list `199907/msg00027`〕 (Cited only, not verified). Alternatively `HMCLR` between per-object strobes. Watch the budget: removing the per-object `sta WSYNC` too absorbs the next block's prologue into the div region (→ over-budget) — keep the WSYNC cuts, drop only the HMOVEs. **★A third fix, for a kernel where you cannot remove an instruction at all: REPLACE the strobe rather than drop it.** In a generated, fully-timed kernel every write below is timed from the top of the line, so deleting three cycles moves everything after it. `bit $80` costs the same three cycles, touches RAM instead of the TIA, and is what this project's own first work uses — `roms/260816_transistor/tools/mk_transistor_ms.py:1023-1027` rewrites a repeat pass's `sta HMOVE` into ``bit $80   ; was HMOVE -- the repeat must not nudge again``, having measured the trap first (*"pass 1 drew at x=19 and its repeat at x=20, every letter on the line one pixel right of the first"*). **The catch, and it is not in that comment: `BIT` is not free.** It sets N and V from the memory byte and Z from `A AND m` (measured, `internal/emu/bitflags_test.go`), so the substitution is only safe where the flags are dead. If they are not, `JMP *+3` keeps them and reads no data — three cycles, but three bytes where `bit $80` was two, so everything after it moves by one byte (and a branch after it can newly cross a page, one cycle more); the bus sees only its own ROM fetches (`JMP abs` is 3 cycles in the engine's instruction table, `Gopher2600/hardware/cpu/instructions/definitions.json`; not measured here). `PHP`/`PLP` also keeps the flags, at seven cycles in two bytes (measured, `internal/emu/oddsleep_test.go`) | runtime (`read_row` a **constant-input** frame N times in a row → the object's left edge must be byte-identical; any per-frame drift = this bug). **The static half is `cmd/timinglint`'s `hmxx-without-hmove`**, and pointing it at the works for the first time on 2026-09-05 found the other end of this same fix: two generated bodies in which EVERY strobe had been quieted, leaving a `lda #$F0` staging block with no consumer at all (`sta HMOVE` = 0, `bit $80` = 11 and 10). Dead rather than wrong, but the comment above it claims a purpose the ROM does not have. **9 flagged ROMs, 0 false positives** — the rule is a whole-file predicate (`internal/cyclebound/timinglint.go:224`, `haveHmxxNonzero && !sawHMOVE`), so a ROM that strobes anywhere is never flagged | in-house: Outlaw gunman horizontal wobble 2026-07-25; the replace-don't-drop fix and its measurement from `roms/260816_transistor/tools/mk_transistor_ms.py`, found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2), verified here 2026-09-05; AtariAge `topic/331474` (octavio-pinho-bokel); stella-list `199907/msg00027` (Eckhard Stolberg), `200108/msg00151` (Manuel Polik) |

| **`VBLANK` written on the LAST picture line blanks that line** | a picture loop that exits with the CPU at the START of the final line, then writes `VBLANK` there, blanks the line it is standing on. It is **invisible on a dark background** and shows as a black line across the bottom of a light one — so it survives every build until somebody inverts the palette. ★**The top edge is the mirror image, and it was reported first.** A timer loop exits wherever the timer runs out, so a `sta VBLANK` straight after it clears the blank mid-line and the first picture line stays black up to that point. Kirk Israel, 2002: *"if I change the background color to non-black, I see a black line at the top"*; Thomas Jentzsch: *"you are disabling VBLANK somewhere (depending on the timer value) in the middle of the scanline. Just add 'sta WSYNC' before 'sta VBLANK' and the long line is gone."* Same fix at both ends, and the same camouflage: black on black. ★★The mechanism is also a tool: `VBLANK` blanks from one colour clock after the clock it is written at (bundled Gopher2600: `futureVblank.Schedule(1, …)`; real hardware **Cited only, not verified**), so set and cleared inside one line it masks both edges of the picture. One author's kernel clears it at cycle 25 and sets it again at 67 to hide objects wrapping at the sides (his prose says the reverse; the code is as written here), and SpiceWare named Stay Frosty 2's title screen as a shipped use (Cited only, not verified). windless (2019) observed in 8bitworkshop and Stella that turning the `VBLANK` black on or off takes effect about one TIA clock after the CPU commits the write, and in his test, which writes `COLUBK` on one line and toggles `VBLANK` at the same point on another, the two boundaries look offset by a dot; he asked whether that is documented, and the thread has no reply (AtariAge `topic/299947`, held here only as distilled notes; emulators only, real hardware **Cited only, not verified**). | `sta WSYNC` before the `VBLANK` write so it lands in the overscan; the overscan loop gives that line back, so the frame is still 262. At the top, `sta WSYNC` before the `sta VBLANK` that clears it | build the same kernel with foreground and background swapped and compare the drawn row count; `read_row` the last picture line, and the first | measured 2026-08-26 in a piece in the private `roms/` repository (its ledger names this file); the top edge 〔stella-list `200208/msg00212`, `200208/msg00223`〕; the mid-line use and wickeycolumbus's *"if it is during the visible part you will get a partial line at the top. Same goes for the bottom"* from AtariAge `topic/233831`; the comparison with `COLUBK` from AtariAge `topic/299947` |
| **colour above or below the picture comes from the program, and `VBLANK` is the switch for it** | iurinery, 2018, asked how to get rid of colour at the top and bottom edges of a generated playfield screen (his screenshot is not held here). Nukey Shay: *"Not sure what you are trying to indicate with the arrows...when the PF colors appear and are removed on the screen? Relocating your VBLANK calls would be that solution."* The author fixed it another way: *"I have fixed the problem with the Top/Bottom edges of the screen by adding COLUPF & COLUBK to the Vertical Blank/Overscan."* (AtariAge `topic/275380`). SpiceWare, 2013, to another beginner, on what the switch is for: *"Think of the TIA register VBLANK as VIDEO ON/VIDEO OFF. When bit 1 of VBLANK is turned on, the video output is turned off. This causes TIA to ignore the colors and bit patterns set for the players (sprites), missiles, ball, playfield, etc. and just send black scanlines to the TV. This is important during Vertical Blank as outputting anything other than black can be interpreted by your TV as something like Closed Captioning (which is sent on line 21 of the Vertical Blank)."* That beginner's `VBLANK` was wrong and had gone unseen because *"The last thing your 192 kernel loop does is set the background color to black"* (AtariAge `topic/219525`). Both Cited only, not verified; the line-21 detail is not checked against a broadcast standard. ★In one report the garbage showed at the other edge from the one blamed. Glenn Saunders, 2001, on an Elevator Action prototype: *"it looks like the programmer didn't turn off TIA output at the bottom of the screen so the first few scanlines at the top has some leftover garbage that looks like closed captioning"* 〔`200111/msg00462`〕 — his reading of the picture; nobody in the thread traced the code (Cited only, not verified). ★**Left on, the switch blanks everything.** Thomas Jentzsch, 2006, naming three faults in a newcomer's first DASM demo, gave as one of them that `VBLANK` was turned on and never turned off again, so the screen stays black — the program assembles (AtariAge `topic/96515`; held here only as distilled notes, so the wording is not checked: Cited only, not verified). Another of the three is the row "A label named after a register stops the build" in §D. ★**The switch is bit 1, so a `1` blanks nothing.** Eckhard Stolberg, 1997, to Erik Mooney: *"you should turn on the Vblank after the last onscreen line. Since PAL can display more lines than NTSC, you would see whatever is in the TIA graphics registers for a couple of lines, if you don't turn the display off."* Mooney: *"Oops, my mistake.. I was writing 1 instead of 2 to VBLANK at the start of overscan.. thanks."* 〔stella-list `199708/msg00102`, `199708/msg00105`〕 A `1` sets bit 0 only; `internal/cyclebound/absint.go` reads the *"VBLANK display-disable bit (bit1 of the last value stored to $01)"*. Cited only, not verified. ★**Through the `VSYNC` lines as well, by one account, and not needed there by another.** Stolberg again, 2001, to Manuel Polik on Gunfight: *"One thing that you should add when you clean up your code are some instructions that turn on VBLANK at the start of the overscan. If VBLANK isn't on during the VSYNC lines, you'd get a strange gloom at the top of the screen. At least on a PAL TV where the displayable area is bigger than the display of your game you would."* 〔stella-list `200102/msg00246`〕 Thomas Jentzsch, 2015, asked how much room there is for clearing it: *"VBLANK is not that critical. If you enable blanking after the display has been drawn and disable at the beginning of vertical sync, that should be more than long enough. You can keep it enabled during vertical sync or even until the next display starts."* (AtariAge `topic/233831`). The second makes blanking through the `VSYNC` lines optional; the first says a PAL set would show a fault without it. Neither has been put on a set here (Cited only, not verified). | set `VBLANK` D1 through the vertical blank and the overscan. Writing the colour registers there instead covers the symptom, as the first author did, and a black background hid the second one's wrong `VBLANK` — derived from the two threads, Not verified | Nothing here flags it, and what a set does with a non-black vertical blank cannot be seen here (the television row in §D) | AtariAge `topic/275380` (iurinery, Nukey Shay), `topic/219525` (SpiceWare); stella-list `200111/msg00462` (Glenn Saunders); AtariAge `topic/96515` (Thomas Jentzsch); stella-list `199708/msg00102`, `msg00105` (Eckhard Stolberg, Erik Mooney); `200102/msg00246` (Eckhard Stolberg); AtariAge `topic/233831` (Thomas Jentzsch) |

## B. Positioning ground-truth (why "the X doesn't match")

| fact | value | source |
|---|---|---|
| **a two-pixel stroke needs ≥5 source columns** | stem 2 + gap 1 + stem 2. Doubled, that row is **10 px** wide (12 px from a 6-column source), while the ball and the missile can only be **1/2/4/8 px**. So **no solid-block object can stand in for the full-width row of a two-stem letter at any scale**, and "just narrow the bars" cannot rescue a letterform that needs two stems on one row — it deletes the gap instead. | measured 2026-08-26 in a piece in the private `roms/` repository |
| **internal draw delay after RESxx** | player draws **+5 CLK** late, missile/ball **+4 CLK** late (RESx resets instantly but the object appears later) — *first suspect when target X is off by ~5px* | 294398, 283075, 305780, 75335-cluster |
| RESx strobe granularity | 3 color-clocks; RESP0 finishing at cy46 → X≈75. ★The premise of dr-pinball's 2021 question, which the distilled notes record as correct: the CPU holds address, chip-enable and data for its whole cycle, so the TIA sees one write as the same input for three colour clocks. jetsetilly's answer, as the notes give it: a ball reset is indeed triggered only at three-clock granularity, then an internal delay follows that varies and is mostly 5 TIA cycles; the notes conclude that with care the ball can still be put on any colour clock. The row above has the ball at +4; the two counts are not reconciled here (AtariAge `topic/319504`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified) | 172089, 137739, 329611, 304182; AtariAge `topic/319504` (dr-pinball, jetsetilly) |
| HMOVE range | −8..+7 px / scanline (asymmetric: +7 left … −8 right); pulse count = upper nibble of `HMxx EOR $80` | 319456 |
| coarse÷15 + fine HMOVE | `eor #7` → 4×ASL, ~30 cy; small real-HW latch differences | 304182, 284554, 160645 |
| ÷15 / X(N) is kernel-specific | the absolute offset includes the prologue cycle count → **measure `read_tia` HmovedPixel, don't hardcode N** | (CLAUDE.md) + 294398 |
| **positioning ÷15 loop crossing a page → judder** | if the `sbc #15` / `bcs` divide loop straddles a page boundary, the **taken `bcs` costs +1 cycle** → each iteration is 6cy(18px) not 5cy(15px); the coarse step no longer tiles with the HMOVE fine (±~7) → the object **judders ~3px at every 15px while moving** AND the X(N) offset inflates by +1cy/iteration. A *static* object hides it (only shows when it MOVES across cells). **Keep the divide loop on one page** (page-align it). Found: PONG smooth-ball, the loop sat at $F0FE/$F100 — moving it to $F100 (whole loop on one page) made the ball perfectly smooth (read_motion jerk→0). detect: runtime (read_motion jerk_rms) / listing (loop address vs page boundary) | in-house: PONG 2026-06-19 |
| **÷15 DELAY 1→2 boundary is HBLANK-compressed → a moving sprite hitches ~2px ONCE near the left wall** | distinct from the page-cross judder: even with the loop on one page, the FIRST coarse cell is nonlinear because a low-iteration strobe lands deep in HBLANK. `litmus-results.md:15-17,31`: coarse step is **+9px for DELAY 1→2** vs a clean **+15px for DELAY≥2** (linear `ResetPixel=15·DELAY−18` only from DELAY 3). So a sprite moving across the **DELAY 1↔2** boundary (target X ≈ clk 12-16, i.e. the leftmost ~15px) steps ~2px BACKWARD once, while every higher ÷15 boundary (DELAY≥2) is smooth. It's HW nonlinearity, not a code bug; a *static* sprite there is fine — only a MOVING one shows it. **Fix for a moving sprite: clamp the ÷15 input so it never uses DELAY 1** (keep coarse ≥2 iterations ⇒ leftmost target ≳ clk14). The sprite loses ~4px of left reach but moves perfectly smoothly (`read_motion` x monotonic, no backward step). Verified: Outlaw left gunman — floored P0X≥15, seam gone | measured (`litmus-results.md:15-17,31`) + in-house: Outlaw gunman left-edge seam 2026-07-25 |
| **an object moved only by `HMxx`/`HMOVE` has no X the program can read** | The TIA answers reads only with collisions and inputs (`$00`-`$0D`, the static-trap note at the top of this file), so code that steers by relative motion alone cannot tell where the object is. flame, 2020, moving a sprite with `HMP` and `HMOVE` and wanting it to *"disappear 'off screen'"* where it wraps: *"I need to know where that sprite is."* Karl G: *"I'm pretty sure there isn't a way to get that info from the TIA directly. You will need to keep track of it yourself with your own variable."* SpiceWare: *"If you need the X position you need to use a byte of RAM to keep track of it. Then instead of using HMPx and HMOVE to shift the object left/right you use a subroutine like PosObject to reposition the object on every frame."* At which X the wrap begins nobody in the thread says; the asker's own guesses are left out. `stella-oracle.md` records the same answer. Cited only, not verified | AtariAge `topic/304108` (flame, Karl G, SpiceWare) |

## C. Cartridge / bank / RAM

| trap | fix | detect | source |
|---|---|---|---|
| **`RandomState` governs far more than the boot bank — INTIM and all of RAM, and it is off** | The row below records one consequence of `RandomState` being false. Counted 2026-09-04: the preference is read in **18 places**, and the two that matter most are not cartridge banks. `riot/timer/timer.go:101` randomises the **divider and INTIM** at reset (`ChipWrite(INTIM, Random.Intn(0xff))`); `memory/vcs/ram.go:51` fills **all 128 bytes of RAM** with random values instead of zero. Nothing in `internal`, `cmd` or `pkg` sets it (`rg RandomState` → 0). **A bank-switching quirk touches bank-switching ROMs; a deterministic INTIM and a zeroed RAM touch every ROM there is.** The corpus names the damage: Thomas Jentzsch, 2003 — **Berzerk seeds its maze from INTIM**, so a console that always reads the same value draws the same maze every time. ★**The console side, and it is disputed.** Eckhard Stolberg, 2004: *"While the 6507 will be reset at power-up, the RIOT will not. It will remain it's state for a short while after you turn the console off."* (his spelling) Berzerk reads INTIM at power-up, and *"If the VCS was turned off for a longer time, INTIM will always read the same. But if you turn your VCS off and then on again, the RIOT timer will be at the same state as it was when you turned the console off."* On his account a console that has been off for a while is as repeatable as this engine, and the seed varies only across a quick power cycle. Alex Herbert answered that *"The RIOT uses the same reset line as the CPU and is reset at power on"*, quoting the datasheet on /RES zeroing the four I/O registers — which on our reading says nothing about the timer; Edwin Blink, replying to Herbert: *"When turning power off and on quickly reset may not get low."* 〔`200409/msg00060`, `200409/msg00119`, `200409/msg00121`〕 Cited only, not verified. | Treat "it behaves the same on every run" as a property of this harness, not of the console, whenever a ROM reads RAM or INTIM before writing it. `defuse`'s `ReadBeforeWrite` finds the RAM half statically; the INTIM half has no detector. For a ROM of our own the rule is unchanged — initialise everything — but for **studying a commercial ROM**, determinism here can hide the very behaviour being studied. | `rg 'RandomState' Gopher2600 --include='*.go'` (18 hits) and `rg 'RandomState' internal cmd pkg` (0). | `Gopher2600/hardware/riot/timer/timer.go`, `hardware/memory/vcs/ram.go`; stella-list 2003 (Berzerk/INTIM); stella-list `200409/msg00060`, `200409/msg00119`, `200409/msg00121` (Eckhard Stolberg, Alex Herbert, Edwin Blink); found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-1) |
| **F8/F6/F4 boot in a random bank — TRUE OF HARDWARE, AND NOT OF WHAT WE MEASURE WITH.** `mapper_atari.go`'s `reset()` picks `Random.Intn(len(cart.banks))` **only when the `RandomState` preference is set**, and `preferences.go` `SetDefaults` sets it to **false**, with nothing in this repository changing it — so our boot bank is `SetBank(Loader.Bank)`, deterministic. A litmus for "the boot bank is random" would show it fixed, and the fixed result would be about our settings. The list draws the same distinction first-hand: *"what if the cart startup is **random (I think so)**"* about hardware, and in the same message *"from testing my games, though, I think Stella always starts in the last bank of an image. Not randomly"* about the emulator 〔stella `199801/msg00368`, Greg Troutman〕. Third of these found in two days, after SuperChip SARA and the VSYNC threshold. The advice below is unchanged — it is what hardware does that matters. |  every bank's reset entry must `JMP` to a common init in bank 0 | static (check each bank start) | 194935, 293970, 261488 |
| **A random start bank broke homebrews and not the old games — and Stella did not show it for one of them** | TomSon, 2017, had his own flash cartridge start in a random bank for the schemes he believed are not fixed at power-on: *"quite a few home-brews had problems - but not the old games - that I stopped this right away"*, *"(just to name one: 'Stay Frosty' dies starting in bank 3, works in other banks (is X==bank guaranteed on harmony, maybe ?!)"*. Thomas Jentzsch checked his copy — *"Mine does NOP $FFF6,X too"* — and *"within Stella the game always starts correctly, even with randomized registers. But it seems that Stella always chooses bank 0"*. SpiceWare traced the `,X` to start-up code he had copied from supercat's post about menu integration. So the bank that start-up selects depends on X at power-on — derived from the thread, which does not settle the cause; Not verified. The row above has a 1998 Stella starting in the last bank; this is a 2017 Stella starting in bank 0. Both are fixed. The Stella observation is about Stay Frosty alone (AtariAge `topic/266200`; Cited only, not verified). | as the row above: every bank's reset reaches a common init, and no register is used as an index before the program has set it | none here — our boot bank is fixed too (row above) | AtariAge `topic/266200` (TomSon, Thomas Jentzsch, SpiceWare) |
| **A bank switch that lands on no code can still look right — through `BRK` and its vector** | Christopher Tumber, 2003, answering *"what's incorrect in this file and why it works anyway?"* about an 8K program: the switch was an `LDX $1FF9` at `$xFEA`, so execution continues at `$xFED` in the other bank — *"Changing banks does not affect registers, including the program counter"* — and the other bank had nothing there. *"So, when your program switches to Bank 2, it presumably hits a BRK ($00). Hitting a BRK causes the program to jump through the BRK vector at $fffe"*, which held `Start`: *"So hitting the BRK has the unintended side effect of sending your program to the start of Bank 2 which is what you really want and "looks right" so it still works okay. Similarily, when switching from Bank 2 to Bank 1, there's also nothing at the destination. (Except more BRKs)"*. "Presumably" is his; nobody in the thread runs it (Cited only, not verified). That the empty bytes are `$00` depends on how the assembler fills them (§D, the `ORG` fill row). Fix: put code at the address after the switching instruction in the bank being switched to — *"When you switch banks, your code continues on from the same point, not at the start of the other bank"* — and do not let a vector cover for a missing landing | manual: read what sits at the address after each switch in the bank it selects | stella-list `200312/msg00140`, `msg00142` (Christopher Tumber) |
| **`NOP`/`BIT` reading `$00`-`$3F`** as a skip on 3F/X07 carts | triggers an unintended bankswitch. On 3F the condition is **A6 and A7 both low** (`addr&0x10c0 == 0`, from the engine's `mapper_tigervision.go`), which in zero page is all sixty-four of `$00`-`$3F` — `nop $04` and `bit $2C` are as dangerous as `nop $00`. `$40`-`$7F` sets A6, `$80`-`$BF` sets A7, so **`NOP $80` is safe for a reason**, not by convention. X07 is wider, and the engine has no X07 mapper: per its spec, while bank 14 or 15 is selected any TIA-space access (A12, A11 and A7 low) switches banks, A6 choosing which, so on X07 `$40`-`$7F` is a hotspot too and `NOP $80` still survives (AtariAge `topic/277674`; Cited only, not verified). `scripts/check_traps.py` flags only `$00`-`$3F`. ★Also emitted by **`SLEEP` with an ODD value** (`nop $00`, or `bit $00` under `NO_ILLEGAL_OPCODES` — the switch changes the opcode and keeps the address), and by a **raw-byte skip** (`.byte $2C`), neither of which a mnemonic grep sees. For an odd delay use `PHP`/`PLP`: 7 cycles, 2 bytes, all flags restored (measured, `internal/emu/oddsleep_test.go`), and besides its own ROM fetches it touches only the stack — in the engine's `cpu.go` PHP writes and PLP reads `mc.SP.Address()` (read from the source; the test does not watch the bus: Not verified) — so a push lands wherever SP points: the same test measures two PHPs from SP=`$1E` turning on ENAM1 and ENAM0. Or `JMP *+3`: 3 cycles, 3 bytes, flags kept, only its own ROM fetches (`JMP abs` is 3 cycles in the engine's `definitions.json`; not measured here). ★**Which raw byte to skip with is a flag question too.** `$2C` is `BIT abs` and sets N and V from the byte it reads and Z from `A AND` that byte (`internal/emu/bitflags_test.go`); the NOP forms change no flag. Thomas Jentzsch, the same day he posted a `.byte $2c` skip for a paddle kernel 〔`200301/msg00187`〕: *"I prefer using an undocumented (illegal) NOP instead ($82 or $$0c), because that doesn't change the flags"* 〔`200301/msg00201`; `$$0c` is his typing〕; omegamatrix: *"Illegal NOP's are used by a lot of 2600 programmers instead of BIT, since all flags are preserved"*, pairing `$24`/`$04` to skip one byte and `$2C`/`$0C` to skip two (AtariAge `topic/215833`). The two Jentzsch names are not interchangeable: per the engine's `definitions.json`, `$0C` is `nop` absolute, three bytes, and reads the address it swallows exactly as `$2C` does, so the 3F/X07 hazard above is unchanged; `$82` is `nop` immediate, two bytes, and reads only its own operand. The same table gives the costs: `$04` two bytes and three cycles, `$0C` three bytes and four, and `$80`, `$82` and `$89` two bytes and two — agreeing with omegamatrix's list of flag-safe forms, `$04` at three cycles and `$80`/`$89` at two (AtariAge `topic/180714`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). Counted from the same table on 2026-10-05, there are 27 undocumented `nop` opcodes besides `$EA`: six of one byte and two cycles, five immediate (two bytes, two cycles), three zero-page (two bytes, three cycles), six indexed of two bytes and four cycles, and seven of three bytes and four cycles (`$0C` and six indexed forms the table marks page-sensitive) — the *"a lot of undocumented NOPs with different lenghts and cycles"* of Jentzsch's 2001 post (his spelling) 〔`200103/msg00052`〕. That is the engine's table; which of them a given console runs as a plain NOP is not measured here (Not verified). Jentzsch had shown in 2001 why the flags matter, with code from his Thrust: a `.byte $0c` that swallows an `adc` after an `sbc`, ahead of the `bvc` and `bmi` that test the result, commented *"here I use an illegal NOP because I need the overflow- and negative-flags!"* 〔`200103/msg00052`; Cited only, not verified〕. So use `$2C` only where N, V and Z are dead after the skip. ★**A branch that was meant to be unconditional can hang on this.** Lee Fastenau, 2004, rebuilt a kernel with `dey`, `SLEEP 13`, `beq .enterHere`; it ran in z26, a Supercharger run went wrong, and he *"had to change an "unconditional" BEQ to a JMP"* 〔`200409/msg00091`〕. David Galloway read the listing: the macro's legal form emitted `bit VSYNC` (`24 00`) plus five `nop`s, so *"what you have is the reliance on bit VSYNC setting the Z bit in the flags"*, with Z set as the accumulator AND the byte read 〔`200409/msg00094`〕; Thomas Jentzsch: with illegal opcodes allowed it would be `nop VSYNC`, *"And there would be no problem"* 〔`200409/msg00096`〕. This is the `SLEEP <odd>` case above seen from the symptom side: the delay changed the flags a later branch read (Cited only, not verified: Galloway's reading, nothing here ran Fastenau's kernel or a Supercharger). ★**A read there can trip a latch but cannot fetch a cartridge's value.** DirtyHairy, designing a larger 3E-style scheme for the UnoCart and turning down a `JMP (0033)` through two of its registers: *"Unfortunately, we can only write to those registers. Once we read, the TIA will respond and drive the bus"* (AtariAge `topic/279113`; Cited only, not verified). The TIA drives D7 and D6 of such a read (§E); the other six bits are the bus-residue question above. ★A skip byte can also be chosen to make both paths cost the same. SpiceWare's paddle macros are `lda INPT0 / bpl .save / .byte $2d / .save sty Paddle1`, commented `; 3 - always 9`: 2 + 4 when the branch falls through into the swallowing `AND abs`, 3 + 3 when it is taken. `$2D` is `AND abs`, so it also overwrites A and sets N and Z; his indexed variant of the same macro uses `.byte $2c`, and he gives no reason for the difference (AtariAge `topic/250156`; Cited only, not verified). ★**The other way to share bytes between two paths is to branch into the middle of an instruction**, so that its operand runs as an opcode. omegamatrix, 2011, showing a `.byte $04` that swallows a one-byte `clv` on the path that falls through, said the skip is the form that makes more sense to a reader (AtariAge `topic/180714`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). | static: `nop`/`bit` with an operand in `$00`-`$3F`, `SLEEP <odd>`, and `.byte` NOP/BIT forms (the last scoped to files targeting a TIA-decoding mapper) | 139089; the address range and the `SLEEP` bytes measured 2026-09-05, `internal/emu/oddsleep_test.go`; AtariAge `topic/279113` (DirtyHairy), `topic/250156` (SpiceWare), `topic/180714` (omegamatrix); stella-list `200103/msg00052` (Thomas Jentzsch), `200409/msg00091`, `msg00094`, `msg00096` (Lee Fastenau, David Galloway, Thomas Jentzsch) |
| **A `BIT` skip in a shipped game touched an "illegal address", and the thread does not agree that it mattered** | dionoid, 2018, from a 2017 PRGE panel (at 27:43): Garry Kitchen, crunching Keystone Kapers into 4K, *"used a BIT instruction as fall-through to save an extra byte"*, and *"they found out that the BIT instruction was accessing an 'illegal address'"*. SpiceWare: *"if he was crunching the code to get within 4K, that suggests the code he was working on was larger than 4K. Maybe the bit instruction accidentally accessed a bank switching hotspot."* omegamatrix pointed at the flags instead: *"It is possible that there was unexpected dependency elsewhere in the code that the bit instruction changed the flags for (N V Z). If the register that was being BIT tested was undefined then it could turn into a nasty bug."* jeremiahk, after watching the talk: *"it sounds like there may not have even been a problem. They had a machine that tested the code, and it was found that an "illegal address" was being accessed"*, and *"I don't think there was actually a real problem with the code, the machine just gave a warning."* debro, from his own disassembly: *"The alert probably triggered on the BIT instruction on $04A9, $05A9, $91E6, $93E6, $A8F6, and $A0D6."* (AtariAge `topic/283906`; Cited only, not verified — the talk itself was not heard here). | Choose a skip's swallowed address as well as its opcode, and use `BIT` only where N, V and Z are dead (row above) | `scripts/check_traps.py` checks `$00`-`$3F` (row above); debro's six addresses are all above that range | AtariAge `topic/283906` (dionoid, SpiceWare, omegamatrix, jeremiahk, debro) |
| **On X07, switching from TIA space is the design, not an accident** | The scheme's description, quoted in the thread's only post: while bank 14 or 15 is selected, *"accessing 0000 will select bank 14 (Eh, 1110b) while accessing 0040 will select bank 15 (Fh, 1111b). This allows for bankswitching by accessing TIA registers at 00-3F or 40-7F without incurring any overhead."* It also says *"Apparently, this was only used on one cart: Stella's Stocking."* So on X07 the accesses the `NOP`/`BIT` row warns about are also how a program is meant to switch: a TIA access it already makes is the switch — derived, Not verified (AtariAge `topic/277674`; the post's question, which address to use, has no answer in the held copy). | On X07, decide which TIA accesses are meant to switch while bank 14 or 15 is selected, rather than only keeping skips off `$00`-`$3F`. | `scripts/check_traps.py` flags only `$00`-`$3F` skips, and the engine has no X07 mapper (the `NOP`/`BIT` row) | AtariAge `topic/277674` |
| **read of a write-only hotspot / SuperChip write-port** | undefined on HW (varies by console) → never read it. ★**The access that switches is such a read, and its flags outlive the switch.** A Supercharger switch is `CMP $FFF8`; Stella and Harmony *"both reveal the byte that is "under" the hotspot, which actually is $00 in all three banks"* (batari), the real cartridge does not, and a caller that branched on the flags its routine had set before returning through the switch — `jsr getbitstatus / beq` — worked in both and failed on the Supercharger, where the sprites got stuck (mr-sql, with Eckhard Stolberg, 2014). What the real one returns was left open: Stolberg's test read the byte at `$FFF8` after one sequence and a newly written value, *"most of the time"*, after another (AtariAge `topic/221197`; Cited only, not verified). Set the flags again after any call that returns through a bank switch. Engine read, not measured: `supercharger.go`'s `access` applies the configuration at `$0FF8` and then returns the RAM or BIOS byte at that address, the byte under the hotspot as Stella and Harmony did | **runtime/ROM-level** (was static/manual — see below) | 169114, 285759, 204819, 111536 |
| **STA to ROM** | no R/W line to the cart → bus contention, never persists; RAM is `$80–$FF` only. ★**What "contention" actually does, added 2026-09-07:** the two drivers fight and the loser is whichever is trying to hold the line HIGH. Bill Heineman, 1998, asked what happens if two ROMs are selected at once: *"the 5 volts from the FF would be **grounded** and all the current would be drained by the chip giving the logic 0. **In most cases this results in a logical "AND"**. Hence **`0xFF AND 0x00` will equal `0x00`**"* 〔`199812/msg00015`〕 — and it is not free: *"You would use a LOT of power… not immediately, but over time the **heat damage** to the chip trying to drive the bus high would **shorten the life of the ROM** by a lot"*. ★★**The engine models neither.** Measured 2026-09-07 by writing `$00` over a ROM byte holding `$FF` and reading it back: it still reads `$FF`, so the write simply has no effect rather than producing the AND. ★★★What was NOT measured is the value on the bus **during** that cycle, which is where the AND would show and where a phantom read or a TIA read of residue could pick it up — nothing in the litmus reads it, and the engine has no contention model for it to read. **This one line is the CAUSE of three other entries in this repository, 2026-09-04.** The cartridge connector carries address and data and *no direction signal*, so a cartridge that wants writable memory must encode direction in the address itself. Everything downstream follows: **(a)** a SuperChip's 128 bytes occupy **256 addresses** — the first 128 write, the second 128 read — which is why its write port is not statically decidable and why a naive dumper reads back whatever it just wrote (`Gopher2600/hardware/memory/cartridge/fingerprint.go:584`: *"because there is no r/w line in the cartridge bus, there 256 addresses related to the superchip"*); **(b)** the Supercharger needs a stateful arming sequence instead — touch `$F0xx` to arm it and the **fifth** subsequent address transition becomes a write regardless of the instruction: the engine sets `Delay = 6`, decrements it on every address *change* (not every access), and commits at `Delay == 1`. Derived from that arithmetic and from alex_79's 2013 account on AtariAge `topic/215572`, not from a traced execution — see the caveat under the Supercharger row in `docs/techniques/bankswitching.md`; **(c)** every "is this address a read or a write" question in the mappers is answered by convention rather than by a signal. Erik Mooney and Eckhard Stolberg, 1998, stated the fork plainly: with no R/W line you either **spend an address line as the direction bit** (halving the RAM) or **do something more complicated, like the Supercharger**. | static | 148390, 62852, 293816; cause traced by the mailing-list distillation (helper-1) |
| **A `STA` to a bank-switch hotspot switches, and is still a write into ROM** | Kylearan, 2015, had seen F4 switches done with `BIT 1FF4` or `LDA 1FF4,x` and asked whether `STA 1FF4` works *"(reliably)"*: *"It works with Stella, but does it work also on real hardware?"* ZackAttack: *"My guess would be that STA would cause bus contention on real hardware and possibly damage or crash the system. But, that's just a guess."* SpiceWare answered with an illegal `NOP` — `nop SelectBank4`, `0c fa ff` in his listing, *"which works just fine"*, his Collect2 having *"originally used CMP SelectBank#"*. Then crispy: *"Centipede uses STA to do its bank switching. It made me nervous when I first discovered what they were doing, but apparently it's OK since I haven't heard of masses of 2600 consoles melting down into piles of sculptured plastic after running Centipede."* Kylearan, who had already changed his `STA`s to `NOP`s: *"the preservation of flags has saved me 160 cycles during overscan"* (AtariAge `topic/239091`). A cartridge that imitates the bus is less forgiving. TomSon, 2017, on his own: *"Almost all cartridges out there do a 'do not care for value' read of those registers. Some a write, just as a trigger"*, and *"Writing is a problem for the Tiara since a 6507 write together with the uProc putting data on the bus does lead to problems … It reliably crashes the bus emulation"* (AtariAge `topic/266200`). Both Cited only, not verified. On a plain ROM cartridge the write meets the ROM's own output, as in the row above — derived, Not verified. | Switch with a read. `nop` absolute (`$0C`) keeps the flags and `BIT` does not (§C's `NOP`/`BIT` row). | `scripts/check_traps.py` accepts a `STA` into cartridge space only on a line marked `@rom-write-ok` (the `STA` into cartridge ROM note near the top); nothing here models the contention (row above). | AtariAge `topic/239091` (Kylearan, ZackAttack, SpiceWare, crispy), `topic/266200` (TomSon) |
| bank-move misaligns code → page-cross | split heavy moves across frames / `ALIGN 256`. ★**`ALIGN 256` costs ROM in a step, not a slope**: it pads to the next multiple of 256, so the padding jumps from 0 to 255 the moment the code in front of it passes the boundary. Thomas Jentzsch, answering an author whose free space fell by far more than he had added: if the `ALIGN` sits at e.g. `$F800`, one byte added before it loses 255 more (AtariAge `topic/287020`). Count one added byte as a possible 256 when budgeting ROM. splendidnut, recommending `align 256` before a table: *"The downside is that the ROM will be padded with zeros to make that happen. So you'll want to keep that in mind if/when you start running out of space."* (AtariAge `topic/360395`; Cited only, not verified). ★Padding is not the only fix: Dionoid keeps each routine and table in its own file and says the split *"allows for reshuffling of the include-files to prevent page boundary issues"*, with a `CHECK_PAGE` macro that stops the build (`ERR`) when a block's last byte sits on a different page from its first (AtariAge `topic/345618`; Cited only, not verified). ★In code someone else wrote, the safe step is a whole page: enthusi, to an author editing a disassembled demo, *"you can only safely move tables or labels in full page steps (256 bytes). Otherwise you might introduce or lose extra cycles for crossing page boundaries"* (AtariAge `topic/257563`; Cited only, not verified) | static (ALIGN) | 307854, 339019, 133720; 287020; 345618; 257563; 360395 |
| **"It runs on a Harmony" is not "it runs on a SuperChip"** — a Harmony/Melody implements SuperChip RAM in its own way, so a hardware test on one is a test of that implementation. batari, 2011, after two owners reported self-modifying code in SuperChip RAM running fine on a Harmony: *"the way Superchip RAM is implemented in Harmony, there should be no issues running self-modifying code there. I can't say this will work with real Superchips, though - I expect code in RAM will run, but likely not self-modifying code in RAM"* — Harmony latches a write and commits it after any address change, while *"A real Superchip might not detect all address changes"*. He splits it once more the next day: code in ROM writing RAM code *"should"* work on a real SuperChip, code in RAM writing RAM *"may not"*. Stella and z26 ran it too. ★**Then someone tried a SuperChip board, and it was worse than batari expected.** wickeycolumbus, who had offered his F4SC EPROM board for the test, three weeks later: *"it is not possible to run self modifying code from SC RAM, or basically any code for that matter. The most you can run is one byte"* — a lone `rts` in SuperChip RAM returned, an `inx` followed by `rts` did not take effect. batari: *"Superchip hardware must do something weird to break code execution. There was actually a note back in the day that said it wouldn't execute code, and it looks like they meant it."* The tester himself left it open — *"it could be that I just did something wrong"* — and one board is one sample. ★A 2022 voice gives a reason and a cartridge that differs: SvOlli, whose demo keeps its code in ROM and uses the CommaVid RAM for data, answering what CommaVid offers over other cartridges with RAM — *"you can execute code in it (the original SARA chip does not allow that, it's too slow.)"*, and *"other solutions mimicking the SARA chip, like the Harmony and Uno 2600, do not have this limitation"*; Thomas Jentzsch, asked whether Stella models that speed: *"No, I don't think so."* ★The 2011 test program also shows the shape such code must take, because every byte of that RAM has two addresses: its nine bytes were written through the write port (`$F000`-`$F008`) and called with `JSR $F080` through the read port — seagtgruff: *"the main trick is simply to remember to modify the RAM code using the write addresses, but remember to execute it using the read addresses"* — and its `STX $F001` rewrites the operand of the routine's own opening `LDX #$00`. | Name the cartridge a hardware test ran on. Do not put code in SuperChip RAM for a real SuperChip; a Harmony pass covers Harmony/Melody only | none here — Cited only, not verified | AtariAge `topic/182372` (seagtgruff, batari); `topic/342021` (SvOlli, Thomas Jentzsch) |
| variable placed at `$FF` | `JSR` push lands on `$0100` mirror and clobbers it → keep vars in `seg.u` from `$80`. ★**How far down the stack reaches is a function of call depth, not a fixed band.** Each `JSR` pushes two bytes and `PHA` one, so from `SP = $FF` five nested calls and a `PHA` write `$F5` — nukey-shay's example, 2008, to a beginner whose code misbehaved around a variable `Run` at `$F5`, after another reply suggested something else was writing it (AtariAge `topic/119719`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified — the thread ended without showing that the stack was what changed it). `scripts/check_traps.py` rule 3 warns only for `$F8`-`$FF`, so `$F5` is outside it: count the deepest nesting plus pushes before placing variables near the top. ★**Borrowing the stack pointer as a spare register is the same question asked from the kernel.** johncl, drawing a six-digit score, kept a value in the stack register because the transfer costs 2 cycles, just enough to fit the last `sta GRP1`, and asked whether that works on hardware or only in Stella; omegamatrix answered that the stack *should* be safe as long as you are not pushing lots of return addresses and are not near RAM you use, and suggested watching it in Stella's debugger while the routine runs rather than calling it fine (AtariAge `topic/136387`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). ★**Why `DS` from `org $80` rather than an `EQU` per variable.** Jim Nitchals, 1996, to a newcomer asking what `DS` is: `org $80` / `ListOfPieces DS 16` / `OtherVariable DS 1` / `TEMP DS 2` *"is easier than saying: ListOfPieces EQU $80 / OtherVariable EQU $90 / TEMP EQU $91 and less error-prone"* (his lines joined with `/`). On our reading the error it avoids is the hand count: `DS` advances the address, so a variable inserted in the list moves every later one, and the last address is the assembler's rather than a sum someone kept (Cited only, not verified). ★**Code run from zero-page RAM meets the same mirror from the other side.** An instruction stream that runs past `$00FF` takes its next byte from `$0100`, which the engine's memory map gives to the TIA (the `CLEAN_START` row in §D), so the next opcode is a TIA read. A 2020 thread, held here only as distilled notes, has that byte come from the collision registers and, with no collision, be `$00`, a `BRK` (AtariAge `topic/304940`; Cited only, not verified). A TIA read drives only some of its bits (the bus-residue table near the top of this file), so the rest are whatever the bus leaves and `$00` is not certain — our reading, Not verified | static (var in $F8-$FF warns) | 302998, 301766, 290790, 119719, 304940, 136387; stella-list `199610/msg00029` (Jim Nitchals) |
| **Leave the top eight bytes empty: `org $fff4` / `.ds 8` keeps a program of 4K or less off every F8/F6/F4 hotspot** | curtisp, 2017, to an author whose program (1K at the time) ended `org $fffc` / `.word START ; RESET` / `.word START ; BRK`: *"if you can spare the 8 bytes"*, put `org $fff4` / `.ds 8 ; Bank Switching Hotspots` before the same two words — *"This will keep your code from accessing any of the bank switch areas, any problems on unmodified Superchargers or Flash Carts."* and *"Of course, this assumes you can keep the entire ROM below 4k."* `$FFF4`-`$FFFB` holds every hotspot of the three Atari schemes in the engine's `mapper_atari.go` — F8 `$0FF8`-`$0FF9`, F6 `$0FF6`-`$0FF9`, F4 `$0FF4`-`$0FFB`, as cartridge offsets (read from the source; Not verified) — so no code or table of the program sits on one (AtariAge `topic/267481`; Cited only, not verified). ★**Ending the code just below the hotspots is not enough.** Eckhard Stolberg, 2001, on a 4K beta of Qb and the Supercharger: *"He doesn't use $fff8, but he has a RTS at $fff7. And since the 6507 always reads two bytes, $fff8 gets accessed anyway, so that this version of Qb won't work on a Supercharger."* 〔stella-list `200103/msg00018`; Cited only, not verified〕 This engine makes the same read: for an implied-mode instruction `cpu.go` reads the next byte — *"the next instruction is read but the PC is not incremented"* — and whether a mapper here acts on that read is not checked (engine read, Not verified). After `org $fff4` / `.ds 8` the last byte of code is at `$FFF3` at the highest, so its extra read lands on `$FFF4` — which is F4's first hotspot listed above, so the eight bytes keep code off the hotspots but not this read — derived, Not verified. The same read used on purpose to switch banks, an `RTS` on a hotspot, has one report of a similar trick that *"worked on CC and emulators, but not on real hardware"* — Thomas Jentzsch's, with the thread's other side, in `docs/techniques/bankswitching.md` 〔`200503/msg00037`; Cited only, not verified〕. | in a program of 4K or less, end with `org $fff4`, `.ds 8`, then the reset and BRK words | nothing here checks it | AtariAge `topic/267481` (curtisp); `Gopher2600/hardware/memory/cartridge/mapper_atari.go`; stella-list `200103/msg00018` (Eckhard Stolberg); `Gopher2600/hardware/cpu/cpu.go`; stella-list `200503/msg00037` (Thomas Jentzsch) |
| **A 2K program reaches the Supercharger's `$FFF8` through its upper mirror** | retrofiends, 2013, on which programs a Supercharger can run: *"You just need to avoid any program access from rom adresses $xFF8 on up. 2k by nature is no problem, since they only use 2k of the addressing space."* alex_79 narrowed it the next day: *"In theory even 2k games can crash: in a 2k cartridge there's an address pin missing (the A11 line), so the rom is mirrored twice in the 4k address space and if the program refers to addresses in the upper mirror ($x800-$xFFF), then there's the possibility to trigger the sc bankswitch. Address $x7F8 and $xFF8 both points to the same phisical address of the rom chip, but using the second one will crash the game on a supercharger."* (his spelling; AtariAge `topic/215572`; Cited only, not verified). | Keep offset `$7F8` of a 2K image free of code and tables, which the row above's `org $fff4` / `.ds 8` does for a 2K program assembled at `$F800` — derived, Not verified. | Nothing here checks it. | AtariAge `topic/215572` (retrofiends, alex_79) |
| **The NMI vector at `$FFFA` is never used by a 6507 — and our decoder still starts there** | reveng, 2016, reviewing a first program: *"Also the NMI vector isn't required/supported on the 6507, so you can skip that and save the 2 bytes of ROM."* The 6507 brings no NMI pin out of the package, and in the engine `CPU.Interrupt` has no caller under `hardware/` (`internal/cyclebound/absint.go`) (AtariAge `topic/253669`; Cited only, not verified). | use the two bytes when they are needed | `internal/cyclebound`'s `decodeFromVectors` decodes from `$FFFC`, `$FFFA` and `$FFFE`, so if the two bytes at `$FFFA` read as a cartridge address, decoding starts there too, and what it finds joins the program that `prove_line_budget` and `defuse` analyse — read from the source, not run (Not verified). A source that starts its vectors at `org $FFFC`, as `roms/litmus/litmus_pos.asm` does, leaves DASM's fill at `$FFFA`, `$00` by default (§D's `ORG` row), which is outside the cartridge and is skipped — derived, Not verified | AtariAge `topic/253669` (reveng); `internal/cyclebound/cyclebound.go` |
| **A file's size is the first check — and the legal sizes are a list, not the powers of two** | DeafAtariFromKansas, 2023, on a beta (`OhNoMorePeril_b2.bin`): *"ROM B2 won't work with Stella, Look like ROM size is incorrect.. 92.71K??"*; the author: *"Yeah, I made a mistake and uploaded an incorrect file."* The engine's list, in `fingerprint.go` after the formats it recognises by content (read from the source; Not verified): 4096, 8192, 10240/10495/10496 (DPC), 12288 (FA), 16384, 24576, 32768, 65536, 131072 and 262144 bytes, plus 8195 for a known bad dump; any other size of 4096 or more is refused as `unrecognised size`, and anything smaller is taken as 2K. The tools around a cartridge keep lists of their own: curtisp, 2017 — *"1K is not always recognized as a valid ROM size. Stella doesn't mind it, but MAKEWAV choked on it"* — moved a 1K program to `org $f800` to make it 2K (AtariAge `topic/353077`, `topic/267481`; Cited only, not verified). ★**Two bytes over is the assembler's output format, not the program.** `internal/build` passes `-f3`; without it DASM writes its default format, which puts the two-byte load address in front, so a 4096-byte cart comes out at 4098 with every byte displaced by two (the comment in `internal/emu/romsize_test.go`). Ruffin Bailey, 1998, explaining his earlier trouble getting a binary to run: *"I think I had a wrong option turned on in DAsm that put a two byte header in the binary, so Stella either choked on the header or ran _really_ strangely if you caught it just right"* 〔stella-list `199811/msg00055`; Cited only, not verified〕. `LoadROM`'s own floor in `internal/emu/emu.go` (`minCartBytes` = `0x100`) lets 4098 through, as that comment says; it is the engine's fingerprinter behind it, given `"AUTO"`, that would refuse it, since 4098 is not on the list above (`unrecognised size`) — derived, Not verified. | check the `.bin`'s size against what its mapper and every tool after it expect, before publishing it | the engine refuses an unlisted size — Not verified; `TestEveryBuiltROMIsACartridgeSize` (`internal/emu/romsize_test.go`) fails any `.bin` under `roms/` that is not a power of two of at least 2048 bytes, `cart_dpc` excepted | AtariAge `topic/353077` (DeafAtariFromKansas, vhzc), `topic/267481` (curtisp); `Gopher2600/hardware/memory/cartridge/fingerprint.go`; stella-list `199811/msg00055` (Ruffin Bailey); `internal/emu/romsize_test.go` |
| **A commercial image's bytes at cart-RAM and hotspot addresses may be the dumper's, not the cartridge's** | Adam Wozniak, 2003, building a dumper: *"When dumping a cart with RAM, the cart RAM contents are essentially random."* His dumper writes `$FF` there, and the Dig Dug image he found on the net *"has random data here. Of course the two images have different checksums and md5sums."* Eckhard Stolberg's reader *"uses $00 for the unused RAM bytes"*. On the hotspots, Stolberg: *"The binaries that have something else there have most likely been read with one of the older cartridge dumpers, that leave out the hotspots, but don't clear their file buffer between games. So if a 4K game has been read and then a 8K game, the hotspots would contain the data from the 4K game even in the 8K binary."* Wozniak had found binaries with *"real data there"* and read the same bytes from his own carts, so he took reading them as *"more correct"*; Stolberg did not think any game relies on them. Stolberg also named *"early F8 dumps"* made *"by hitting one of the hotspots and then reading the entire 4K bank"*, so that *"both banks contain the system vectors of the second bank"* 〔stella-list `200301/msg00185`, `msg00199`, `msg00209`, `msg00213`〕. Cited only, not verified. | Before reading meaning into those bytes of someone else's image, treat them as the dumper's; two dumps of one game can differ there | Nothing here marks them | stella-list `200301/msg00185`, `msg00199`, `msg00209`, `msg00213` (Adam Wozniak, Eckhard Stolberg) |

## D. CPU / 6502

| trap | fix | detect | source |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The tool answered a different question and sounded certain — the investigator's traps, and their name** | Three rows here have been read as one family of "tool traps". They are two, and the split is **who is deceived**. ★**When the PROGRAM is wrong and the tool accepts it** — a forgotten `#`, an assembler taking `jmp (abs,x)`, DASM promoting `LDX zp,Y` — the deceit lands in the binary, and those rows belong beside the other 6502 traps, where they are. ★★**When the INVESTIGATOR is wrong and the tool agrees** the binary is fine and the *belief* is wrong, and nothing downstream can catch it, because there is no artefact to check. That second family is the one with a history here. `rg -rn` replaced every match with the letter `n` and exited 0, and its output had already been written into a technique page as a count. A phase probe reported **62** single-phase assertions and every one was false — it paired output lines to plan entries by position while the runner emits frame-major (`gate-ledger.md`). `check_wiring` asked *"has every doc got a reader?"* for months and never *"has every reference got a target?"*. A search for a quotation returned nothing because it required two things that lived in two messages, and the archive was recorded as not containing it. And DASM, told to assemble a source whose `processor` line is missing, prints three lines naming three instructions and never mentions line 1. ★★★**Every one of these was loud, fast, and confidently wrong about the thing it was not measuring.** | **Ask what the instrument can see, not only whether it ran.** Three habits, each earned: a result that is *surprisingly clean* deserves the same suspicion as a result that is surprisingly bad — four times on 2026-09-05 a checker reported mass failure and the checker was wrong, and on 2026-09-06 one reported an absence that was not there. **A zero needs a positive control**: `phase_probe.py --selftest` fires on a known alternation before the sweep is believed. And **the give-away is usually one value, not the count** — `bank.number` cannot be 177 on a cartridge with four banks. | `rg -rn` found by the distillation (helper-1); the assembler pair and the forgotten `#` by helper-2 〔`199710/msg00045`〕. **The grouping was left open deliberately**: helper-2 wrote two of the three rows and declined to rule on whether their own additions formed a family. Measured by `git blame` before ruling — 323 helper-1, 324 and 339 helper-2, none by the session writing this row |
| **`rg -rn` is not "recursive + line numbers"** | ripgrep's `-r` is `--replace` and **takes an argument**, so `-rn` means *replace every match with the letter `n`*. The output looks like a normal search result with the searched-for words missing and no line numbers, and the exit status is 0. ripgrep recurses by default, so `-r` was never needed. It silently corrupted two searches here on 2026-09-04, one of which had already been written into a technique page as a claim about how many places call `CPU.Interrupt()`. | Use `rg -n`. If a result reads oddly — a quoted line missing the very phrase you searched for — check the flags before believing the content. | Re-run with `rg -n` and compare. A `--replace` run and a plain run differ visibly on any file that matches. |
| **An impossibility and its counterexample have to be about the same ROM** | 2017, Todd's 5.51 in Dragster: the thread opened on Omnigamer having reverse-engineered the game and found the time impossible, from a spreadsheet model of its code. ZackAttack: *"I think proving it is impossible is about as feasible as proving there are no purple giraffes. Just cause you have never seen something and you don't know how to find it doesn't mean it doesn't exist. Of course proving the opposite is as simple as providing a single counterexample."* The same day Thomas Jentzsch: *"using my old hack which limits the revs, I was able to score exactly 5.51 just a minute ago. Go figure! And the sheet is wrong."* ZackAttack on what that did not yet show: *"Technically we should take this one step further and repeat those results with an unmodified ROM."* Four days later Jentzsch had checked the sheet against the code — *"I even created my own sheet based on his one. And I did not find any mistake or missing parameter"* — fed the sheet's input to the game through another hack, *"And the result is exactly like his sheet"*, and concluded *"Nevertheless, based on my current knowledge, I have strong doubts that 5.51 is possible with the dumped NTSC ROM."* The same day Omnigamer reported that someone had *"used my spreadsheet model and exhaustively checked it via a dynamic programming algorithm"*. The 5.51 had run on a rev-limited hack, so it was never a counterexample to a claim about the original ROM, and the model was not refuted (AtariAge `topic/269192`; Cited only, not verified). | Name what an impossibility was proved over — which ROM, which model, which search space — and what a counterexample ran on. A run on a different artefact refutes nothing about this one, however decisive it looks; an exhaustive search is exhaustive over its model. | The ∀ tools here are of the first kind: `prove_line_budget` is exhaustive over the program it decoded, and reports the regions it could not bound instead of passing them. | AtariAge `topic/269192` (Omnigamer, ZackAttack, Thomas Jentzsch) |
| **A beam position read in the wrong coordinates "corrected" a timing that was right** | sunpazed, 2026, co-authored a game in assembly with Claude Code and taught it to drive Gopher2600's headless debugger. Checking a `sta HMOVE` that its comments placed at cycle 74, the agent broke on that instruction's address, read `FR=0001 SL=049 CL=145`, and concluded: *"At the gap's sta HMOVE, the beam is at SL=49, CL=145 (color-clock 145 of 228 ≈ CPU cycle ~49) — not the "cycle 74" my comments and mental model claim. The kernel positions correctly and the 262 fix is verified, but this means my documented timing rationale (and the "post-HMOVE WSYNC eats a line because HMOVE is at 74" reasoning behind the eaten-line explanation) doesn't match what the chip actually does. The emulator reads it directly; I was reasoning from a wrong premise the whole time."* Nobody in the thread questioned the reading (AtariAge `topic/391096`). The engine prints that line with `fmt.Sprintf("FR=%04d SL=%03d CL=%03d", s.frameNum, s.scanline, s.clock-specification.ClksHBlank)` (`hardware/television/television.go`), and `ClksHBlank = 68`: `CL` counts from the first visible pixel with HBLANK negative, the convention `CLAUDE.md` gives for beam coordinates. Read that way, `CL=145` is colour clock 213 of 228, the start of CPU cycle 71, and a three-cycle `sta HMOVE` that starts there writes on cycle 73 counting from 0, 74 counting from 1 — the cycle-73/74 strobe of §A. Derived from the source, Not verified: the ROM is not here, and where in an instruction a PC breakpoint stops has not been checked. | Find the origin of a clock before reading a position off it. A reading that overturns a careful count gets the same check as one that confirms it. | Nothing here relabels a number read off the engine's own terminal. | AtariAge `topic/391096` (sunpazed); `Gopher2600/hardware/television/television.go` |
| **two assembler traps from the list that our toolchain does NOT have — checked 2026-09-04 — and a third, `LDA (ptr),X`, and the 65C02's `BBR`/`BBS` — and DASM 2.20.10's `lda #-1`, reported fixed, not checked here** | 1997: an assembler accepted `jmp (abs,x)`, a 65C02 addressing mode the 6507 does not have, and the author lost time to a program that assembled and misbehaved. 2003: DASM silently promoted `LDX zp,Y` to `abs,Y` (+1 cycle, no error) while *rejecting* `LAX zp,Y`, so a documented and an undocumented instruction with the same addressing mode were treated differently; half a day lost. ★Also 2003: `LDA (pointerP0Graphic),X` assembled for Kirk Israel and drew *"random garbage that varies even with P1 flapping"* 〔`200309/msg00093`〕. The 6502's indirect modes are `(zp),Y` and `(zp,X)` only; Andrew Davie on why it went through: *"I'm surprised the assembler is letting you do this, but it's probably interpreting the statement as an expression with indexed addressing. Which would come out to basically just lda pointerP0Graphic,x"* 〔`200309/msg00094`〕 — the parentheses read as grouping, so it reads RAM at the pointer's own address plus X. Six years before, Greg Troutman had written in the comments of a beginner's corrected copy loop that `LDA (TEMP),X` *"is illegal (may actually assemble without an error message"*, *"as..."* `LDA (TEMP,X)`, *"which is different than what you want and would produce goofy results)"* (comment semicolons dropped) 〔`199709/msg00374`〕 — a different guess from Davie's at what an assembler makes of it, and both are hedged (Cited only, not verified). ★2000: a 65C02 datasheet lists `BBR0`-`BBR7`/`BBS0`-`BBS7` (branch on bit reset/set), and Mark De Smet noticed that *"The datasheet makes note of the instructions that are new from the original 6502, but these are not marked as such"*; doppel: *"the BBR0-7 and BBS0-7 instructions are not supported on the original NMOS 6502, or on the 6507"* 〔`200007/msg00051`, `200007/msg00053`〕. ★2005, a regression in DASM itself: Andrew Davie, releasing 2.20.10b, *"I have been made aware of an error introduced into 2.20.10 -- lda #-1 and similar will not compile. Use instead lda #<-1."* mathys66 found it by stepping 2.20.07 and 2.20.10 side by side: *"Version 2.20.07 had a typedef called ulong (in asm.h)"*, `typedef long ulong`, *"actually a signed value"*, and *"In version 2.20.10 this typedef has been removed, and all (?) occurences of ulong have been replaced by "unsigned long""*; B. Watson: *"That was partly my fault"* 〔stella-list `200508/msg00055`, `msg00115`, `msg00116`〕. Cited only, not verified. | Nothing to do. **DASM 2.20.14.1 here rejects `jmp ($F800,x)` outright** — *"error: Illegal Addressing mode"*, fatal — and assembles `lax tbl,y` to `b7 80`, two bytes, no promotion and no error, matching `ldx tbl,y` at `b6 80`. Measured 2026-09-30 with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository): `lda (ptr),x` is *"error: Illegal Addressing mode '(ptr),x'"*, fatal, exit 3, while the control `lda (ptr),y` assembles to `b1 80`. Measured 2026-09-24 with the same DASM version (a scratch build outside this repository): under `processor 6502`, `bbr0 $80,L` stops with *"Unknown Mnemonic"*, exit 5. `lda #-1` was reported, in the mailing-list distillation's run of 2026-08-27 outside this repository, to assemble to `a9 ff`, the same bytes as `lda #<-1` — Not verified here. | Re-run the two one-line fixtures if the toolchain version changes; both traps are version-specific. |
| **`LDA zp,Y` does not exist, and the assembler builds something else without a word** | Zero-page,Y exists only for `LDX`/`STX` (and the undocumented `LAX`/`SAX`); `LDA` with `,Y` exists only as absolute,Y. So `lda $90,y` cannot be encoded as written and comes out as `lda $0090,y`: one byte longer, and a read that is page-sensitive where `lda $90,x` is not. The engine's instruction table says so — `$B9` `lda` absolutey, 3 bytes, pageSensitive; `$B5` `lda` absolutex, 2 bytes, not; `$B6` `ldx` absolutey, 2 bytes (`Gopher2600/hardware/cpu/instructions/definitions.json`, where a zero-page form is spelt `absolute` with 2 bytes). Thomas Jentzsch, 2023: *"There is no opcode for lda zp,y. Instead the assembler creates lda abs,y, which requires 1 extra byte. But there is an opcode for lda zp,x. So to save space, using X when loading from ZP RAM is more efficient."* ★It also hides a typo: a 1999 kernel wrote `LDA $90,Y` where `LDA ($90),Y` was meant — a pair of parentheses between an indirect read through a pointer and an indexed read of RAM — and it assembled; the first reply was *"Shouldn't this be LDA ($90),Y?"*. Unlike the `LDX zp,Y` promotion in the row above, this is not a toolchain bug and no DASM version will make it go away. ★The list answered it again in 2004, for an author who had saved *"a lot of rom"* by changing `,y` to `,x` in his copy loops and *"can't see any rason why"*. Erik Mooney: *"Actually, the \_only\_ instructions that can do an 8-bit zeropage address with a Y index, are LDX and STX. Everything else always uses a 16-bit address if indexing with Y (DASM will silently do that for you.) It takes the same 4 cycles in either mode, so it doesn't matter except for the one extra byte of ROM."* — documented instructions only; the first sentence of this row adds `LAX`/`SAX`. Eric Ball gave the exception to "the same 4 cycles": *"ABS,X/Y will go over a page boundary (+1 cycle), while ZP,X/Y will not. So with ZP,X/Y you can have a negative index."* 〔stella-list `200404/msg00313`, `msg00316`, `msg00321`; Cited only, not verified〕 ★**Nor is there a `cpx abs,y`, and going without it costs cycles.** Thomas Jentzsch, 2003, trimming a kernel that compared its line counter in X with a table: `txa` / `ldy counterPFBlock` / `cmp BlockTable,y`, commented *"no cpx abs,y, too bad! :-("* — the counter is copied to A first, and his listing counts the `txa` as 2 cycles 〔stella-list `200309/msg00144`; Cited only, not verified〕. The engine's `definitions.json` has `cpx` at `$E0` (immediate), `$E4` (absolute, 2 bytes — its spelling of zero page, as above) and `$EC` (absolute, 3 bytes) only (read from the source). | Index zero-page RAM with X. Where an indirect read was meant, write the parentheses, and check the opcode in the listing (`B1`, not `B9`). | DASM emitting `b9 90 00` with no warning and exit 0 is the distillation's report — Not verified here.
