Nothing here flags the form. | AtariAge `topic/352846` (Thomas Jentzsch); stella-list `199909/msg00025`, `199909/msg00026`; `200309/msg00144` (Thomas Jentzsch); `200404/msg00313`, `200404/msg00316` (Erik Mooney), `200404/msg00321` (Eric Ball) |
| **`lda #<Label,Y` does not exist either: an immediate takes no index** | shazz, 2013, wanted a pointer partway into a routine of `nop`s and wrote `ldy #10` / `lda #<MyRoutine,Y` / `sta RoutinePtr` / `lda #>MyRoutine,Y` / `sta RoutinePtr+1`; he got *"RoutinePtr = $0000...."* and does not say whether the assembler complained. SpiceWare: *"There's no such command as LDA Immediate,y ."* For the offset: *"If the 10 is a constant value, let DASM do the work"* — `lda #<[MyRoutine + 10]` and `lda #>[MyRoutine + 10]` — and *"If it changes during runtime, use 16 bit addition"*: `clc` / `adc #<MyRoutine` / `sta RoutinePtr` / `lda #0` / `adc #>MyRoutine` / `sta RoutinePtr+1`, with the offset in A (AtariAge `topic/215637`; Cited only, not verified). | Put a constant offset inside the expression, in brackets; add a run-time one with a 16-bit add. The `.w` in the same thread is the row below's. | What DASM does with `lda #<MyRoutine,Y` has not been assembled here (Not verified). The engine's `definitions.json` has no indexed immediate mode: its modes are absolute, absolutex, absolutey, immediate, implied, indirect, postindexed, preindexed and relative (read from the source). | AtariAge `topic/215637` (shazz, SpiceWare) |
| **`$0013` is still zero page: leading zeros do not choose the addressing mode** | Just Jeff, 2018, wanted a four-cycle store to time `RESM1` and wrote `sta $1300`; SeaGtGruff pointed out that the absolute form is written `$0013`; ZackAttack: *"Try sta.w resm1. $0013 = $13 so it probably still assembles as ZP without explicitly saying otherwise with .w"*. SpiceWare gave the reason — the operand is evaluated to a number, and *"Once evaluated, the assembler has no idea if there were leading zeros or not because $0001, $01, and even just 1 all evaluate to the exact same 32-bit binary value of 1"* — and quoted DASM's force extensions (`b`, `z`, `d` for a byte address; `w`, `e`, `a` for a word). SvOlli's form needs no extension: *"I use $100+RESM1 for this case."*; TheHoboInYourRoom: *"The assembler would add the value of the RESM1 symbol ($13) to $100 giving an effective address of $0113, forcing the assembler to emit the absolute version of whichever instruction you're using, while exploiting the mirroring of the TIA's address space in the VCS so that the same register is hit in either case."* RevEng: *"I think a "$0013" operand resulting in a ZP opcode isn't contradictory, even if it might be counter-suggestive to some."* (AtariAge `topic/280751`; Cited only, not verified — nothing here has assembled `$0013`.) | Force the width with `.w` (`sta.w RESM1`) or write `$100+RESM1`; the engine's memory map gives `$0100`-`$017F` to the TIA (the `CLEAN_START` row below). | The listing: `sta` zero page is `85`, two bytes, three cycles; `sta` absolute is `8d`, three bytes, four (the engine's `definitions.json`). | AtariAge `topic/280751` (Just Jeff, SeaGtGruff, ZackAttack, SvOlli, TheHoboInYourRoom, SpiceWare, RevEng) |
| **DASM hanging on `REPEAT`/`REPEND` — one more for the `our toolchain does NOT have` row** | rbairos, 2017: *"I've tried 3 versions of dasm (one I just compiled from scratch to be sure), and it keeps hanging/crashing"*, *"Whether it not it compiles successfully is completely finicky."*, *"Deleting an extra blank line will fix the problem etc."* Thomas Jentzsch's first answer was a workaround — *"DASM has problems with Linux CR/LF. So if you are on Windows, make sure that the file is not in Linux format."* — and he added *"Somehow the problem is only occurring with REPEAT."* RevEng, who could not reproduce it on Linux, *"gave a theory a shot"* with a rebuilt DASM; Thomas Jentzsch tried it on a source with Unix newlines and answered *"That seems to fix it."* RevEng's account of the change: *"I changed the fopen() call used to open the assembly source file (and includes) to use binary mode ("rb") instead of text mode. ("r")"*, because *"For non-POSIX systems, text mode will translate newlines. Unfortunately the functions responsible for reporting/moving the current position within the file (ftell and fseek) don't have a correct definitive behavior when translating newlines"*, and *"The dasm REPEAT/REPEND functionality uses ftell and fseek to bounce around in the assembly code, which made me think of the above being the cause of the bug."* — a cause inferred and confirmed by one test, not traced (AtariAge `topic/272727`; Cited only, not verified). | Nothing to do on this machine, which is POSIX: the distillation of that thread assembled one `REPEAT` source with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository) with LF and with CRLF line ends, and got identical 4096-byte images with the loop expanded ten times — the distillation's report, Not verified here. `REPEND` is in use here — `roms/litmus/cyclebound_branch.asm`, `roms/litmus/litmus_vdel_bits.asm`. | Re-run the LF/CRLF pair on a Windows build, or when the toolchain changes. | AtariAge `topic/272727` (rbairos, Thomas Jentzsch, RevEng) |
| **A DASM macro called with an argument missing stops with "Not enough args", and `IFNCONST` inside it did not prevent it** | Greg Troutman, 1997: *"I tried using the IFNCONST and MEXIT commands to try and not assemble when a parameter isn't provided, but I keep getting, "Not enough args passed to Macro.""* — the list did not reply 〔stella-list `199704/msg00000`; Cited only, not verified〕. The distillation of that message assembled a two-argument macro called with one argument under DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository) and got the same message word for word, followed by `Illegal character` errors on `{2}`, and exit 5; its reading is that `{2}` is substituted before the conditional is evaluated, so the conditional never sees a missing argument. It tried `IFNCONST`, not `MEXIT` — the distillation's report and reading, Not verified here. Write one macro per argument count. | DASM's own error and a non-zero exit (5 in the distillation's run) | stella-list `199704/msg00000` (Greg Troutman) |
| **`(zp,X)` does not take a page in X** | BNE Jeff, 2019, with 14 graphics pointers in 28 bytes of RAM, all sharing one high byte, read `LDA ($44,X)` in a 6502 guide and hoped X held the page, so that `ldx #$FB` / `lda (Player0Ptr,x)` could drop the high bytes. Nukey Shay: *"Radically different address modes"* — *"With (indirect,x) the value of x determines which base address to use from a group of base addresses"*, so `ldx #2` / `lda ($80,x)` reads through the pointer at `$82`/`$83` — and *"Since zero page is usually in short supply on most platforms, this address mode is far less useful than (indirect),y"*; and *"you are stuck with using 2 bytes for either address mode"*. On the 2600 all 128 bytes of RAM are zero page, so a table of pointers for `(zp,X)` takes its RAM from the variables — derived, Not verified (AtariAge `topic/291287`; Cited only, not verified). ★**The same pair mistaken in a copy loop.** childofcv, 2021, disassembling an NES ROM (Mike Tyson's Punch-Out), asked about `LDA ($31),Y / INY / STA ($E1,X) / INX / DEC $E0 / BNE copy_loop`; with a count of 2, *"The first byte went to the intended location (5C5), but the second was written to 405."* Thomas Jentzsch: *"The code might work if X would be increased by two."* `(zp),Y` adds Y to the address it read; `(zp,X)` adds X to where the pointer is read from, so `INX` steps onto a pointer that overlaps the first (AtariAge `topic/325492`; Cited only, not verified). ★**A pointer whose low byte sits at `$FF` takes its high byte from `$00`, not `$0100`.** Asked in 2005 by someone building a 6502 in Verilog, Bryan answered *"All zero-page instructions use an 8-bit PC (they actually shut off the high-byte) so they wrap at $FF"*, and djmips quoted VICE's `64doc` for `(zp),Y` — *"The effective address is always fetched from zero page, i.e. the zero page boundary crossing is not handled"* — adding *"I cannot gurantee that this document is authoritative"* (AtariAge `topic/72382`; Cited only, not verified). It is the zero-page twin of the `JMP ($xxFF)` wrap, which is measured (`litmus_6502`, `verified-coverage.md`); the zero-page case is not. On the 2600 `$00` is the TIA, which answers a read there as `CXM0P`, so such a pointer's high byte would come from a collision register — derived, Not verified. ★**And once more with a sprite pointer.** Glenn Saunders, 2001, could not get a pointer to his car shapes to work — *"I don't think I understand how to do this properly"* — and the attempt, left commented out in the source he posted, was `LDA (P0_CurrentFramePtr,X)` with a row counter in X. B. Watson: *"You're trying to use LDA (addr,X)... you should be using LDA (addr),Y"*, and *"There are no (addr),x or (addr,y) modes, either... X is always pre-indexed and Y is always post-indexed."* Thomas Jentzsch gave the same swap, `LDY P0_CurrentRowCounter` / `LDA (P0_CurrentFramePtr),Y` 〔stella-list `200110/msg00085`, `200110/msg00086`, `200110/msg00087`〕 (Cited only, not verified). | To save the high bytes, Nukey's alternative: *"Have you looked at Alien? That game uses a single byte for each sprite as a pointer to 2 page long data tables. Slower than (ind),y but more Ram efficient"*, with the sprites *"limited to that 512-byte range"*. The pointer bytes can also be temporary RAM outside the kernel *"so long as you reset them before their display area(s) begin"*. | Nothing here flags it. Its one cheap use, `lda ($80,x)` as 6 cycles in 2 bytes, is in the delay table below. | AtariAge `topic/291287` (BNE Jeff, Nukey Shay); `topic/325492` (childofcv, Thomas Jentzsch); `topic/72382` (Bryan, djmips); stella-list `200110/msg00085`, `msg00086`, `msg00087` (Glenn Saunders, B. Watson, Thomas Jentzsch) |
| **A pointer built at run time needs two stores, and the program assembles with one** | `(zp),Y` reads its address from two bytes, low at `zp` and high at `zp+1`; set-up code that stores only one of them leaves the other holding whatever was there. Andrew Davie, 2020, reading a playfield routine another poster had offered to shorten someone's game: *"I haven't looked at the logic of that code, but the above is missing a `sta temp2+1` just before the loop"*, and, inside the loop, *"it looks like it should be `lda (temp2),y` not `lda (temp1),y`"* (AtariAge `topic/307123`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). As the excerpt is held, the fragment stores `#>` (the high byte) at `temp2`, so adding that store alone would leave the two bytes in the reverse of the order `(zp),Y` reads — our reading of a distilled excerpt, Not verified. The score kernel here avoids the question by setting a pointer's high byte once at start-up, because its font sits in one page (`docs/techniques/score-kernel.md`). Store both bytes of every pointer, low byte at the lower address, next to each other in the source. | Nothing here flags a pointer with one byte stored. `defuse` records no read of the pointer bytes: `accessOf` marks an `(ind)`, `(ind,X)` or `(ind),Y` access `Unbounded` (*"the pointer lives in RAM and is not tracked"*, `internal/cyclebound/defuse.go`), and `ReadBeforeWrite` skips unbounded accesses, so it cannot see the unstored byte whatever wrote it before (read from the source, not run) | AtariAge `topic/307123` (Andrew Davie) |
| **`processor 6502` in column 1 is a label, and then every instruction is "unrecognized"** | DASM reads a word that starts in column 1 as a label, so `processor` becomes a label and `6502` is looked up as a mnemonic. Tempest, 2001: *"I'm getting all kinds of unrecognized mnemonic errors. I start my program with processor 6502 include vcs.h and it says It doesn't recognize 6502 or vcs.h"*; Manuel Polik: *"You need to TAB both lines for DASM. Now it's assuming 'processor' as label and '6502' as mnemonic."* The first error does name line 1 — Tempest's own words are *"doesn't recognize 6502"* — but as an unknown mnemonic, not as a misplaced directive. Measured 2026-09-30 with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository): `processor 6502` in column 1 gives `(1): error: Unknown Mnemonic '6502'`, the same source indented assembles, and with the `processor` line removed line 1 is not named at all — the missing-`processor` case in the investigator's-traps row above. | Indent every directive and mnemonic; start only labels and `=` equates in column 1. When an error calls `6502` a mnemonic, the directive before it is in column 1. | The assembler's own error on line 1; no fixture in this repository. | stella-list `200102/msg00251`, `200102/msg00253` |
| **A label that does not start in column 1 is read as an instruction** | The mirror of the row above. A beginner assembling a tutorial got `error: Unknown Mnemonic 'Reset'.` and `'StartOfFrame'.`, and with a colon added, `'Reset:'.` — the colon did not help. debro: *"Make sure these labels (Reset and StartOfFrame) are all the way on the left with no spacing or tabbing."* Andrew Davie: labels and symbol definitions *"must start in the very first column (that is, no whitespace before them). Opcodes and assembler directives (eg: LDA or ORG) must NOT start in the very first column."* The same output listed both names as unresolved with `(R )`, which another helper read as a RAM address; debro: *"The ® means the symbol was referenced."* The thread did not close cleanly — the author removed the space, still saw the error, and got a build after changing a `jmp` (AtariAge `topic/54451`; Cited only, not verified). | Start labels and equates in column 1 and indent everything else, as in the row above. | `internal/build`'s hints do not name it — read from the source, not run (Not verified): `mnemonicStormHint` counts only 6502 instruction names and `operandAsMnemonicHint` only tokens that start like a value, and `Reset` is neither. | AtariAge `topic/54451` (jesterdev, Nukey Shay, debro, Andrew Davie) |
| **A prototype's source can still carry its development system, and DASM stops on it** | Joust's 1983 prototype source, assembled in 2017, stopped with `segment: Bank0 1000 eqm vs current org: e004` and `error: Origin Reverse-indexed.` Its first bank opened with `ORG $E000` and four bytes commented as debugging controls (*"STORE 80 IN HERE TO FREEZE"*, *"SLOWDOWN SPEED"*); nukey-shay: *"the ORG for the "development system" is still getting in the way. So cut the IF ORIGINAL text and paste it a bit higher between the SEG and ORG lines"* — after which it *"Assembles just fine now when ORIGINAL = 0"*. The error names an origin, not the lines that set it. ★**What the message means.** nanochess, 2019, to just-jeff, whose build stopped at the vectors with `segment: CODE fffa vs current org: 10000`: an `ORG` is moving the location counter backwards — the code has already passed the address that `ORG` names. There the counter stood at `$10000`, past the end of a 4K image, so, read from the printed addresses, the code had outgrown the space before `$FFFA`; in the Joust case above it was a development-system `ORG` instead, so the message says which origin was overrun and not why (AtariAge `topic/287020`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). | Before assembling a period source, find the `ORG`s that belong to the development hardware and the conditional meant to switch them off, and assemble with it off. | Not verified here — no such source in this repository. | AtariAge `topic/262401` (doctorclu, nukey-shay); `topic/287020` (nanochess) |
| **`-o` without its `o`: DASM prints its whole usage and names nothing** | doctorclu, 2017, typed `-joust.bin` for the output file. DASM 2.20.11 printed its banner, its full usage — one line of it *"-oname output file name (else a.out)"* — and *"Fatal assembly error: Check command-line format."* `-j` is not in that list, and the answer was on the screen without being pointed at. His next try dropped the option: *"Fine! Just output it as "a.out". I'll just rename the stink'in file manually. Whatever..."* (AtariAge `topic/262401`; Cited only, not verified.) Measured 2026-09-30 with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository): the full usage comes out when DASM is given two source files or an option it does not know. ★**The same screen in 1999, for options that belonged to another program.** Ruffin Bailey, with the Mac port: *"When I just type DAsm -pafs spam.asm > spam.bin, I just get the help file outputted to the worksheet."* Its porter answered that the syntax is *"dasm sourcefile options"*, with the source file first, and *"BTW, -p isn't a valid option"*; Eckhard Stolberg: *"the command line options you used with it are for DISTELLA not for DASM. (If you exchange the .asm with the .bin, that is.)"* 〔stella-list `199903/msg00048`, `199903/msg00049`, `199903/msg00051`〕 (Cited only, not verified). The porter meant to change that option handling; whether DASM 2.20.14.1 still needs the source first was not checked here (Not verified). | Write `-o` and the name with no space between; SpiceWare's line in the same thread is a template: `dasm collect.asm -f3 -v0 -scollect.sym -lcollect.lst -ocollect.bin`. | Not reachable through `internal/build`: `Assemble` and `AssembleWithListing` pass `-o` joined to the path themselves, with the source as the first argument (`internal/build/build.go`). A hand-typed `dasm` meets it. | AtariAge `topic/262401` (doctorclu, SpiceWare); stella-list `199903/msg00048`, `msg00049`, `msg00051` (Ruffin Bailey, the Mac port's porter, Eckhard Stolberg) |
| **Old source against today's `vcs.h`: names it already defines, and reads it no longer puts at `$30`** | Today's `vcs.h` defines every register as a label (`ds 1` inside `SEG.U`), so a source that defines them again meets it. kamakazi's 2008 code redefined `COLUP0`, `CTRLPF`, `SWCHB` and others with `EQU` after the include, and batari answered that those equates are all predefined, except that `CNTRLPF` is now `CTRLPF`. Measured 2026-09-17 with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository): `COLUBK EQU` with a **different** value after the include is fatal and writes no output (a label mismatch and an `EQU` value mismatch), and the same line before the include fails too; with the **same** value it assembles silently, byte-identical to the build without it. The values in kamakazi's code match `vcs.h`, so the redefinitions were not what broke it. ★The read registers moved as well. Thomas Jentzsch, 2013, to someone whose DASM 2.20.11 would not build his Pitfall! disassembly: *"Add `TIA_BASE_READ_ADDRESS = $30` at the very beginning of the file. My code uses something like `CXP0FB-$30` because the DASM TIA read address was HARDWIRED TO $30 back then."* `vcs.h` 1.04 added that switch *"for convenient disassembly/reassembly compatibility"* (its own changelog); it defaults to `TIA_BASE_ADDRESS`, which defaults to 0. | Before assembling a period source, delete its own register equates or check them against `vcs.h`, and set `TIA_BASE_READ_ADDRESS` before the include when the source assumes reads at `$30`. | DASM stops on a different value; a matching duplicate passes and is harmless. No ROM under `roms/` includes `vcs.h` (the 3F note near the top). | AtariAge `topic/129762` (kamakazi, batari), `topic/213258` (samiam, Thomas Jentzsch) — both held here only as distilled notes, so the wording is not checked against the posts: Cited only, not verified; `vcs.h` 1.06 as shipped with DASM 2.20.14.1 |

It is the row above with a label in place of the `EQU`. Measured 2026-09-21 with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository), two sources that include the same `vcs.h` and differ only in one label's name: with `MyStart`, `Complete. (0)` and exit 0; with `VBLANK`, `error: Label mismatch` at both the header's definition and the source's, then `Fatal assembly error: Source is not resolvable.`, exit 3. | Give no label a name `vcs.h` already defines. | The assembler stops. | AtariAge `topic/96515` (Thomas Jentzsch) |
| **DASM says `Complete.`, exits 0, and writes a zero-byte binary** | Manuel Polik, 2003, once `vcs.h` had started defining segments: old unsegmented sources produced *"a zero length file"*, with the old DASM as well as the new, and *"after almost an hour"* he found the cause, *"->Segments<-"*. His fix was one more `SEG` (`SEG Bank0`) at the end of `vcs.h`; Andrew Davie *"added a dummy SEG at the end of the file"*, and the header has ended that way since 1.03. Reproduced 2026-08-31 with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository): a header that opens `SEG.U` and a body with no `SEG` gives `Complete.`, exit 0 and a 0-byte `.bin`; one `SEG` line at the end of the header gives 4096 bytes. So the cause is an uninitialised segment left open into the code. ★**Our build refuses it** (since 2026-10-02). After an exit of 0, `internal/build.Assemble` and `AssembleWithListing` check the size of the image DASM wrote and refuse a 0-byte one, naming an open `SEG.U` as one cause (`quietlyIncomplete`); DASM prints nothing here, so the size is the only mark. `TestAssembleRejectsQuietlyIncompleteImages` (`internal/build/diagnosed_test.go`) builds this source and its `SEG`-closed control with the real DASM. | End every header that opens `SEG.U` with a plain `SEG`. Check the size of the `.bin`, not only the exit status. | `Assemble` and `AssembleWithListing` refuse the 0-byte image (`quietlyIncomplete`). | stella-list `200305/msg00040` (Manuel Polik), `200305/msg00041` (Andrew Davie); the `vcs.h` 1.03 changelog line; `internal/build/build.go` |

| **`ORG`'s fill value takes effect late, at the next directive that generates data** | DASM's manual, posted by Andrew Davie: *"NOTE that no filler is generated until the first data-generating opcode/psueoop is encountered after this one"*, with the example `org 0,255 / org 100,0 / org 200 / dc 23`, which *"will result in 200 zero's and a 23"* — the 255 is never written. The delay is what lets a later `ORG` move back to a lower address without an error, as long as nothing was generated in between. Measured 2026-09-24 with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository), `-f3`, after a first `dc 1`: the 199 bytes before `dc 23` are all `$00`; with the last `org` changed to `org 200,255` all 199 are `$FF`; and an `org` with no value keeps the previous one (`org 0,255 / org 100,255 / org 200` fills with `$FF`). | Put the fill value on the `ORG` that opens the gap it is meant for, or use the forms the same post names: *"Normally, DS and ALIGN are used to generate specific filler values."* | Read the bytes in the gap; nothing here checks fill values. | AtariAge `topic/302251` (Andrew Davie) |

Dennis Debro, 2017, reverse-engineering Barnstorming: *"There is a place in the code (i.e. $F3EF) where I need to push code 3 bytes into the next page. I send BOUNDARY 3 to the macro and it pushes to $F3F0 instead of $F403 because the mod of $F0 and 0 is 0."* `$F0` is 240, a multiple of 3. His rewrite, which also let the fill byte be set to something other than `$00` (*"Barnstorming fills empty bytes with $EA"*), did not go into `macro.h`: *"I guess I maybe the only one using the BOUNDARY macro"* (AtariAge `topic/267367`; Cited only, not verified). | To place code at byte `n` of the next page, pad to the page with `ALIGN 256` and then `n` bytes, and check the address in the listing | No source here calls `BOUNDARY`; the `SLEEP` check in `scripts/check_traps.py` was measured on the same `macro.h`, not on this macro | AtariAge `topic/267367` (Dennis Debro); DASM 2.20.14.1 `machines/atari2600/macro.h` |

| **DASM labels are case-sensitive: one letter's case makes a second label** | B. Watson, in the same reply as the row above: *"Also, DASM is case-sensitive, you've used HSPFirstLoop and HSPFIrstloop to mean the same label, but to DASM they're 2 different labels."* The DASM V2.12.04 output he attached spells the unresolved one `HSPFIrstLoop`, and lists `HFPFIrstLoop` beside it, so his `HSPFIrstloop` looks like his own retyping. Measured 2026-09-23 with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository): without the parentheses `error: Value must be <$100`, exit 5; with them the instruction assembles, `a2 2d`. | **Data the CPU walks into is executed** | A table assembled straight after code is, to the 6502, the next instruction. krool885: graphics *"stored in a table … seems to execute as code, as sometimes the graphics become illegal opcodes in the emulators debug view and break everything"*; splendidnut: *"once the "Clear" loop finishes, the very next byte the CPU encounters is your graphics data. You either need to move the data, or jump over it."* A 2013 case showed only a black screen, and RevEng's answer was the same: *"Stick your data outside of the code flow. What you've written will cause your data to be executed as opcodes."* The symptom shows in the picture; the cause is the end of a loop. | Place tables after an unconditional `jmp`/`rts`, never between two pieces of code that run in sequence. | Nothing here flags it; krool885 saw the table's bytes as illegal opcodes in the emulator's debug view. | AtariAge `topic/345516` (krool885, splendidnut); `topic/210789` (RevEng) |
| **In a disassembly, an immediate load can be half of a pointer, and moving what it points at breaks the program without a word** | just-jeff, 2016, editing a DiStella disassembly of a demo, had cut the data tables after `ORG $F000` and found *"it messes up the whole thing"* — though, he wrote, *"I've moved and deleted tables all the time"* in his own code. Thomas Jentzsch: *"The labels are not the problem. But some data (e.g. immediate loads or tables) contains pointers. E.g. After LF7A2 the code loads and stores the song pointers. Unless you replace the immediate loads with labels (like in my disassembly), DASM only sees two fixed values here. But if the song data address has been changed, the song pointers will be pointing at the wrong address. And the song data itself is also just a bunch of pointers. Without labels you cannot move the data they are pointing at, too."* One such pair in the same listing, `lda #$40 / sta $96 / lda #$1F / sta $97`, sets the pointer to `$1F40` for the `lda ($96),y` that follows. ★**The listing's silence about code is not proof either.** Of a block after a `JMP` that no label points into: *"unless there is an indirect jump like JMP() or BRK (which doesn't exist in this code), which DiStella cannot detect, this code is never executed."* (AtariAge `topic/257563`; Cited only, not verified.) ★**The same break in 2002, from one added byte.** Joel Park, working on a labelled Adventure disassembly, added a line to the sword graphic (Prescott's reply says *"the arrow graphic"*) and removed one of the *"like 9 bytes that are labeled as "Unused""* at the end to stay at 4K. The sprites looked right, *"the dynamic backgrounds"* did not (*"a garbled mess nearly impossible to navigate"*), and he could not find a place that used an address instead of a label. Brian Prescott: *"The problem is in the RoomNumToAddress subroutine. This routine calculates an address into the room data table. By adding a byte to the arrow graphic, these addresses are all off by one."* The fix was a `RoomDataTable` label at `FE1B` and a final address built from `#<RoomDataTable` / `#>RoomDataTable`; Park afterwards: *"Your fix worked, I'm able to move code around at will with no changes to the gameplay."* 〔stella-list `200206/msg00018`, `200206/msg00019`, `200206/msg00030`; Cited only, not verified〕 | Before moving anything in a disassembly, find the immediate pairs stored into two adjacent bytes and replace the constants with `#<Label` / `#>Label` — derived from the thread, Not verified; and move by whole pages (the `bank-move misaligns code` row in §C). | Nothing here relabels a disassembly. `internal/cyclebound` does not guess an indirect target either: a region holding an indirect `JMP` is reported as *"indirect JMP in region (target not statically known)"* (read from `cyclebound.go`). | AtariAge `topic/257563` (just-jeff, Thomas Jentzsch); stella-list `200206/msg00018`, `msg00030` (Joel Park), `msg00019` (Brian Prescott) |
| **In a disassembly, `LDA VSYNC` is a read, and a read of `$00` is `CXM0P` — and it may be there for its three cycles** | Manuel Polik, 2000, found a blank-line loop in Starmaster with an `LDA VSYNC` and four `NOP`s after the `STA WSYNC`, and asked what they were for. Thomas Jentzsch: *"LDA VSYNC is a problem of DiStella, it doesn't show read accesses correct (should be LDA CXM0P)."* Polik, a day later: *"The LDA CXM0P is really for nothing, just an instruction taking uneven (3) cycles. I found out that the subroutine which does the player positioning must start after 15 cycles and you can't do that with just DEX + BPL + x * NOP , they're all taking even cycles..."* — the loop's exit is the `BPL` not taken, two cycles; taken it is three. A listing that names a read by the register written at that address hides both: what is read, and that the read is padding. The read names are `$00`-`$0D` (the static-trap note at the top of this file). Cited only, not verified; whether `cmd/dissect`'s annotated listing names reads correctly is not checked here. | Read an address in a load by its read name; when an instruction's result is never used, count its cycles before deleting it. | manual | stella-list `200012/msg00005`, `msg00006` (Thomas Jentzsch), `msg00007` (Manuel Polik) |
| **In a disassembly or a trace, a `JMP` to an address outside the ROM can be a jump into it** | A-Team's second bank, assembled with `RORG $F000`, starts `LDA $FFF8` / `JMP $BC96`. Manuel Polik, 2002, first read that jump as *"heading towards nirvana"*, then saw z26's log carry on at `bc96: sei` and asked *"How does this work?!?"* 〔stella-list `200212/msg00081`, `msg00084`〕. Thomas Jentzsch: *"1. The log is wrong! 2. The 6507 has only 12 address lines -> The jump goes to $xc96. Or $fc96 if you should prefer that"* 〔`200212/msg00090`〕; Glenn Saunders: *"The 6507 has 13 bits of available addressing off the chip through the pins. The 2600 throws away a bit on the way to the cart port."* 〔`200212/msg00096`〕 The two differ on the count and agree on the effect: the upper address lines do not reach the cartridge, so `$BC96` and `$FC96` are the same ROM byte. A listing or trace that shows a target outside the cartridge window (`$1000`-`$1FFF` and its repeats, such as `$F000`-`$FFFF`; this bank is assembled at `$F000`, its jump target reads as `$BC96`) is not an error for that reason alone. The ROM mirroring is in `docs/fundamentals-audit.md`, where it is listed as not measured by us; the A-Team bank is Polik's report and was not examined here (Cited only, not verified). | Before calling a target outside the window a bad disassembly or a jump into nothing, drop the bits above A12 and look there. | manual | stella-list `200212/msg00081`, `msg00084` (Manuel Polik), `msg00090` (Thomas Jentzsch), `msg00096` (Glenn Saunders) |
| **A source listing is not necessarily the shipped ROM, and assembling it is how you find out** | Dennis Debro, 2004, transcribing Moonsweeper from a listing *"supplied to me"*, kept in the format *"Bob originally had it"* (the post does not say who supplied the listing): *"I've compiled the DASM version and it works. This doesn't compile to the ROM that's been dumped though. I know of one table that had moved in the final ROM. I denoted it in the DASM version."* He also had to take the `XTABLE` data from a DiStella listing, since the supplied listing lacked it: *"Hopefully it was the same but no harm done if it isn't."* 〔stella-list `200406/msg00015`〕 One report on one game: the post does not say which table moved, and nothing here assembled or compared it (Cited only, not verified). | Take the ROM's bytes as what shipped. Where a source is the reference for a table or a label, assemble it and compare the result with the dump first. | manual: assemble, then compare the bytes with the dump | stella-list `200406/msg00015` (Dennis Debro) |
| missing `CLD` | BCD math gives garbage; D is undefined at power-up. ★**`CLD` in init does not protect a kernel that pulls flags.** Kirk Israel's 2004 kernel pointed SP into the TIA's enable registers and ended each line with `PLP`; it broke on a Supercharger while *"it's fine and dandy on Z26 and PCAEwin"* 〔`200403/msg00077`〕. Christopher Tumber: *"you pull an undefined value from the stack onto the flags which is having the unfortunate effect of turning on the BCD bit and screwing up your arithmatic operations. Replace both instances of PLP with PLA"* 〔`200403/msg00130`〕, after making it come back by adding a `SED` before the missile skipdraw 〔`200403/msg00120`〕. The pulled byte is a read of a TIA address, so which machine sets D is the bus-residue question above. The author was left unsure which of two fixes did it — *"I'm not positive if it was the PLP/PHP thing, or my messed up CLEAN_START"* 〔`200403/msg00132`〕 (Cited only, not verified). `docs/techniques/missiles-bullets.md` already restores SP with `PLA`. ★**The other way round is quiet: a forgotten `SED` breaks a threshold, not the picture.** Piero Cavina, 1997, in the Oystron 2.9 release notes: *"I had forgotten to set the decimal mode before adding to the score to beat to win the next ship, whith the results that you can imagine... :-/"* 〔stella-list `199711/msg00000`〕. Soeren Gust asked why he now got an extra ship every 4000 points where 2.8 had given one every 5000, *"Is it a bug or a feature ?"*; Jim Nitchals: *"Feature. Without the BCD score bug, you'd get fewer extra ships with a 5000 point setting. At 4,000 it's roughly the same number as before."* 〔`199711/msg00018`, `199711/msg00019`〕 So the bug had given more extra ships than a 5000 setting gives without it; the thread does not say how far off the score was, and the detector in this row looks for `CLD`, not `SED` (Cited only, not verified). | static (`CLD` in init) | 318346, (CLAUDE.md); stella-list `200403/msg00077`, `msg00120`, `msg00130`, `msg00132`; `199711/msg00000` (Piero Cavina), `msg00018` (Soeren Gust), `msg00019` (Jim Nitchals) |
| `ADC` without `CLC` / `SBC` without `SEC` | carry contaminates the result. ★**Leaving `CLC` out on purpose needs a carry that is known, and the compare that makes it known has to be the unsigned one.** e1will, 2009, needing two cycles in a kernel, asked whether after `cmp (BK_PTR),Y` / `bmi SomeplaceElse` the carry is always set at the following `adc`, so that `CLC` could go and the constant be made one less *"and just let the carry get added in"*; both values were within `$00`-`$7F`. batari: *"Given your explanation, the carry should always be set, but you should use this code, as it will not fail with values larger than $7F."* — the same code with `bcc` in place of `bmi` and no `clc`. e1will: *"there are probably a couple more places in my kernel where a BCC would be more appropriate than a BMI."* (AtariAge `topic/145489`; Cited only, not verified). Falling through `bcc` is itself the proof that C is set; `bmi` gives it only while both values stay within `$00`-`$7F` (the `BPL`/`BMI` row below) — our reading, Not verified. The same move with a constant is in `docs/techniques/kernel-micro-idioms.md` ("Fold the borrow into the constant"). ★**A known carry can decide which instructions a routine may use, and its entry carry can go unwritten.** Manuel Polik, 2002, positioned two players in one line: the Battlezone `SBC #$0F` / `BCS` divide loop, then a 16-byte table lookup through `Y` for `HMP1`, then `SBC #$0F` again for `HMP0` — *"Tada: carry is clear, so subtract $0F instead of $10"*. Thomas Jentzsch offered `EOR #$0F` / `SBC #$06` and four `ASL`s in place of the table, to keep `Y` free: *"The only problem with that code is, that the carry might be set after the last ASL. So it will not work for Manuel, but might be useful elsewhere."* Manuel: *"I just desperately needed the cycles gained from using the table and the reliable carry state."* Eric Ball, after trying the routine: *"There's an implied CLC or equivalent before the first STA WSYNC."* No reply in the thread takes that up 〔`200211/msg00165`, `200211/msg00167`, `200211/msg00168`, `200211/msg00201`〕; Cited only, not verified. On our reading the entry carry matters because the loop's first `SBC` uses whatever carry the caller left — Not verified. | manual | 157598, 75335, 63853, 63389; AtariAge `topic/145489` (e1will, batari); stella-list `200211/msg00165`-`00201` (Manuel Polik, Thomas Jentzsch, Eric Ball) |
| **`SED` makes `ADC` and `SBC` decimal, not `INC` or `DEC`** | Erik Mooney, 1997, offered a clock counted down with `dec FRAMES` / `bne` and kept in BCD: *"Simply put a SED at the beginning and a CLD at the end of the routine I posted"*. Piero Cavina: *"So you say that DECs would work in BCD afer a SED? You may be right, but I admit I have never checked this."* Mooney, the next day: *"er, sorry... it doesn't work that way :)"* and *"In decimal mode, DEC still decrements $10 to $0F. You could load the value into A and do a SBC #1, though, and it will correctly change the $10 to $09."* 〔stella-list `199710/msg00029`, `msg00046`, `msg00047`〕 Cited only, not verified on hardware. This engine agrees: `INC` and `DEC` in `cpu.go` add `1` or `$ff` without looking at `DecimalMode`, which only `ADC`, `SBC` and the undocumented `ARR`, `ISC` and `RRA` read — engine read, Not verified. | Step a BCD counter with `lda` / `sec` / `sbc #1` / `sta` (or `clc` / `adc #1`) under `SED`, not with `dec` / `inc` | Nothing here flags `INC`/`DEC` on a BCD variable | stella-list `199710/msg00029`, `msg00046`, `msg00047` (Erik Mooney, Piero Cavina) |
| **`and #MAX-1` works as "modulo `MAX`" only while `MAX` is a power of two, and breaks the day the constant grows** | Andrew Davie, 2003, reading Dennis Debro's Climber 5 source, of `ldy level` / `dey` / `tya` / `and #MAX_LEVEL-1` / `tay`: *"That ONLY works because MAX_LEVEL currently happens to be a power of 2. If you added one more level, the whole thing would fall over."* 〔stella-list `200304/msg00192`〕 The code is right as it ships; the fault waits in the constant (Cited only, not verified). Where a mask stands in for a modulo, write the power-of-two assumption next to the constant, or bound the index with a compare instead — derived, Not verified. | Nothing here flags it. | stella-list `200304/msg00192` (Andrew Davie) |
| **On a VCS the B bit of a pushed status byte cannot tell `BRK` from IRQ, because there is no IRQ** | Eckhard Stolberg, 2005: *"The B flag is wired directly to the interrupt input pin on the 6502. In the 6507 this pin is internally set high, so on the VCS the B flag should always be set"*, and *"None of the instructions in the 6502 affects the B flag. It's hardwired to the interrupt input pin, so you can't change the bit yourself."* Shown 6502.org's tutorial — *"The sole purpose of this flag is to distinguish a BRK from a IRQ"* — he called his account *"the theory I think is most plausible"*, allowed that *"the tutorial is correct and the BRK/IRQ mask out the B flag before pushing it to the stack"* might be, and named the test, *"connect the IRQ line on a 6502 to ground and check what value a PHP would push to the stack"*, which nobody in the thread ran 〔stella-list `200507/msg00172`, `msg00180`〕. Cited only, not verified. On either reading a VCS enters its `$FFFE` handler only through `BRK` — `missiles-bullets.md`: *"the 2600 has no path by which an IRQ can reach the 6507"* — so testing the bit there decides nothing — derived, Not verified. This engine holds B set: reset, `PLP` and `RTI` load the status through `Status.Load`, which always sets it, `BRK` sets it, and nothing clears it — the handling Stolberg agreed was *"probably the best way to handle it in a VCS emulator"* 〔`200507/msg00188`〕 (engine read; each of the 25 status checks in `Gopher2600/hardware/cpu/cpu_test.go` — `rg -c 'EquateRegisters\(t, mc\.Status,'` — expects `B` set, including after the test clears it by hand before a `PLP`). | Do not branch on bit 4 of a pushed status byte; on a VCS every entry to the `BRK` vector is a `BRK` | Nothing here flags it, and the engine cannot show a difference | stella-list `200507/msg00172`, `msg00180`, `msg00188` (Eckhard Stolberg); `Gopher2600/hardware/cpu/registers/status.go`, `Gopher2600/hardware/cpu/cpu.go` |
| **Depending on a power-up value, not just failing to initialise one** | The rows below say *initialise everything*, and `design-principles.md` gives the eight-byte sequence that does it. Neither says **do not read a power-up value on purpose** — and someone did. stella-list `200111/msg00309`, Chad Schell talking a homebrew author out of it: *"I think **relying on the state the carry bit comes up in** is a little risky myself. You don't know how much hardware will have a problem running it, **both now and in the future**."* The author's aim was to make the game refuse to run on a particular cartridge — deliberate use of an undefined value as a copy-protection device — and the argument that stopped him was not correctness but the cost of maintaining it. **Worst possible direction**: green in every emulator, broken on somebody else's console, and the author never finds out. | Treat every register, flag and RAM byte as garbage until this program wrote it. If you want a machine to behave differently on different hardware, choose a value you can *read* (`SWCHB` switches, a cartridge signature) rather than one that is merely *undefined*. | Statically decidable from `internal/cyclebound`'s CFG — `defuse`'s `ReadBeforeWrite` already reports RAM reads no path wrote; the flag equivalent is not implemented. | stella-list `200111/msg00309`; found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2) |
| **"Frying" — players deliberately corrupt the power-up state, and it works** | The row above says do not *depend* on a power-up value. This is the same fact from the other side, and it is why that row is not theoretical. From the list, 1998: *"**'frying'… means turning your 2600 on in such a way that it doesn't boot up quite right**"* — used to reach the *"fast walkers"* in The Empire Strikes Back — *"**this is probably a method reserved for the OLD STYLE Atari 2600s, since the newer versions had slightly different switches**."* Players were corrupting RAM on purpose, getting reproducible-enough results to trade as technique, and finding that it depended on which console you owned. A 2005 thread describes the mechanism precisely enough to emulate: bits are ANDed away at pseudo-random addresses and **never set**, so power-up corruption only *clears*. | Two consequences. **For a ROM**: an uninitialised value is not merely undefined, it is something a player can reach into and change — `CLEAN_START` is protection against a person, not only against chance. **For a fixture**: a "fried" state is a legitimate thing to test against, and `save_state`/`restore_state` make one reproducible (B. Watson, 2005: *"once it produces a 'fried' effect, we can save the state of the emulator … loading that state file will produce the same fried state"*). | `rg -i '\bfry(ing)?\b'` over docs/internal/cmd/roms/scripts returns nothing; `internal/mutate` is ROM-byte fault injection, a different thing from corrupting live RAM and TIA at run time. | stella-list, 1998 (player technique) and 2005 (mechanism); found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-1 and helper-2 independently) |
| **post-reset SP / RAM / flags undefined** | uninitialised everything → `CLEAN_START` is mandatory (proven on 5 consoles). ★**Undefined is not uniformly random.** Kevin Horton, 2001, asked whether power-up RAM could seed a random-number generator as it stands: *"Even though the RAM holds junk on startup, the junk is usually "biased" heavily; i.e. every powerup, location 080h may hold 23h. The determining factor for this is the sizes of the transistors on the chip; which ever one has more current drive will "win" and stick the flipflop in the same state usually. There is no way to predict which bits will go which way, and it varies from chip to chip and manufacturer to manufacturer."* His own seeding routine XORs the power-up RAM and TIA reads into the generator *"just out of good practice just in case"* 〔stella-list `200110/msg00121`, `200110/msg00145`; Cited only, not verified〕. So a value that differs between consoles can still repeat on one, and a seed taken from power-up RAM alone may start the same way on every power-up of the same machine — derived, Not verified. The routine does it in the loop that clears memory: for X from `$00` to `$FF` it XORs the byte at `$00,X` into one of four seed bytes, writes 0 there and does `txs`, so it ends with the stack pointer at `$FF` (*"we get a "free" way to set the stack"*) and X = 0, then makes the seed nonzero if all four bytes came out zero 〔`200110/msg00121`; its `eor lda #0` is read here as `lda #0`〕. Its writes cover `$00`-`$7F` too, the TIA's write side, as `CLEAN_START`'s do in the row below — our reading of the listing, Not verified. | static (CLEAN_START) | 261488, 312005, 316071; stella-list `200110/msg00121`, `200110/msg00145` (Kevin Horton) |
| **`CLEAN_START` also writes every TIA register, strobes included** | Its loop in DASM 2.20.14.1's `macro.h` is `dex / txs / pha / bne`, pushing zeros from `$01FF` down through page 1, and `$0100`-`$017F` is the TIA's write side twice over (the engine's memory map lists `0100 -> 017f TIA` and masks a TIA write with `$003f`). So each register is written twice, which is also what clears `VDELP0`/`VDELP1`/`VDELBL` for the menu row below. z26 marked two of the pushes *"weird (12)"*; Manuel Polik: *"The Pha is strobing HMOVE at cycle 12, where the HMOVE results are considered to be *weird*"*, and of the second, *"there's just HMOVE *again*. The TIA registers are mirrored in certain ranges"* — `$016A` and `$012A` 〔stella-list `200309/msg00269`, `msg00270`, `msg00272`〕. The addresses are worked out from the macro and the memory map, not run here — Not verified. ★**A loop that clears RAM alone does not do this.** scitari, 2018: *"The problem I encountered when moving to original hardware was that I was only clearing the RAM during initialization as I had seen done in many other code examples. I had to clear the registers as well to get it to work properly."* (AtariAge `topic/275869`; Cited only, not verified — the post names neither the register nor the console or cartridge.) ★**The clear also strobes the position registers, so each object starts in the same place every time, and a program that never sets one comes to rely on it.** Chris Cracknell, 1997, had started two sprites where an emulator put them, *"the far right hand side"*, and on a console found *"The default sprite position on a 2600 is actually about 1/3 the way in from the right."* Erik Mooney: *"Actually, the 2600's default sprite position should be random, and the emulator default sprite position is wherever the author puts it. But, are you doing the standard "zero everything" loop at the beginning? When you do that, you hit the sprite positioning registers, so they'll always end up at the same place."* 〔stella-list `199709/msg00324`, `199709/msg00332`; Cited only, not verified〕 Cracknell did not say whether his program had such a loop; his own conclusion was to place each sprite where he wanted it from the start. A start position that comes from the clear is lost the day the clear is cut down to RAM alone — derived, Not verified. | Nothing here flags it; whether a strobe during start-up matters to a given ROM is not measured. `scripts/check_traps.py` asks only for a `CLD` or a `CLEAN_START`, so a ROM with `CLD` and a loop that clears RAM alone passes it (read from the script). | AtariAge `topic/275869` (scitari); stella-list `200309/msg00269`-`00272` (Rodrigo Silva, Manuel Polik); `199709/msg00324` (Chris Cracknell), `msg00332` (Erik Mooney); `Gopher2600/hardware/memory/memorymap/`; DASM 2.20.14.1 `macro.h` |
| **Zeroing the TIA again every frame undoes your own settings** | Eckhard Stolberg, 1996, on a newcomer's program whose routine *"that claims to do the overscan"* ran each frame: *"All it does is filling a couple of ports with zero. This is stupid, because this way you have to reset things like the backgroundcolour again in every frame. You don't even have to call it once, before the first frame, because writing zeros to all ports is allready done by the very first loop in your code."* So the clear that is needed is the start-up one (the `CLEAN_START` row above), and a register set once then keeps its value until written — our reading of his reasoning. Cited only, not verified | Clear once at start-up; in overscan, write only what changes | manual | stella-list `199610/msg00020` (Eckhard Stolberg) |
| **A cartridge that runs a menu first hands the game a console that is not at power-up** | Every row here about power-up state assumes the game is the first program the console runs. On a flash cartridge it is not. tokumaru, 2015: carts *"that run software prior to running the games (as is the case with the Harmony's menu) temper with the power-up state of the console … it does happen sometimes that a program doesn't work when run directly because it relied on something that was initialized by the flash cart it was tested with"*; cpuwiz, to the premise: *"If you rely on that, you have already failed."* It fails in the other direction too. A year later in the same thread a game ran in Stella *"even with the "randomize" checkboxes checked"* and, for crispy, on an FPGA 2600 and a 2600 Jr (his cartridge is not named), but not at all from two Harmony carts; it had jumped banks through the stack *"BEFORE clearing memory"* (AtariAge `topic/232824`). Thomas Jentzsch, on a startup bug in his own game: *"I had expected that you would use the PlusCart and therefore tested the ROM with it. There the error doesn't show. But on Harmony it does. It turned out to be a stupid typo mistake (using tsx instead of txs in the TIA/RAM initialization code)"* (AtariAge `topic/343591`). The TIA's registers are state too: alex_79 read a Harmony run in which player 0 was never drawn as *"VDEL is enabled (at least for player 0) by the Harmony menu software"*, with the fix *"Try clearing VDELP0/VDELP1/VDELBL before writing to the graphic registers"*; to observe the real start-up state *"you must flash the rom in single game mode"*, because *"If you load it from the menu, the latter will influence the results"*, so a seed taken from it *"might fail in all cases where the game isn't executed at the console power-on (multicarts with menu, supercharger, etc..)"* (AtariAge `topic/273214`). ★A menu can also wipe a value a game reads on purpose. The 2600/7800 test reads `$D0` and `$D1` for `$2C` and `$A9` before clearing RAM; omegamatrix: *"it will fail on a Harmony cart or Krok cart if they are in multigame mode. It works if a single image is burned."* SpiceWare, whose Stay Frosty 2 runs that test: *"My understanding is the Harmony Menu will restore $D0 and $D1 when it launches a game"*, and then, on his two cartridges, *"Original has 1.05, SF2 detects 7800 as 2600"* and *"Encore has 1.06, SF2 correctly detects 7800"* — so the answer also depends on the cartridge's firmware (AtariAge `topic/194119`). Cited only, not verified. Here RAM starts at zero, so that test should take the 2600 branch on every run — derived, Not verified. | Set SP, every RAM byte and every TIA register the program depends on before it depends on it — SP before the first `jsr`/`rts`. Do not seed from power-up state. With a hardware result, name the cartridge, its firmware version, and whether a menu ran first. | Here no menu runs and RAM starts at zero (`RandomState`, §C), so a run in this harness is a third starting state, not a check of either. `defuse`'s `ReadBeforeWrite` finds RAM read before written; nothing here flags a TIA register or SP used before the program set it. | AtariAge `topic/232824` (tokumaru, cpuwiz, gauauu, crispy), `topic/343591` (Thomas Jentzsch), `topic/273214` (alex_79), `topic/194119` (omegamatrix, SpiceWare) |
| page-cross `+1` cycle on reads | reads (not stores) take +1 across a page; throws off cycle budget. ★**A table's SIZE can decide it, not only where it sits.** SpiceWare, on masking a sprite through a table of zeros, the sprite's `$FF`s and zeros again: *"if your use a mask large enough to cover the screen you'll end up crossing the page, which adds a +1 to the cycle count, so it would be 14 cycles instead of 13."* Budget the crossing figure when choosing how big a table read through `(ptr),Y` may be. ★**In a mid-line rewrite the extra cycle shows as a band of the picture, not as a line count.** An asymmetric playfield rewritten mid-line from four tables read with `lda PFBitmapN,y`: *"it looks like the left pf2 is being reflected until about 1/4 of the way down the map"*; splendidnut: *"If any of those PFBitmap arrays cross a page boundary, there's a single cycle penalty which will skew your cycle time where that page crossing occurs"*, and the fix he gave was `align 256` before the table; the author reported *"That worked like a charm!"* (AtariAge `topic/360395`; Cited only, not verified). ★**Which glyphs break can point at where the crossing is.** vitoco, on a score in another author's game: *"This happens when the last digit is 4 (1 bar) or 5 to 9 (3 bars). I guess it is because there is a page boundary in the middle of the digit's bitmaps (the first one starts at $FEE9 and finishes at ~$FF1A)."* It is his guess, and the distilled notes do not record it being confirmed (AtariAge `topic/312285`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). On our reading the glyphs that break are the ones read across the boundary, so the set of bad digits brackets its address — Not verified. ★**A DiStella listing's cycle counts leave the `+1` out.** DiStella's README on its `-s` option: *"It only includes the basic cycle count, and does not adjust for page boundries (YET)."* Bob Colbert, its author, 1998, advising cycle counting by assembling with DASM and disassembling with the cycle flag on: *"I don't know if I added the page-boundry cycle to Distella, but it would be an easy addition and would be all anyone needs."* 〔stella-list `199806/msg00110`〕 `cmd/dissect` calls DiStella with `-a` only (read from `cmd/dissect/main.go`, not run), so it does not take its cycle counts from this. The README line is the tool's own statement; nothing here ran `-s` (Cited only, not verified). ★**The `+1` was also offered as a way to buy one cycle.** Andrew Davie, 2001, needed to waste exactly one cycle between a `bcs` and `lda (S0),y`; Chris Wilkson: *"Can you align the code so the the LDA instruction crosses a page boundary? That'll give you an extra cycle."* Davie: *"That's the most brilliant suggestion :)"*, but *"there's a branch back to the top of the code, so that will take an additional cycle, too, and bugger up things. But utilising the extra cycle when branching over a page boundary is certainly something worth looking at."* 〔stella-list `200102/msg00154`, `200102/msg00159`〕 Nobody in the thread reports trying it. By the rules above, the `+1` of `lda (S0),y` comes from where `S0`+Y lands, not from where the instruction sits, so the cycle is bought with the data's placement and moves when the data or the code around it moves — our reading, Not verified. The thread's other answer moves the write instead: Kurt Woloch, *"If you replace that with sta.w GRP0 (if that works), I think you would end up with the thing taking up one more cycle and thus GRP0 written one cycle later."* 〔`200102/msg00156`〕; that a write lands when the store completes is in §A's `GRP`/`COLUP` row (Cited only, not verified). | static: `prove_line_budget` charges the `+1` on an `abs,X`/`abs,Y` read unless the proven index range keeps every target in the base's page, or the base is page-aligned (`$NN00`), which is charged nothing for any index, unknown included (`pagePenalty` in `internal/cyclebound/absint.go`; `TestPageAlignedBaseIsNotChargedForACrossing`); any other unknown index, or a `(zp),Y` read, stays at the conservative `+1` (`CHANGELOG.md` S3; `internal/cyclebound/pagecross_test.go`). **No `CHECKPAGE` macro exists in this tree** (the name's only occurrence was this cell), **and no `err` statement exists in its `.asm`** (`rg -n -i -g '*.asm' '^\s+err\b' harness roms` → 0 on 2026-10-05; other forms of assembly-time check were not searched); `align 256` before the table is the fix the thread gave | 132913, 125755, 147642, 335346, 360395, 312285; stella-list `199806/msg00110` (Bob Colbert), DiStella `README.txt`; `200102/msg00154` (Chris Wilkson), `msg00156` (Kurt Woloch), `msg00159` (Andrew Davie) |
| **An indexed read that crosses a page first reads an address the program never named** | The `+1` in the row above is a whole bus cycle, and the CPU spends it reading. Bradford Mott, 1997, working out the Supercharger, with X = `$FF` and `cmp $f0ff,x`: *"The thing to notice here is that a read to location $f0fe occurs on cycle 4 before the read to $f1fe at cycle 5. This "false" read doesn't affect most software but when your hardware has side effects it does :-) So the value to be poked is $fe."* And: *"This same sort of thing happens for both $nnnn,Y and ($nn),Y."* 〔`199705/msg00027`〕 The extra address is the base's high byte with the indexed low byte. The next day he planned a slower CPU core for Stella that would *"issue all of the "false" reads generated by the various addressing modes"* 〔`199705/msg00029`〕 — so the emulator of the day did not. | Where an indexed read can reach a page that holds a hotspot, check the unfixed address as well as the intended one. | Engine read, not measured: Gopher2600's `hardware/cpu/cpu.go` issues it — `read8Bit((indirectAddress&0xff00)\|(address&0x00ff), true)` for absolute,X/Y on a page cross and for every indexed store or read-modify-write, and the same shape for `(zp),Y` — through the same `mem.Read` as any other access, so a mapper that acts on reads sees it here too. No litmus observes the bus on that cycle. | stella-list `199705/msg00027`, `199705/msg00029` (Bradford Mott); `Gopher2600/hardware/cpu/cpu.go` |
| **An indexed read that crosses a page loads from the next page: the index does not wrap** | cisano, 2022, reading a 250-entry table with `lda DATA,y`, saw the read position go wrong at `$F100` and suspected the page. Thomas Jentzsch and dave-c answered that `lda DATA,y` reads correctly across a page boundary; dave-c put the table across one with `ORG $f2ff` and showed it running in DASM and Stella, and the shift cisano saw was another bug. glurk added the cost, the `+1` two rows above (AtariAge `topic/339019`, held here only as distilled notes, so the wording is not checked; Cited only, not verified). The row above gives the bus order: the uncorrected address is read first, the corrected one second, and the second is the read that loads — derived from Bradford Mott's account, Not verified. | Nothing to fix for the value; budget the crossing's cycle as two rows above says. | What the tests here measure about a crossing is its cost: `TestPageCrossPenaltyRules` (`internal/cpudiff`) counts cycles, and `litmus_pagecross`'s `FarTbl` at `$F0F8` reaching `$F1C0` (`internal/cyclebound/pagecross_test.go`) is built on the value being right without checking it. | AtariAge `topic/339019` (cisano, Thomas Jentzsch, dave-c, glurk) |
| **A taken branch onto another page costs a fourth cycle, and DASM can be made to say so** | Kirk Israel, 2004: a kernel that *"seemed pretty solid before"* broke after changes made only in VBLANK, and a certain number of `SLEEP` cycles there *"seemed to make the issue go away"*. Dennis Debro: *"Counting the cycles at the maximum cycle count shows the last branch happening at cycle 73. The loop branch crosses a page boundary so that's 4 cycles vs. 3. Doing this produces an extra scan when all your objects are on the same scan line."* The author had once written a perl script to find cross-page jumps and *"stopped using it"*. B. Watson: *"dasm can already do this for you"* — `align 256` before the kernel, a label after it, and `if (>kernel != >end_kernel)` / `echo "WARNING: Kernel crosses a page boundary!"` / `endif`. The line prints *"once per pass"*, and his sample run still ends `Complete.`: it warns and does not stop the build. He sketched a macro per branch (`bne {1}`, then `if (>* != >{1})`) but had *"never done this"* 〔stella-list `200402/msg00031`, `msg00046`, `msg00047`, `msg00049`〕. SpiceWare, 2016, to gauauu, who wished DASM *"should even know if it's crossing a page boundary"*: *"macro.h in Collect has a number of macros which take care of that. Search for SAME PAGE BRANCH CHECK as well as DIFFERENT PAGE BRANCH CHECK"*; gauauu: *"That's slick! But I want it integrated with my editor/IDE."* (AtariAge `topic/256433`; Collect's `macro.h` is not read here). The `align`/`if`/`echo` check is reported to print its line for a block that crosses and nothing for one that fits under DASM 2.20.14.1, run outside this repository in the review (2026-09-17) — Not verified here. ★**A crossing in a wait loop was blamed for a frame that changed length.** quohog, 2020, had a game that *"looks solid in Stella, and on my heavy sixer, vader, and jr"*, but on a friend's light sixer *"it vibrates up and down, gaining and losing a scanline about every frame"*. His later account, a guess in his own words: *"what I think happened is I added more code so that the tight loop in my keyboard reading code moved off a page boundary. I loop to wait the 400 microseconds to required to read the keyboards and every once in a while I push that loop onto a page boundary and everything goes to heck."* The thread does not say why one console showed it and the others did not, and the rare white flash and roll in the same game was a separate bug, which he located with Stella's `breakif _scan>#262`. SpiceWare answered with branch macros from his own `macro.h`, credited to supercat — `sbne`, `sbpl` and the rest, each a branch followed by `if (* ^ {1}) & $FF00` / `echo "PAGE CROSSING","WARNING ",{1}," at ",*` / `err` / `endif` — which he thought were *"probably never in the official macro.h file"* (AtariAge `topic/309619`; Cited only, not verified). | Assert the page at assembly: the `if`/`echo` pair above to warn, or `ERR` as in the `CHECK_PAGE` macro of the bank-move row (§C) to stop. | `cyclebound` charges the cycle (`pen = 2 // taken branch crossing a page costs +2 total over base` in `internal/cyclebound/cyclebound.go`; `pagesensitive_test.go` requires all eight relative branches to be page-sensitive), so `prove_line_budget` counts it. Nothing makes the assembly say so: the `.asm`/`.inc`/`.h` files outside the vendored engines have 15 `align` lines and no `echo` or `if` line (counted 2026-10-01) | stella-list `200402/msg00031`, `msg00046`, `msg00047`, `msg00049` (Kirk Israel, Dennis Debro, B. Watson); AtariAge `topic/256433` (SpiceWare, gauauu); `topic/309619` (quohog, SpiceWare) |
| **`ECHO` prints once per pass, and a symbol defined after it makes it print once** | primordial-ooze, 2012, wanted the free ROM space at every build; `echo "bytes free:" *-$F800` *"only produces garbage output"*. omegamatrix: `ECHO ([$FF00-*]d), "bytes free"` followed by `ORG $FF00` *"will print the number of bytes left (in decimal). DASM will print it twice though"*, and the cure is to wrap it in `IF ECHO_1` / `ENDIF` with `ECHO_1 = 1` after it: *"the first pass DASM doesn't know if the IF statement is true or false, so it doesn't execute it. On the second pass the IF statement is resolved, and DASM executes it, so it only prints once, not twice."* The address in the `ECHO` has to match the `ORG` after it, and each `ECHO` needs a symbol of its own (AtariAge `topic/203783`; Cited only, not verified). The row above has the same doubling in B. Watson's page check, *"once per pass"*. ★**The padding of each `ALIGN` can be summed in the source.** Asked in a 2005 thread how to know how big a binary really is *"since dasm pads it out to 4k"*, Manuel Rotschkar removed every `ORG` but the first and the last and read the binary in a hex editor; Erik Mooney put `ECHO *` before the final `ORG`; Thomas Jentzsch: *"Or you can use a variable and sum up all free spaces: E.g. put FREE SET FREE + 256 - (. % 256) before each ALIGN 256"*; Dennis Debro: *"This is what the BOUNDARY macro does as long as you stay within 256 bytes."* 〔`200501/msg00010`, `200501/msg00011`, `200501/msg00012`, `200501/msg00013`〕 (Cited only, not verified). By the arithmetic, the line has to come before the `ALIGN`: placed after it, `. % 256` is 0 and the line adds 256 each time, and even before it, an `ALIGN` reached exactly on a page boundary pads nothing while the line adds 256 — Not verified, not assembled here. | To report free space, `ECHO` the distance to the next `ORG`, inside an `IF` on a symbol defined after the `ECHO` | Nothing here prints it: the row above counted no `echo` line in the `.asm`/`.inc`/`.h` files outside the vendored engines | AtariAge `topic/203783` (primordial-ooze, omegamatrix); stella-list `200501/msg00010`-`00013` (Manuel Rotschkar, Erik Mooney, Thomas Jentzsch, Dennis Debro) |
| unstable illegal opcodes | `LAX/SAX/SBX/DCP` are HW-stable **on original NMOS silicon, which is not the same as "on every machine that plays 2600 cartridges"** — ★`SBX` and `ARR` are reported as not working on the **Flashback 2**, a chip-level reimplementation rather than a 6507 (AtariAge `113732-clean-assembly`, found by the mailing-list distillation 2026-09-05). This row already scopes `ASR` to *late Taiwanese Atari Jr units*; the same scoping applies here and was missing. ★★Unmeasured by us: we have no Flashback 2, and the engine models a 6507, so this is recorded as the source's claim. A second thread agrees on `SBX`: on `SAX` (it stores A AND X and changes no register or flag), batari is recorded as finding `SAX` stable on hardware and on the Flashback 2, and `SBX` as the only one of the illegal opcodes in his own game that had to be removed for the Flashback 2 (AtariAge `topic/139505`, 2009, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified); **`LXA/XAA` are not** (chip/temp dependent); **`ASR`/`ALR` is NOT stable** — fails on late Taiwanese Atari Jr units (official hardware), Thunderground score corruption, confirmed independently by omegamatrix on real hardware | static (opcode allowlist) | 168616 (both the stable list AND the ASR caveat), 294471 §32, 132496, 139505 |
| **One illegal opcode, two names: a search for one misses the other** | batari, on an `SBX` trick: *"Actually, SBX is an illegal opcode that stores to X the result of (A&X)-Immediate (and it ignores the carry!)"*; Manuel (cybergoth): *"Oh... I thought that opcode was called AXS"*; batari: *"I've noticed that different documents use different mnemonics for the illegals, so maybe SBX=AXS. Though SBX works in dasm, maybe AXS does too"* (AtariAge `topic/71120`; Cited only, not verified). The engine's `definitions.json` calls opcode `$CB` `sbx` and nothing else. `scripts/check_traps.py` met the same split in its unstable-opcode rule — *"Five spellings, three instructions, and only three of the five assemble."* — and lists both `sbx` and `axs` in its mnemonic pattern. Whether DASM accepts `axs` is Not verified. | Search for every spelling of an illegal opcode, and name its opcode byte when writing about it. | `rg -i 'sbx\|axs'`, not one name. | AtariAge `topic/71120` (batari, cybergoth) |
| **Reading an illegal opcode from its byte: `x7` is `x6` then `x5`, at `x6`'s cost — except `$87` and `$97`** | Jim Nitchals, 1997, answering whether the 6507 has the 6502's undocumented instructions: *"The 6507 is just a 6502 in cost-reduced packaging. The undocumented instructions will work the same. Sure, they'd screw up an emulator and possibly redesigned 2600's like the TV-Boy, as later 6502 designs made use of the opcode space for other purposes."* — the Flashback 2 in the `unstable illegal opcodes` row is, on our reading, a later case of that caveat — and a rule of thumb: *"x7 does x6 followed by x5."* Read against the engine's `definitions.json` (read from the source): `$07` `slo` beside `$06` `asl` and `$05` `ora`, and likewise `$27` `rla`, `$47` `sre`, `$67` `rra`, `$C7` `dcp`, `$E7` `isc` and `$A7` `lax`, each with its `x6` neighbour's cycle count (5 for the read-modify-write ones, 3 for `lax`), and the addressing follows `x6` too (`$B7` is zero page,Y like `$B6`). `$87` and `$97` are `sax` (zero page and zero page,Y), which stores A AND X — not `STX` then `STA` (its meaning is the usual description, Not verified here). His companion rule for `x3` is not examined here. Cited only, not verified | Use the rule to read bytes, then confirm the opcode in the engine's table | manual | stella-list `199709/msg00138` (Jim Nitchals) |
| **`LAX 0` is `LAX CXM0P`, and adding the `#` gives `LXA`, called unstable except, by one report, at `#0`** | Omegamatrix, 2014, on a 3D engine's start-up: *"I did notice in your start-up you are doing LAX 0. This translates to LAX CXM0P. There are a lot of undefined bits in there. It's better just to go with LDX #0, and TXA. There is another way to save that byte (LXA #0), but it LXA unstable."* (his wording). enthusi (*ist* is his spelling): *"LXA/LAX ist 'stable' (from the result's point of view) when (and only then afaik) the argument is #00."* (AtariAge `topic/222981`; Cited only, not verified). It is landing zone (a) of the forgotten-`#` row — `$00` is a TIA read register, so no write-only rule fires — on an opcode whose immediate form is a trap of its own. | `ldx #0` / `txa` | Measured 2026-10-01: `scripts/check_traps.py` on a scratch file flags `lax #0` (the `LAX #imm` rule) and `lxa #0` (unstable opcode) and says nothing about `lax 0` | AtariAge `topic/222981` (Omegamatrix, enthusi) |
| **signed subtraction of two positions that can be >127 apart** | `dx = A − B` as a signed byte **overflows** when \|A−B\|>127 (two sprites on a 160-px field): −132 wraps to +124, mis-read as *positive* → a sign/direction test aims the wrong way (and a ±1-frame drift as the objects close within the signed range). The `V` flag catches it but is rarely tested. Sibling of the bit7-clamp traps in §A, but here it's the **difference of two wide-range values**, not one | take the **sign from an unsigned `CMP`** (is A≥B?) and the **magnitude from larger−minus−smaller** (always ≤255); never trust bit7 of a raw signed difference | poke the two coords >127 apart, read the derived sign/direction | in-house: Combat enemy-AI aim, `dx=TankX0−TankX1` 2026-07-19 |
| **`AND #$7F` is not an absolute value** | Piero Cavina, 1997, asked for something faster than `BPL plus` / `EOR #$FF` / `CLC` / `ADC #1`. Chris Wilkson offered `AND #%01111111` *"; this should mask off the sign bit"*. Jim Nitchals: *"Won't work for absolute values. $FD is -3, but $7D is 125 when masked."* Wilkson withdrew it: *"There was supposed to be a 6809 complement instuction stuck in there."* In two's complement, clearing bit 7 of a negative `v` gives `128 + v` (−1 → 127, −3 → 125, −128 → 0) — arithmetic, Not verified. Cited only, not verified | Cavina's own `EOR #$FF` / `CLC` / `ADC #1` after the `BPL`, or Nitchals' table: `TAX` / `LDA ABSVALUE,X`, *"for a total of 6 cycles, at a cost of 256 bytes of ROM (less if the value is known to be smaller than +/- 128)"*. −128 has no 8-bit positive: the `EOR`/`ADC` form returns `$80` — arithmetic, Not verified | not static: whether a byte is signed is not in the code | stella-list `199703/msg00311` (Piero Cavina), `199703/msg00316`, `199703/msg00319` (Chris Wilkson), `199703/msg00317` (Jim Nitchals) |

| **Opposing joystick directions cannot be tested here at all** | Real `SWCHA` is four independent switch lines, and a **dance pad, a homebrew controller or a worn stick can close up and down together** — nothing in the RIOT prevents it. Gopher2600's peripherals prevent it, deliberately, and **it is not specific to the stick**: `gamepad.go` carries the same four `cancel()` calls with the same comment, and `keypad.go`/`paddle.go` have no directions to cancel — so **every direction-carrying peripheral the engine has** enforces the exclusivity. `Gopher2600/hardware/peripherals/controllers/stick.go` — *"we don't want to allow impossible positions for the stick. for example, holding left and right at the same time is impossible. the cancel function cancels any existing position that is in opposition to the new axis"*. Measured 2026-09-04: `SetInput(up)` then `SetInput(down)` reads `$DF` (down only, up cancelled); all four pressed reads `$5F` (down+right). **`Poke($0280, …)` does not get around it** — the peripheral rewrites SWCHA on the next update, so a poked `$CF` reads back as `$FF`. There is no `UpDown` axis constant, so `DataStickSet` cannot express it either. | Do not test opposing-direction handling through `SetInput`; it silently gives you a *different* input than the one you asked for, and the ROM sees a legal single direction. Write the branch defensively (treat `up&&down` as a real case) and note in the scenario that it is unverified here. **This is the dangerous direction of divergence**: the tool is *narrower* than the hardware, so a ROM that mishandles the state passes and fails on someone's pad. | Press two opposing directions and read `$0280`: if the result has only one direction bit clear, the cancel ran. Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-1) from `discotech` (2003), a homemade dance pad. | `Gopher2600/hardware/peripherals/controllers/stick.go` (the engine's own comment); stella-list `discotech` thread |
| **`SWCHA` is active-low, so "a direction is pressed" is `< $F0`, not `> 0`** | Player 0's stick is the high nibble and a pressed direction reads 0 (`fundamentals-audit.md`, verified by `litmus_input`), so with nothing pressed the byte is `$F0` or more whatever player 1 is doing, and `SWCHA > 0` is true nearly always. The author who tried it: *"causes it to animate 24/7. I only want it to animate when it's moving"*; the correction was to compare against 240. The engine readings in the row above agree (`$DF` down only, `$5F` with all four pressed). | Compare unsigned: `lda SWCHA / cmp #$F0 / bcc pressed` — not `bmi`/`bpl`, which read bit 7 alone (P0 right). | Hold nothing and read `$0280`: the high nibble must be `$F`. | AtariAge `topic/277720` (ioi_xd's report, bogax's correction) |
| **A counter tested with `=` is skipped by any step larger than 1** | Two paths that each add 1 to the same counter add 2 in one frame — a diagonal is the common case, running both the up branch and the left branch. A counter checked with `= 10`, `= 20` … and reset with `= 40 → 0` then never lands on a test value once its parity is off: the animation freezes while the diagonal is held, and the counter runs through 255 instead of resetting at 40. Report: *"Whenever I move diagonally, or press two joystick buttons at once, there's a chance that the moving animation just freezes for a couple of seconds"*; diagnosis (Kiwi): *"there's 2 branches open up that increase the f variable by 2"*. In assembly the same shape is a counter tested with `cmp #n / beq`. ★**Tested with `=` and never stopped, a counter is not skipped but hit again: a byte wraps after 256 steps.** tidusrenegade, 2018, reset an enemy to the right edge when a counter `l`, *"just the loop iteration so starts the enemy sprite moving on first iteration"*, equalled 1, and found that *"sometimes it only gets 2/3s of the way there and then restarts"*. bogax: *"When l gets (up) to 1 player1x gets reset"*; the author: *"Is there a maximum value for variables at which point they loop back to zero? That would explain it"*, then *"Yep that was it, l was looping."* (AtariAge `topic/277463`; Cited only, not verified — the attached code was not read). | Step the counter once per frame however many inputs fired, test ranges (`cmp #n / bcs`) rather than points, and reset with `>=`. A counter that gates a one-time event has to stop, or the event comes back every 256 steps. | Hold a diagonal from an odd counter value and trace the counter with `read_ram_trace`. The engine cancels only OPPOSING directions (row above), so this one is reproducible here — Not verified with a fixture. | AtariAge `topic/277720`, `topic/277463` (tidusrenegade, bogax) |
| **Stella blocks opposing directions too, and a driving controller needs them** | The row "Opposing joystick directions cannot be tested here at all" says this engine cancels up+down. Stella can do the same. quohog, 2020, with a driving controller on a 2600-daptor II, printed `SWCHA` from his own ROM: turning right gave *"the sequence 01-10-11 repeated"* in Stella, and on a Harmony on a console *"00-10-11-01 which is right and good!"*; Indy 500 worked in the same Stella. RevEng: *"Stella believes you're using a joystick, so it's blocking impossible joystick directions. 00=up+down"*, and *"Indy500 is set for driving controls, because stella knows the rom."* The author: *"Yes, the zero was missing."* The setting was never pinned down: the Stella bundled with Atari Dev Studio gave the short sequence, and pointing the editor at his own Stella install gave *"00-10-11-01"*; RevEng's `-joyallow4 1` was *"just a guess"*, and no versions were given (AtariAge `topic/308090`; Cited only, not verified). So agreeing with Stella does not cover a path that only an "impossible" stick state reaches, such as the up+down slot of Combat's `CTRLTBL` (`docs/casebook.md`) — derived, Not verified. | Expect all four two-bit states from a driving controller, `00` included, and check such a ROM on a console or in an emulator set for that controller. | `SetInput` cannot produce up+down here either (the row named above), and the engine has no driving controller: `hardware/peripherals/controllers/` holds stick, gamepad, paddle and keypad | AtariAge `topic/308090` (quohog, RevEng, mksmith) |
| **`VBLANK` D7 is also the QuadTari's port select** | The bit that dumps the paddle capacitors has a second meaning when a QuadTari adaptor is plugged in. vitoco, quoting the adaptor's documentation (the thread is held here only as distilled notes, so the wording is not checked against the post): *"you can select which input joystick to use (1&2 or 3&4) by setting or clearing BIT 7 of VBLANK (ie register $01. By setting bit 7, it drags INPT0-3 to ground)"* and *"After changing bit 7, the QuadTari will need some time to stabilize. It is safest to wait at least one scanline (76 cycles) before reading the new joystick ports."* So a `VBLANK` write with D7 set — `docs/techniques/paddle.md` uses `VBLANK = $82` — also chooses which pair of sticks the next read sees. | Decide D7 on purpose in every `VBLANK` write when a QuadTari may be attached, and leave a scanline between changing it and reading the ports. | Nothing here models a QuadTari — Cited only, not verified. | AtariAge `topic/312285` (vitoco) |
| **A colour/B&W switch read as a position is a button on a 7800, and it cannot be held** | Bob Colbert, 1997, to Piero Cavina, whose demo chose NTSC or PAL by the colour/B&W switch: *"I decided not to use the color/bw switch foro Pal/NTSC because 7800's do not have one!"* (his spelling), *"While the pause switch does work like the color/bw switch, there is no way to latch it on, so you will not be able to lock NTSC if you go that route."* and *"I had to settle for a difficulty switch on Okie Dokie."* Chris Wilkson proposed *"a software latch"*: a RAM bit, set when the switch is seen in the PAL position and not read again after that.
