Cavina: *"I modified my program to work in that way. The switch is checked at startup and when Reset is pressed, so one with a PAL 7800 must load the program with Pause pushed, or press Pause and Reset together to start a new game in PAL."* 〔stella-list `199708/msg00116`, `msg00118`, `msg00121`〕 Cited only, not verified — the 7800 is not modelled here. `design-principles.md` has the switch as a TV-standard option and, from Andrew Davie, the 7800's switch as momentary, with the advice to read changes rather than position. | If the ROM may run on a 7800, read a mode switch only at power-up and Reset into a RAM bit, or use a difficulty switch | Not reachable here: the engine has no 7800 | stella-list `199708/msg00116`, `msg00118`, `msg00121` (Bob Colbert, Chris Wilkson, Piero Cavina) |

| **The 400 µs port-direction settling time is not modelled** | The Programmer's Guide requires **400 µs between writing SWACNT/SWBCNT and reading the port** — 477 cycles at 1.19318 MHz, **6.3 scanlines**, which is a whole kernel's worth of budget. Gopher2600 does not implement it and says so in `hardware/peripherals/controllers/keypad.go`: *"We're not emulating this here … I'm not sure what's supposed to happen if the 400ms is not adhered to. **!!TODO: Consider adding 400ms delay for SWACNT settings to take effect.**"* Measured 2026-09-04 (`litmus_swacnt` band 5): reading 480 cycles after the write gives the same byte as reading 4 cycles after it. **This is the opposite direction from the joystick trap above** — there the tool is *narrower* than the hardware, here it is *looser*: a ROM that skips the settling time passes and may not on a console. | If you drive a port, wait 400 µs before reading it, and put the wait in the kernel's budget — it is 6.3 lines, not a rounding error. **The figure is a rule of thumb, not a specification.** The oldest post on it (`200011`, a year before the two below) does the arithmetic — *"1194720 cycles per second times 0.0004 seconds = 477.888 cycles = 6.288 scanlines?"* — and immediately hedges it: *"**I assume 400 microseconds is an upper bound as a general rule of thumb**."* Nobody in the thread had measured it; the question it opens with is *"has anyone written a small program to **verify this**?"* — which is the question `litmus_swacnt` answers 26 years later, and answers only for this engine. Budget the full 6.3 lines until someone measures the real settling time on hardware. **The list answers the engine's TODO**: Chad Schell, running serial off the port at 38.4 kbps (`200111/msg00194`), *"If you only read the port, and thus don't change it's configuration, the 400 uS delay does not apply"* — so the constraint binds **when you change the direction**, not on every read. | Grep the ROM for a `SWACNT`/`SWBCNT` write followed by a port read within ~477 cycles. Nothing here detects it automatically; both ROMs in this repository that write a DDR are litmus fixtures that violate it deliberately. | Stella Programmer's Guide, quoted in the engine's own source; **stella-list `200011` (`more-keyboard-nonsense`) is the earliest — it states the arithmetic and calls 400 µs an upper bound**, and separately settles a wrong theory in passing: the delay is *not* switch debounce, because *"what if the user presses the button a short while **after** you do the output?"*, and *"the better way to debounce is simply to read slowly … which is a different thing than waiting between the reads and writes"* — the same separation Chad Schell arrives at a year later; then `200111/msg00192` and `200111/msg00194`. Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-1 for the 2001 posts, helper-2 for the 2000 one) |
| **A port bit driven as an output reaches hardware this engine has no model of — three reports** | All three are held here only as distilled notes. ★**Current.** flickertail, 2020, putting a rumble motor on port A: SWACNT is port A's direction register (1 = output), and the thread's hardware point is that the RIOT's I/O pins are meant for logic and supply almost no current, so a motor needs a drive stage; the parts the thread names are a high-side switch (MIC2091) and a stabilising capacitor (AtariAge `topic/309065`). ★**A closed switch.** In a thread that works out how to use the free `SWCHB` bits as RAM (`lda #$34` / `sta SWBCNT`), groovybee: *"Personally I wouldn't recommend making any bit that is connected to an external switch into an output. Lets say you make a difficulty switch port bit into an output. If that difficulty switch is in the B position it will connect the corresponding RIOT pin directly to ground (for as long as its in the B position). If your code sets the port data register bit to a '1' then that pin on RIOT will try and drive a logic high straight into the ground plane (GND) because the switch is a permanent short circuit. This isn't good for RIOT and is best avoided."* svolli, in the same thread, on which port can hold a value: *"this only works on port B, while port A (SWCHA) may be affected by the controllers even when the pins are set to output"* (AtariAge `topic/197100`). The notes do not settle which `SWCHB` pins are wired to switches beyond the difficulty switches. ★**A keypad scan leaves the port low.** danjovic, 2022, who had used CurtisP's 2009 `keyread.asm`: *"it was necessary to modify the code to RETURN ALL THE SWCHA BITS TO 1 AFTER READING THE LAST ROW, otherwise the right joystick kept stuck to the right position"* (AtariAge `topic/332201`). This repository measures what a driven port reads back (`litmus_swacnt`, `docs/fundamentals-audit.md`), not an electrical load, and has no keypad litmus (`docs/techniques/input-budget.md`); whether any check here would flag an output bit wired to a switch, or a scan that does not restore the port, is not tested — derived, Not verified. The three reports are Cited only, not verified. | Before making a port bit an output, find what is wired to it: a bit on a console switch is the case groovybee warns about, and a load such as a motor needs an external drive stage. After a keypad scan, write all ones back to `SWCHA`. | Nothing here detects it; the settling-time row above is the timing side of the same registers. | AtariAge `topic/309065` (flickertail), `topic/197100` (groovybee, svolli), `topic/332201` (danjovic) |

| **`BPL`/`BMI` after `CMP` is a SIGNED test on an unsigned compare** | `CMP` sets **carry** for the unsigned comparison; `BPL`/`BMI` read **N**, bit 7 of the difference, which flips across `$80`. `LDA #$7F / CMP #$80 / BPL bigger` takes the branch although $7F < $80. **2600 kernels compare scanline counts and screen positions**, which cross $80 routinely, so this is not a corner case here. Caught once on the list by reading, not by running — Greg Miller on someone else's code 〔`199710/msg00043`, Greg Miller, 1997-10-03 — **recovered 2026-09-06 after this row spent a day saying the citation could not be found**〕: *"is there a good reason why you're not prepping the carry bit before the SBC? … **You sure this is working with `bpl`?**"*, answered *"Ummm.... because I forgot."* ★**Over several bytes, `BCC` alone is not enough — each byte has three outcomes.** A 2015 high-score check tested the top byte with `cmp / bcc ret` and moved on to the next: *"I tried getting a score of 10050, with a high score of 1200. It thought 1200 was larger!"* In BCD the top bytes are `$01` and `$00`, so the first test fell through; the middle bytes are `$00` and `$12`, and there it exited. The working version tests equality first — *"beq checkMid ; if score byte = high score byte, the next digit needs to be examined"* — then `bcc` (lower: done) and `bcs` (higher: decided) at every byte but the last (AtariAge `topic/246860`; Cited only, not verified). ★**A published table carries the mistake.** carlsson, 2017, posting text *"copied from a tutorial"* under *"Notice the differences between unsigned and signed, and between < and <="*: *"If the N flag is 1, then A (signed) <= NUM (signed) and BMI will branch"* and *"If the N flag is 0, then A (signed) > NUM (signed) and BPL will branch"* (AtariAge `topic/271881`; Cited only, not verified). Equal operands leave a difference of 0, so N is 0 and `BPL`, not `BMI`, takes A = NUM — worked out from `CMP`'s definition, Not verified; and `CMP` does not set V, so N gives a signed order only while the difference does not overflow, the trap this row is about. ★**One way to keep the flags straight.** Thomas Jentzsch, 2023, to someone who always had to look `CMP` up: *"CMP sets the flags like SBC with carry set. That's how I remember it."* (AtariAge `topic/355941`; Cited only, not verified). As a memory aid it covers N, Z and C; `CMP` leaves V alone, as above, and compares in binary with `D` set — the last from the 6502's definition, Not verified. | Use **`BCC`/`BCS`** for unsigned comparisons and reserve `BPL`/`BMI` for genuinely signed values or for testing bit 7 of a byte you loaded. Prep carry with `SEC` before `SBC`, `CLC` before `ADC` — the same post caught both faults in one reading. | **Nothing here detects it.** `prove_line_budget` and `defuse` look at cycles and at read/write order, not at what a branch *means*; a wrong branch costs the same cycles as a right one. Read the comparison and ask which flag answers the question you asked. | stella-list 〔`199710/msg00043`〕. **The number first recorded here pointed into `199712`, past the end of a month that stops at `msg00101`, and a search over all 18,900 messages then reported nothing.** That search asked for one post carrying **both** this quote and the `#` trap below; the two are in **different messages of the same thread**, so the condition excluded the answer it was looking for. Widening it to either quote alone finds both in a minute (`grep -l 'prepping the carry' */msg*.html`). A search that measures the wrong thing returns a confident nothing, and nothing is indistinguishable from absence — found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2 for the quotes, helper-1 for the recovery); the multi-byte compare AtariAge `topic/246860`; the tutorial table `topic/271881`; the memory aid `topic/355941` (Thomas Jentzsch) |
| **A forgotten `#` turns an immediate into a zero-page read** | `AND $f0` reads RAM `$F0`; `AND #$f0` masks the accumulator. The assembler accepts both, and on the 2600 the address form usually reads a byte nothing ever wrote — so it "works" in an emulator that zeroes RAM and does something else on hardware. Same thread, the author's own reply: *"Ummm.... because I forgot. … Also realized the hours didn't work because I was doing `AND $f0` instead of `AND #$f0`. DOH!"* 〔`199710/msg00045`, crackers@, 1997-10-03〕. ★The same author, Chris Cracknell, 1998, read the B/W switch with `and %0010000` / `cmp %0010000`, found it on D5 rather than where the manual puts it, and asked whether the Stella manual was wrong. Eckhard Stolberg: *"It's your code again, but this time it's a more popular mistake. :-) You forgot to put a '#' in front of your binary numbers, therefore you are using zeropage addressing instead of imediate addressing. Also your binary numbers are one bit too short, so that you are using $10 instead of $20."* — and yet *"strangely enough the B/W switch is also detected"* on Stolberg's PAL console. The author's guess: *"whatever is at that zero page location I was reading just happens to be an 8"* 〔`199809/msg00008`, `199809/msg00011`, `199809/msg00014`〕. Two errors in one operand, a program that still worked, and a wrong conclusion about the hardware drawn from it (Cited only, not verified). A later case sat in plain sight in a kernel posted for help, 2024 — `ldy 9 ; counter to draw 8 rows of bitmap` — and nobody in the thread pointed at it (AtariAge `topic/359556`). `$09` is landing zone (a) below: the TIA answers the read as `INPT1`, so the loop count is that input plus bus residue, not 9 (Not verified: not run here). The same kernel bounded its sprite with `cmp Player0XPos, 50`. Measured with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository; 2.20.15-SNAPSHOT and `-S` agree): it assembles, with no warning, to `c5 80` — `, 50` is dropped, so the line compares A, still holding the joystick mask `#%10000000`, with `Player0XPos` itself. (bent_pin's reading in the thread, the byte at `$80 + 50 = $B2`, is not what this DASM builds.) `lda Player0XPos` then `cmp #` with a constant made the ship stop — but only at some constants: *"If I change the left side to CMP #10, My sprite jumps all over moving left and right. If it is CMP #13.. works fine."* bent_pin: *"You are only checking for equality when you should be checking for a range"* — the `bne` after it (Cited only, not verified; the counter row above is the same trap). ★**And a console that runs it is not a console that proves it.** PitKat, 2020: the author had tested on a Harmony with two consoles, then omegamatrix found in Stella's debugger *"a # is missing for ldx #3 … It is being compiled as ldx zeropage instead, and is loading CXP1FB which has a bunch of undefined bits. In developer mode of stella these bits get randomized so these errors become easy to detect"*; the author's reply: *"even running on real hardware may not find bugs on other hardware systems"* (AtariAge `topic/308669`). That landing zone is (a) as well, and this engine fills the undriven bits from the last bus byte, not at random ("Bus residue" above) — Not verified on that ROM. ★**At the definition the `#` does nothing** — see the row "A `#` in an equate is dropped without a word" below, which carries two more reports. ★**And at the stack.** ZackAttack, 2018, reading a game's start-up code: *"you're initializing the SP to a random value. (Forgot # to make ldx immediate)"* — `ldx $ff` then `txs` loads the stack pointer from RAM `$FF`, landing zone (c), and *"If it's < $80 the game will probably crash"*; the author reported fixing it in his next post (AtariAge `topic/274546`). Cited only, not verified. ★**It did not look like timing, and was hunted as timing.** Kirk Israel, 2003: *"I went crazy building a perl tool to hunt for page-crossing branches, when really my program was getting weird simply because I was forgetting the "#" in front of constants"*, and his rule of thumb: *"Geez, it's not like I don't know what timing problems look like; they're generally boring things that stretch the screen elements, not things that look like nuclear meltdown."* 〔stella-list `200309/msg00147`〕 One author's experience, not a test (Cited only, not verified). ★**It can wait for a picture change to show.** A month earlier, Kirk Israel uncommented a `STA PF0` and *"it all breaks. the guys get pinned to the top... it's almost like an enormous amount is being subtracted from P0VertSpeed or P1VertSpeed rather than just the constant FlapStrength."* His equates were `FlapStrength = #4` and `GravDelay = #6`; Dennis Debro pointed at `LDA GravDelay`, which *"will load the accumulator with the value in address $06 or CXBLPF"*, and Kirk: *"It's so strange how such a big error can be almost completely hidden for a long time, and then BAM...things go CRAZY."* 〔`200308/msg00097`, `200308/msg00098`, `200308/msg00100`〕 (Cited only, not verified). In the posted code the flap itself is `SBC FlapStrength`, which by the same rule reads `$04`, `CXM0FB`, with both missiles enabled and the ball's `ENABL` commented out — so on our reading the latch the new playfield changed is missile 0's rather than the ball's `CXBLPF`, and either way it is landing zone (a) below; Not verified, not run here. ★**It worked until an unrelated change, and the list did not agree why.** Manuel Polik, 2002, on Star Fire, after first posting the mistake as a missing `%`: *"Typed that too quick. Not the "%" I had forgotten, but the "#"."* — so the code was `AND %00000110` / `ORA %00010000`, and *"As long until I changed RESBL to RESM0, it perfectly did"* what the immediate forms would have done. Matthias Domin: it reads the contents of `$06` and `$10`. Chris Wilkson: *"I think you may be doing the AND and OR instructions with the contents of the collision registers...."*, which the `RESxx` change could alter. Thomas Jentzsch: *"Well, RESP0 is at $10, but \*reading\* shouldn't matter at all. So ANDing and ORing can't have an influence here. Maybe a different cycle count was the reason."* Wilkson again: *"No, I meant reading the collision registers for the ball and the missle."* 〔stella-list `200210/msg00209`, `msg00210`, `msg00212`, `msg00216`, `msg00217`, `msg00218`〕 The two addresses land in different zones below: `$06` is `CXBLPF`, zone (a); `$10` is in zone (b), the write side's `RESP0`, but a read there comes back as `CXM0P`, since the engine folds TIA reads to the low nibble (`maskReadTIA = 0x000f`, in the Supercharger notes near the top) — our reading. The `check_traps` rule for zone (b) parses `$` operands and register names, not `%` binary ones (`scripts/check_traps.py`, read from the source), so `ORA %00010000` would pass it. The thread ends without a test (Cited only, not verified). | Grep new code for `and|ora|eor|cmp|adc|sbc|lda|ldx|ldy` followed by a bare `$xx` where a mask or a constant was meant. | **The detector exists but only covers one of three landing zones**, which is why this row is worth having. `internal/cyclebound/defuse.go`'s **`ReadBeforeWrite`** flags a read that some path reaches with no write to that address having definitely happened first. Where a dropped `#` lands decides whether that helps: **(a) a small constant** (`AND $0F`) reads `$0F`, a TIA *read* register — a real value, never flagged; **(b) `$0E`–`$2C`** reads a write-only TIA register and returns bus residue — the `check_traps` static rule catches that one; **(c) `$80`–`$FF`** is RAM, and `ReadBeforeWrite` only fires if *nothing* wrote it. `$F0` in the 1997 example is unwritten, so it is caught — but **a binary immediate with bit 7 set is always `$80`–`$FF`**, which is where live variables are, so the most idiomatic version of this mistake is the one that slips through. Second report, 2003, from someone who called it a habit: `ORA %10000000` ≠ `ORA #%10000000`. The capability was there; nothing pointed the reader at this use of it. (This is also the concrete use for the `Writers`/`Readers` pair previously noted as unused.) | stella-list 〔`199710/msg00043`〕. **The number first recorded here pointed into `199712`, past the end of a month that stops at `msg00101`, and a search over all 18,900 messages then reported nothing.** That search asked for one post carrying **both** this quote and the `#` trap below; the two are in **different messages of the same thread**, so the condition excluded the answer it was looking for. Widening it to either quote alone finds both in a minute (`grep -l 'prepping the carry' */msg*.html`). A search that measures the wrong thing returns a confident nothing, and nothing is indistinguishable from absence — found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2 for the quotes, helper-1 for the recovery). The 1998 double error: 〔`199809/msg00008`, `199809/msg00011`, `199809/msg00014`〕. Kirk Israel's timing hunt: 〔`200309/msg00147`〕. The playfield that exposed it: 〔`200308/msg00097`, `200308/msg00098`, `200308/msg00100`〕. Star Fire: 〔`200210/msg00209`, `msg00210` (Manuel Polik), `msg00212` (Matthias Domin), `msg00216`, `msg00218` (Chris Wilkson), `msg00217` (Thomas Jentzsch)〕 |
| **`BCS` after `CMP` is "greater or equal", not "equal"** | atari2600land, 2017, telling food types apart, wrote `cpx #1` / `bcs Wrong` and `cpx #2` / `bcs Wrong`. `BCS` branches whenever X is at least the operand, so the first test is taken for type 2 as well as type 1 — worked out from `CMP`'s definition, Not verified on his ROM. kiwi's reply changed them to `beq` (*"branch on equal to 2"*), and the author: *"Thank you so much! That worked. I must remember to use beq instead of bcs, though. I thought bcs did what beq did."* The tutorial table quoted in the `BPL`/`BMI` row two rows above, from the same thread, has both lines: *"If the Z flag is 1, then A = NUM and BEQ will branch"* and *"If the C flag is 1, then A (unsigned) >= NUM (unsigned) and BCS will branch"* (AtariAge `topic/271881`; Cited only, not verified). | Test one value with `BEQ`/`BNE`; keep `BCS`/`BCC` for a range. | Nothing here detects it; as in the `BPL`/`BMI` row, a wrong branch costs the cycles of a right one. | AtariAge `topic/271881` (atari2600land, kiwi, carlsson) |
| **A wrong equate in a shared header fails the same way everywhere** | Most rows here separate the emulator from the machine. This one cannot, because the program is wrong before either runs it. Glenn Saunders, 2001: *"Whenever I assemble a program that reads SWCHA I'm getting all zeroes. This thing happens both on the emulator and the real thing."* The `VCS.H` he used had `SWCHA = $0281` and `SWACNT = $0280`, swapped 〔`200110/msg00460`, `200110/msg00461`〕; the port-B pair was wrong too 〔`msg00463`〕; and Brian Prescott, who had started from the same file, added *"there are some things missing (some reset addresses) and the AUDV1 value is incorrect"* — *"a real pain when you try to figure out why the sound isn't working right"* 〔`msg00469`〕 (Cited only, not verified). **Agreement between engines is no evidence here**: they all execute the wrong address correctly. ★**A downloaded header, and a proposed standard one, had the difficulty bits swapped.** Erik Eid, 2002, adding the difficulty switches to his Euchre game, found `P0_Diff = $80` and `P1_Diff = $40` in the `vcs.h` he had downloaded and in Glenn Saunders's proposed standard constants file, where the Stella Programmer's Guide gives P1 difficulty on D7 and P0 on D6; Nicolás Olhaberry answered that the guide is right and the masks should be swapped 〔`200207/msg00372`, `200207/msg00373`〕 (Cited only, not verified). This engine puts P0 difficulty on D6 — `roms/litmus/scenarios/swchb.json` sets the P0 switch and asserts the bit `litmus_swchb` copies from D6. | Check a header's equates against a second source before the first program leans on them — the engine's own register map, e.g. `Gopher2600/hardware/memory/cpubus/cpubus.go`'s `0x0280: SWCHA`. | A scenario that asserts the intended response — press the stick, expect movement — fails here as it would on the console; a vote between engines (`cmd/oraclevote`) would pass. No ROM under `roms/` includes `vcs.h` (the 3F note near the top). | stella-list `200110/msg00460`-`00469`; `200207/msg00372` (Erik Eid), `msg00373` (Nicolás Olhaberry) |
| **A `#` in an equate is dropped without a word** | e1will, 2009, asked whether `LEFT_BORDER = #9` differs from `LEFT_BORDER = 9`. SeaGtGruff tried it: *"when I tried it they were interpreted identically. So that means even if you use the pound sign in the equate, you'd still need to use a pound in an instruction if you want it to use the immediate mode"*. tokumaru, before that: *"these would be treated differently in other assemblers I know of"* (which, he does not say), and *"It's not good programming practice to include the "#" in the definition though, because it will be harder to tell immediate and absolute/ZP instructions apart in the code, and that may result in bugs that will take you a long time to find."* Measured 2026-09-24 with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository): after `K = #5`, `lda K` assembles to `a5 05`, a zero-page read, and `lda #K` to `a9 05`; exit 0, no warning. So an equate written to carry the `#` gives the forgotten-`#` trap at every use. Two later reports. A beginner wrote `USA_BLUE = #$84` and then `lda USA_BLUE`: *"It took me a while to realize that the assembler was treating USA_BLUE like a memory address"* — RAM `$84`, the forgotten-`#` row's zone (c). Thomas Jentzsch: *"You missed the # in from of the label (the # at the definition does nothing)"* (AtariAge `topic/266202`, 2017; "from" is his spelling). And the habit has a reason that does not hold: Kirk Israel, told in 2004 that `NUMBER = 8` was *"more standard"* than his `NUMBER = #8`, explained it as *"a side effect of being previously burned by leaving out the # sign when using the constants (i.e. LDA NUMBER when I meant LDA #NUMBER)"* 〔stella-list `200402/msg00023`〕 — by the measurement above, that `#` guards nothing (our reading). Cited only, not verified. | Keep `#` out of equates and write it at the instruction. | As in the forgotten-`#` row: `K = #5` lands in its zone (a), a TIA read register, which nothing here flags. | AtariAge `topic/150617` (e1will, tokumaru, SeaGtGruff), `topic/266202` (Thomas Jentzsch); stella-list `200402/msg00023` (Kirk Israel) |
| **A leading zero makes a number octal: `08` stops the build, and `010` is eight** | Manuel Polik, 2003, assembling a table of equates (`SAW_C4 = 07 \| 00`, `SAW_Aw3 = 08 \| 00`, …): *"Will produce errors like: music.h (16): error: Syntax Error '08 \| 00'. music.h (17): error: Syntax Error '09 \| 00'. Replacing 08 & 09 with $08 & $09 will work though...?!?"* Greg Miller: *"A leading zero is the traditional way of specifying that a number is in octal rather than decimal, and 0-7 are the only valid digits in an octal number."* Andrew Davie: *"Leading 0 prefix indicates an octal number. 09 is an illegal octal number :) So is 08"*. Andy Mucho's cure: *"Turn your 08 \| 00 into 8 \| 0"*. The stop is the safe half. Measured 2026-09-23 with DASM 2.20.14.1 (a scratch build outside this repository): `lda #07` assembles to `a9 07` and `lda #08` stops with exit 5, but `lda #010` assembles to `a9 08` and `lda #012` to `a9 0a`, without an error. So a decimal of 10 or more written with a leading zero, and with no 8 or 9 in it, is read as octal and becomes a different number. | Write decimals without leading zeros and hex with `$`. A program that writes tables must not zero-pad decimal numbers. | Nothing here flags it. | stella-list `200308/msg00093` (Manuel Polik), `200308/msg00094` (Andy Mucho), `200308/msg00095` (Greg Miller), `200308/msg00101` (Andrew Davie) |
| **A binary literal wider than eight digits loses its high bits in `.byte`** | zilog_z80a, 2018, on sprite rows such as `BYTE %00000111100` in ZackAttack's masked-sprite demo: *"isn't a sprite 8 bits long per slice? this sprite is 11 bits per slice!!"* SpiceWare: *"dasm treats all numbers as 32 bit values. When outputting for byte it only uses the lowest 8 bits, so the left 3 bits are lost. You can see how that works if you generating a listing using dasm's -l option"* — in his listing `%10110011100` becomes `9c` — and, quoting `dasm.txt`, *"ALL ARITHMETIC IS CARRIED OUT IN 32 BITS"*: *"DC.B / BYTE will use the rightmost 8 bits of the 32 bit value, and ignore the rest."* ZackAttack: *"I took those sprites from the free sprites thread and didn't notice that they were more than 8 bits wide. So yeah, let's just call that a bug in the example code."* (AtariAge `topic/276058`; Cited only, not verified). | Give every `%` literal in a graphics table eight digits. | Nothing here flags it. Counted 2026-10-01 over the `.asm` sources here, the vendored engine excluded: 154 `%` literals on data lines, every one eight digits. | AtariAge `topic/276058` (zilog_z80a, SpiceWare, ZackAttack) |

| **The analogue glue — five boundaries this harness cannot reach, and their name** | A 2001 question to a homebrew author who had cut down a console (`200101/msg00093`) asks how anyone learns *"how do you know **when to use a capacitor**, and what size? … doesn't **cutting out the excess 2600 parts alter the voltage, line noise, and timing requirements**? … how do you learn all of the **'analogue glue'** that holds the digital design together?"* Five separate limits recorded across this file and `fundamentals-audit` turn out to be one category: **the TV's own contrast through RF**, **console-to-console variation**, **the TIA's propagation delays and dynamic logic** (a VHDL model built exactly from the schematic *oscillates*, and real silicon does not), **cable length and wiring**, and **operating temperature** (the engine's `RESPxHBLANK` calls `HeatThreshold()`). ★**Not one of the five, but the same kind: the CPU's activity can show in the picture.** Kurt Woloch, 1998, with the brightness turned up until black read as grey: *"There are some stripes appearing, which seem to be the processor cycles, since they're all 3 pixels wide. Still, they only appear at some carts, and not at the whole screen"* — below the picture on Ms. Pac-Man, the whole screen on Q*bert, and on Mario Bros.' title screen *"only … at scanlines where something's displayed"*; none on Moon Patrol, Smurf or Carnival. Eric Fischer, on his own set: *"they're a lot more visible when the color saturation knob is turned up really high, and they do go away during the wait for a WSYNC"* 〔`199805/msg00296`, `199805/msg00300`〕. That the stripes are the processor's cycles is Woloch's reading from their width. This engine's picture has no path from bus activity to a pixel (row 11 of the configuration surface: no composite waveform), so a dark screen here is clean whatever the CPU does — derived, Not verified; the reports are Cited only, not verified. Lee Fastenau, 2004, had cleaned a console with the metal shield off the top and bottom of the board and saw *"game-specific noise... almost like ghosting of the game logic across each scanline. When I replaced the shield, the picture was VERY crisp and very colorful."* Chris Wilkson: *"This is normal. It is in fact logic ghosting. Trying pluggin in Kaboom! and moving the paddle to see this effect in a dramatic way."* (his spelling; his emphasis on *dramatic* dropped) 〔`200404/msg00389`, `200404/msg00391`〕. On our reading the shield is part of what keeps that path quiet on a console, so its picture is not the TIA's output alone; Cited only, not verified. | Not a gap to close — a **frontier to name**. This project measures the digital side and can say so precisely: register values, cycle counts, pixel positions, frame structure. Everything downstream of the TIA's pins belongs to somebody with a console, a television and a soldering iron. Say "measured" for the first and "reported" for the second, and never let a green run stand in for a picture nobody looked at. | By construction, no. Each of the five has its own row or note; this one exists so they can be recognised as the same kind of thing. | stella-list `200101/msg00093`; found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2); the stripes `199805/msg00296`, `199805/msg00300` (Kurt Woloch, Eric Fischer); the shield `200404/msg00389`, `200404/msg00391` (Lee Fastenau, Chris Wilkson) |
| **A recording of a console's sound has been through capacitors that this engine's output has not** | andromeda-stardust, 2017: *"Someone recorded output from their VCS playing bass notes and opened it in audacity to study the waveforms, and the distortion curve looked like a textbook example of a high pass. Something to think about, the VCS does apply a bit of analog filtering on the audio which color and shape the sound."* RevEng: *"It was SeaGtGruff that did the study you're talking about. Pretty much all consumer audio gear has capacitors in series with the signal output, to block DC in the signal. What SeaGtGruff observed was only the DC component being blocked. This would also block very low frequencies, but I don't believe it's an attempt to shape the sound, so much as an attempt to avoid burning out the audio circuits or speaker coil in somebody's TV."* and *"It's even likely that his soundcard had caps to block DC at the inputs."* So the shape came from the chain between the TIA and the file, and the thread does not say which stage of it (AtariAge `topic/267305`; Cited only, not verified). This engine's samples pass through no such stage: `mix.Mono` returns entries of a table built from non-negative volumes, so its output sits at or above zero — read from the source, Not verified. | Do not read a droop, or a missing low end, in a recording as a property of the TIA. Compare with the mean removed — `pkg/audio`'s harmonic measure removes it before the transform (`TestHarmonicsFOnADCSignalReportsNoHarmonics`) — or high-pass both signals the same way before comparing shapes. | Nothing here flags it; like the row above, it is downstream of the TIA's pins. | AtariAge `topic/267305` (andromeda-stardust, RevEng); `Gopher2600/hardware/tia/audio/mix/mix.go` |
| **When it works here and not on the machine, suspect things in this order** | The companion to the two frontier rows below: they say what this harness cannot reach, and this says what to do when you are standing at the console anyway. The order is from the archive, and the surprise is how far down the chip is. ★**1. The ROM.** The commonest cause and the only rung you can test from here. A 2K homebrew, 2020, had two in one thread — *"garbage coming on the bottom of the screen for a split second after an enemy has been shot that was on real hardware and not emulator"*, and a title-word bug *"that doesn't happen with an emulator"* — and both were fixed in the ROM (AtariAge `topic/311006`; the author gives no cause). One ROM bug can also look like three different things. xucaen, 2005: a playfield exercise flickered in Stella and looked fine in z26; robert-m suspected missing VSYNC lines, and jacob-rose, loading it into his 2600 through a Supercharger, saw it flicker there too and then go grey and stop, and said he checked his own displays with z26's scanline count. The author counted — 343 lines at 45 FPS — and found `ldx 192` where `ldx #192` was meant (the forgotten `#` row above) (AtariAge `topic/63521`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). ★**2. The contact.** Albert Yarusso, 2003, on a cartridge that ran on a 2600 and not a 7800: *"the connection tabs on the 7800 have to be **a little longer on the cart circuit board** — he extended them and it works fine"*. Chris Wilkson, 1998, on a cartridge too thin to seat: *"push forward on the end of the cart while you turn it on. If this works, you can **use a Combat cart to prop it up**. (The prop wont work on a 2600 jr…no ridges for the Combat to push against)"* — note that the fix is model-specific, which is already rung 3. ★**3. The console's model and era.** Aaron Bergstrom, 2003: *"The **latter era** 7800s removed certain components that Atari Corp in their wisdom deemed unnecessary. It could be that some **2600Jr's, due to cost-cutting**, also have…"* — the same machine name covers boards that differ. ★A tested case, 2016: one bus-stuffing demo on five PAL consoles drew correctly on two 2600 Jr and a Vader and shifted a column of graphics by one pixel on a Light Sixer and a 7800 — first reported as *"shifted 1 pixel to the left"*, then read from the photo by SpiceWare as *"player0 on the "even start" line is shifted to the right by 1 pixel"*, which alex_79 accepted (*"Right."*) — and alex_79 *"swapped the TIA between the Vader and the Sixer, but it made no difference, so the different behaviour is caused by the console itself, not the TIA revision"*. The same model name does not decide it either: omegamatrix *"ran the rom on a NTSC light sixer and a 4 switch. No problems to report."* The code strobes `RESP0` three cycles after `HMOVE` — inside the 24 cycles that §A keeps `HMxx` writes out of, and SpiceWare *"would expect that same consideration to be taken to not strobe the RESxx registers during that time"*. SpiceWare's answer was a per-console `HMP0` — `$C0` on that Sixer instead of `$B0`, chosen with the right difficulty switch, which alex_79 confirmed — and his plan to tell the consoles apart at run time, *"positioning the ball just right so it'll collide with player0 on one system, but not the other"*, was still unfinished when the thread ends (AtariAge `topic/183085`; Cited only, not verified). ★★**4. The chip, and almost never.** Richard Kennehan, 2000, having powered a console through an adapter head fitted backwards: *"I could see **liquid boiling out of this capacitor** on my motherboard… **Amazingly, my Atari still works**. These components are built like tanks! I even soldered wires directly to the TIA chip as part of my composite mod"*. | **Work down the list, not up.** Every rung above the chip is cheaper to test and likelier to be the answer, and rung 4 has a 2000 datapoint saying the silicon survived reversed polarity that boiled a capacitor. Rungs 2 and 3 are also the two this harness is structurally blind to: it has one console, in perfect contact, forever. | 〔`200302/msg00278`, Albert Yarusso; `199805/msg00231`, Chris Wilkson; `200302/msg00277`, Aaron Bergstrom; `200008/msg00078`, Richard Kennehan〕; the five-console test AtariAge `topic/183085` (alex_79, SpiceWare). Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2). The prop citation was given as `199805/msg00242`, which is the reply asking what "prop" means; the advice itself is eleven messages earlier — re-checked here before quoting; the three presentations AtariAge `topic/63521` (xucaen, jacob-rose, robert-m) |
| **The television is the other half of the display, and this harness ends at the frame buffer — six boundaries, and their name** | Sitting beside *The analogue glue* is a second frontier, and it is the one that touches drawing. Our television takes a **digital sync flag**; a real one takes a **voltage** and works out where the picture is. Everything that happens in that gap is outside this repository, and six separate threads in the archive land in it. ★**(1) Sync extraction needs a DC reference, and a correct line count is not enough.** Chris Wilkson, 2005: *"you do need some **DC level reference** before and after the vsync pulses, otherwise **the sync extraction won't know what is the voltage threshold** for sync pulses"* — in a thread where two experts had already answered, correctly and from the manual, that VBLANK has nothing to do with sync. It does not; the picture rolled anyway. The same message gives a number, and hedges it: *"If you aren't changing the luma values it should be ok, I think. But just to be sure the TV can sync properly, you should add 3 VBLANK lines before VSYNC and 3 lines after. Some TVs are just \*really\* picky. This is why I was surprised that pacman and outlaw wait so long to blank the display??"* — two commercial cartridges that, on our reading, do not keep that margin (Cited only, not verified). ★**(2) The 2600 cannot make a compliant interlace signal**, whatever the field pattern looks like. Chris Wilkson, 2002: *"These demos won't be producing true NTSC interlace due to the **lack of proper equalizing pulses and vertical sync serrations**"*; David Schweinsberg, independently, four days later: *"As far as I can tell the **2600 cannot generate half HSYNCs**, so we can never generate a fully compliant interlace signal"*. ★**(3) The same half-line gives different answers on different sets.** Eckhard Stolberg, 2002: *"we would have to know exactly when a "half line" causes the interlace to occur and when it just creates **PAL colour-loss**. But that might be different on didfferent TVs"* 〔`didfferent` is his spelling〕. ★**(4) One 2600 scanline occupies two TV lines, one of them blank** — Clay Halliwell predicted the test (*"The usual 2600 scanline is equal to exactly one NTSC scanline … The test of interlaced output would be if a single scanline was TWICE AS THICK"*), Billy Eno measured it, Glenn Saunders named the mechanism (**phosphor bleedover**), and Andrew Davie stated the geometry: *"a single '2600 scanline is actually **TWO NTSC scanlines (one blank)**"*. ★**(5) An intermediate frame rate can be worse than either end.** Erik Mooney asked whether to *"split the difference and run at **55 FPS**"*; Thomas Jentzsch: *"Some TVs may still roll (maybe even more, since they might be **optimized for fixed frequencies of 50 and 60Hz**)"*. ★**(6) Rolling and wrapping are different failures.** Adam Wozniak, 2005: *"the monitor **cannot sync to the horizontal frequency** I'm giving it. The image is **totally stable vertically** … but it looks as though **each line is wrapped around the screen twice**"* — vertical roll is a vsync problem, sideways wrap is a horizontal-frequency problem, and this harness reports neither, only a line count. ★The two tolerances are not the same size. Bryan, 2016: *"The lines must be sent at a rate of ~15.7KHz or the TV will not sync"* and *"The beam must be returned to the top of the screen at a rate of ~60Hz or the TV will not sync (some are more flexible here than others)"*; potatohead, who had tried *"50 HZ NTSC"* on American sets (*"most them will display it properly"*): *"Of those two hard limits, the horizontal is the primary one. It can be a little off, rounded to a nearest cycle, which the Atari actually does, but not much more than that. The vertical can be off by more, depending on how it's done"* (AtariAge `topic/247329`; Cited only, not verified). "Most" is not "all": (5) above is Jentzsch's warning that some sets roll at an in-between rate. | **Not gaps to close — a frontier to name**, the same shape as row *The analogue glue*. There is no voltage in this engine, no waveform, no half-line, no blank line between scanlines, and no second opinion from a different receiver. Adding any of them would mean modelling a television, not a console. What the boundary buys is knowing which failures cannot be reproduced here **before** spending a night trying: if a picture is right in this harness and wrong on a set, the answer is in this row, and the next step is a television, not a debugger. | Sync/DC 〔`200503/msg00091`, Chris Wilkson〕, in the thread 〔`200503/msg00088`, Thomas Jentzsch; `200503/msg00089`, Dennis Debro〕 — the same message is already cited by row 11 for the composite waveform, which is the point: one message names two boundaries at once. Interlace 〔`200208/msg00124`, Chris Wilkson; `200208/msg00203`, David Schweinsberg〕. Half-line 〔`200208/msg00134`, Eckhard Stolberg〕. Geometry 〔`200208/msg00097`, Clay Halliwell; `200208/msg00098`, Billy Eno; `200208/msg00100`, Glenn Saunders; `200208/msg00132`, Andrew Davie〕. 55 FPS 〔`200404/msg00013`, Erik Mooney; `200404/msg00018`, Thomas Jentzsch〕. Horizontal frequency 〔`200505/msg00003`, Adam Wozniak; `200505/msg00004`, Fred Quimby〕; the two tolerances AtariAge `topic/247329` (Bryan, potatohead). Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2). Every quotation above was re-extracted here with `_work/quote.py` rather than copied, and the Halliwell attribution was re-checked after it was corrected twice: the first search missed the earliest message because that message says TWICE AS **THIN** |
| **Where on the line `VBLANK` or `VSYNC` is written can decide whether a real set rolls — one 2005 thread, and what it left open** | Aaron's Fall Down, 2005: fine in Stella and z26, and *"several people have reported that the title screen rolls on a real machine"* 〔stella-list `200503/msg00096`〕. Dennis Debro, on a 7800 through a CuttleCart 2, first found that the title screen synced with the old `VERTICAL_SYNC` macro and not with Edwin's "VSYNC in 9 bytes", the one then in `macro.h` 〔`200504/msg00007`〕, and then: *"It's VBLANK again. For the title screen you're starting VBLANK at cycle 13. I moved this to right after the WSYNC so it starts at cycle 0 and the screen syncs perfectly now."* 〔`200504/msg00018`〕 Eric Ball, that evening, from the television signal: *"VSYNC has to be asserted before the horizontal sync pulse ends. This causes the horizontal sync pulse to be turned into a serration pulse. Worst case, the STA VSYNC has to end on cycle 10 after the STA WSYNC. (Based on the NTSC spec, some calculations and assumptions.)"*, adding *"there may be TIA limitations which would require the VSYNC to be asserted (and released) earlier, i.e. immediately after STA WSYNC"* 〔`200504/msg00020`〕. Fred Quimby, from the TIA schematics: *"There are no such limitations because VSYNC appears to be basically asynchronous on the TIA"*, and *"there is nothing that would cause syncing problems when asserting VBLANK at weird times. In fact it appears that we can call VBLANK any time we want, even in the middle of a scanline."* As a possible cause he offered a guess about `VSYNC` alone: gate delays in its latch might give a short high-impedance state (*"maybe 5-20 ns or so"*), possible only *"when the latched value and the newly written value are both 1"*, that flutters the composite sync. Of why `VBLANK` would affect `VSYNC` he could say only that they *"happen to be physically close to one another in the schematic"*, and then said *"Other than this wild speculation, I see nothing on the schematics to indicate any crossover effect at all."* 〔`200504/msg00021`〕 Debro narrowed his own result the next morning: *"this syncing problem doesn't occur for the "late" VBLANK write when the old VSYNC macro is used"*, and *"I ran this on a 7800. I have not run this on a VCS"*; Aaron: the problem *"was originally reported on both machines though"* 〔`200504/msg00022`, `200504/msg00023`〕. So his result is not `VBLANK` alone: the late `VBLANK` rolled the picture with the 9-byte macro and not with the old one, and Debro had himself wondered whether the three `VSYNC` writes made `VBLANK` *"sensitive"* 〔`200504/msg00019`〕. The thread does not reconcile Ball's cycle-10 deadline with Quimby's reading. Our television takes a digital sync flag (row 11 of the configuration surface), so neither effect can appear here (Cited only, not verified: a 7800 result, a calculation from the NTSC spec and a schematic reading, no run here; the cycle-10 figure is Not verified). | Write `VSYNC` and `VBLANK` straight after a `WSYNC`, not a dozen cycles into the line; where a real set rolls and a green run here says nothing is wrong, check that cycle first. | `beamtrace` gives the beam clock of each `VSYNC` write; nothing here can show what a set does with it. | stella-list `200503/msg00096` (Aaron); `200504/msg00007`, `msg00018`, `msg00019`, `msg00022` (Dennis Debro), `msg00020` (Eric Ball), `msg00021` (Fred Quimby), `msg00023` (Aaron) |
| **Changing SWACNT and reading straight away passes here and is against the guide** | The Stella Programmer's Guide requires **400 microseconds** between **writing** the port-A direction register and reading the TIA input ports 〔quoted on the list, `200111/msg00192`〕. Chad Schell, who ran a 38.4 kbps serial link through the port, gives the bound that makes it usable: *"If you **only read** the port, and thus don't change it's configuration, the 400 uS delay **does not apply**"* 〔`200111/msg00194`〕 — so ordinary controller reading is unaffected, and only a direction change starts the clock. ★**The engine does not model the delay.** Its author says so and says he does not know the consequence: *"I'm not sure what's supposed to happen if the 400ms is not adhered to. !!TODO: Consider adding 400ms delay"*. Measured rather than taken from the comment (`internal/emu/swacntdelay_test.go`): writing `$F0` to SWACNT and reading SWCHA on the **next instruction** gives `$0F`, and reading **608 cycles** later — past the 477 that 400 µs works out to — gives `$0F` too. | **Put the wait in even though nothing here will ask for it.** Eight WSYNCs clears 400 µs with room. The cost is eight scanlines once, at a moment when the program is reconfiguring a port rather than drawing. ★★And note what is NOT known: the guide states the requirement with no consequence, the engine's author does not know it, and the thread bounds when it applies without saying what breaks. **The leniency is measured; the hardware behaviour is unrecorded**, so "it seems fine on my console" is not evidence either way. | 〔`200111/msg00192`, Nicolás Olhaberry quoting the guide; `200111/msg00194`, Chad Schell〕. Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-1), who matched the engine's open TODO to the thread that answers half of it |
| **A light gun on an LCD: display and detection were to be decoupled, and the gun did not fire** | Thomas Jentzsch, 2017: on a CRT a black frame, then a white square per target, are each *"displayed and detected"* in one frame — *"For a LCD this is not the case."* His plan separated the two with values calibrated per screen: b black frames (*"the maximum time the LCD needs to switch from any brightness to black"*), w white frames (*"the time the LCD needs to switch from black to white"*), and a lag l, the test for black coming *"l + b frames"* after the trigger. The values in his picture, l = 2, b = 2, w = 3, are an example curve, not a measurement; with them *"the whole process requires 11 frames (instead of 2 for a CRT)"*, and *"The LCD values vary between LCDs and even for one LCD depending on its settings and mode."* RevEng added that a TV treating 240p as 480i, or interpolating motion, could smear one target's frame into the next. The CRT test came first: polled once a line, his gun fired only near where it was polled; alex_79 set the input latch (`VBLANK` bit 6) and *"it works in the entire screen"*; Jentzsch: *"it seems, that INPT4 reacts to the racing beam and not what the human eye sees."* After his own LCD TV and monitor: *"I am afraid that this is the end of my experiment regarding LCDs"*, his reading being *"there must be some kind of signal filtering happening"* (AtariAge `topic/261732`; Cited only, not verified). | On a CRT, latch `INPT4` (`VBLANK` D6; the latch mode is verified by `litmus_input` per `fundamentals-audit.md`) or poll it along the whole line, as Jentzsch says the original games do. For an LCD the thread ends with no software answer, and nothing here models a light gun (`hardware/peripherals/controllers/` holds stick, gamepad, paddle and keypad). | AtariAge `topic/261732` (Thomas Jentzsch, RevEng, alex_79) |
| **The browser preview beats against your monitor, and there is no setting that fixes it** | The row below says the emulator judges pictures more harshly than a television. This one says the **previewing** of them adds a defect of its own that no configuration removes. Eckhard Stolberg, 2004, answering someone who suggested finding the right refresh interval: *"**there is no 'best' interval to refresh the display when the emulator and the monitor run at a different frame rate. There always would be artefacts**"* 〔`200405/msg00238`〕. ★An NTSC 2600 frame is **59.92 Hz** — 3,579,545 / 228 / 262, or 1,193,182 / (76 × 262); this line said 60.0544 until 2026-09-30, which is 3,579,545 / 227.5 / 262, the broadcast line length rather than the 2600's 228 colour clocks. A monitor is 60, 120 or 144. Nothing divides. Whatever the previewer does — drop a frame, repeat one, tear — it does something, forever, and the beat between the two rates lands **on top of** whatever the ROM itself is doing. ★★**That matters most exactly where this project judges by eye**: a two-frame flicker seen through a mismatched refresh acquires a slow pulse that is not in the ROM, and a duty ratio chosen for calmness can be undone by it. `build/loops.html` — javatari in a browser — is inside this layer. `build/picture.html` is not: it embeds PNG stills that `cmd/still` rendered beforehand (the generator only reads `build/shots/<name>-4x.png`) and runs no emulator (`grep -c javatari` on it → 0; its generator, `roms/260816_transistor/tools/mk_picture_page.py`, says *"a still that `still` rendered at generation time, not the ROM running"*), so it cannot show a flicker at all. `internal/emu` is outside it: it is headless and calls `SetFPSLimit(false)`, so it never refreshes anything. ★**A 2024 case, and a recording is a second clock too.** Cisano's Terra7 stuttered *"on any emulator I've tried, particularly with the PAL60 and SECAM60 versions"*; Thomas Jentzsch could see it, *"but when I step though the frame one by one (either in debugger or in Time Machine) or rewind and replay it is gone"*. A tester on a PAL 2600 Jr found the movement smooth live and saw the stutter only in his videos — *"I see it on recorded video, but I didn't notice it on the real monitor while I was playing"* — and, asked whether his monitor ran at 60 Hz: *"It was running at 50Hz! After switching to 60Hz it was much better!"*; a PAL light sixer on a CRT had *"no noticeable problems"*. The author was not persuaded — *"If it is not the code, why it happens on real hardware?"*, and after the videos, *"The problem is always there"*. The thread's last test: Silvio Mogno ran the PAL60 build as NTSC, *"and despite the wrong colors it seems the problem disappeared"*, noting that Stella showed *"a round 60.0fps value"* for NTSC and *"59.5fps"* for PAL60; Jentzsch: *"That can partially be explained, the PAL console is slightly slower than the NTSC console"* — *"59.92 Hz for NTSC and 59.38 Hz for PAL-60"* (AtariAge `topic/371257`; Cited only, not verified). ★**And the emulator inside a page has a version.** A beginner's copy of the `RESPx` multi-sprite trick drew a wrapped copy *"off by two pixels"* in 8bitworkshop and correctly in Stella. jetsetilly: *"It's a flaw in 8bitworkshop's emulation unfortunately"*; Al_Nafuur: *"they are using a very very old version (v0.91) of javatari … The current version of javatari is v5.0.4"*, and the author confirmed the demo *"works correctly in the latest version of Javatari 5.0.4"*. jetsetilly added that 5.0.4 still drew the bottom of that screen wrongly, *"NUSIZx related"* on a quick look (AtariAge `topic/346786`; Cited only, not verified). That is a fault in what is drawn, not in the refresh. | **Do not tune artwork against a browser preview's flicker.** Use it to see WHAT is drawn, not HOW it pulses. When the judgement is about how something LOOKS over time, the instruments are a television, or a still comparison, or the duty ratio computed from the ROM (`internal/emu/flickerduty_test.go`) — none of which have a second clock in them. If a preview looks worse than the numbers say, suspect the beat before the design. Before trusting even WHAT a page draws, know which emulator version it embeds. | 〔`200405/msg00238`, Eckhard Stolberg〕. The same thread carries the cost that makes it structural: an emulator of that era needed 8 ms per frame at 500 MHz but a 2 GHz machine to run smoothly 〔`200309/msg00312`〕 — **three quarters of the work was managing the display, not emulating the console**. Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2). The javatari version AtariAge `topic/346786` |
| **The emulator is a HARSHER judge than a television, and this file only names the other direction** | Every row above is a trap of one shape: **passes here, breaks on hardware**. That is the title of this file. The opposite shape has almost nowhere to live, and it costs more in the work actually being done. B. Watson, 1997: **flicker looks worse on an emulator than it does on a television.** The one place this repository says anything like it is `invisible-probe.md`, and it says it only about invisibility: *"the emulator's pixel equality is **stricter than a CRT**, so passing here is necessary and not sufficient"*. ★**Count the directions**: an entire file for *the emulator is too lenient*, one line for *the emulator is too strict*. ★★**And the asymmetry points the wrong way for drawing.** Being wrong on the lenient side ships something broken — bad, visible, and eventually reported. Being wrong on the strict side means an author looks at a picture, judges the flicker unacceptable, and **throws away a design that would have been fine on the machine it is for** — and nothing ever tells them. `max_flicker_area`, `FlickerArea` and every pixel-exact comparison here inherit this: they are sharper than any console ever was. | **There is no gate for this and there should not pretend to be one.** What there can be is the habit: when a judgement is about how something LOOKS and the answer is *too much*, that verdict was taken on an instrument that exaggerates. Look at it through `cmd/crtview`, or on a television, before discarding anything. | B. Watson 〔stella-list `200109/msg00253`〕; the strictness line predates it in `invisible-probe.md`. Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2), who first reported harness had only an LCD-vs-CRT axis, corrected themselves to three emulator-vs-television passages, and **the correction made the finding stronger** — the passages exist and all three point the same way |
| **A Flashback is tuned for its built-in games, and where it departs from the console is not written down** | The reason, from billloguidice, 2016, answering homebrew authors whose games did not run from the SD card: *"these are designed to play the built-in games, so that's what the emulator is tuned for. Every time new games are added, more tuning is done"*, *"The SD card option is considered a bonus feature"*, *"(and yes, sometimes fixing one game breaks another, which then needs to be fixed, and so on and so forth)"*, and why: *"These are low price, low margin products that sell in known quantities each year, so the budget you're talking about is quite tight."* Asked what had changed in the emulator over the years, keatah: *"Since the flashback hardware has changed several times over the years I would expect the internal emulator to have been re-written several times or have changed altogether. If you're looking for a changelog like that which accompanies Emulator Stella or Emulator MAME, it's not likely to be revealed. That's internal company documentation."* kaeruyojimbo, having *"only skimmed the compatibility list"*, read the failures as *"many of the homebrews that don't work"* being games *"that use newer developments like DPC+ and the Melody board"* (AtariAge `topic/260084`). The symptoms, from Nukey Shay, 2019, who ports games to the Portable: *"Consider playfield register updates: at the latest, these must occur at specific cycle counts. Most of the time the portable follows spec. Other times, the cycle count needed will deviate for no discernible reason and need a bit of trial/error to fix. CTRLPF is a real mind bender...often when called midscanline (or even at the end of a scanline), it will affect everything that had already been drawn. And of course, VBLANK...no rhyme or reason why it pushes the display in some programs but not in others."* — the last of the three is in the row below as well. His 8K Pac-Man hack would not start on a reader's Flashback 9 or on his Portable (*"Both banks are nearly full"*, *"F8 standard banking only"*, and *"AFAIK, the flashback emulator is the only platform Pac-Man8k fails on"*); he fixed it later and gave a belief, not a measurement: *"I believe that the portable's emulator treated this game as using Superchip Ram - effectively making all the bankswitch instructions and all other program code on the lowest page of Rom unable to execute. I already had enough free space in both banks to keep the lowest page unused, so even if it still misreads it as a Superchip program, it doesn't matter."* (AtariAge `topic/296200`). Getting a ROM recognised at all has two recipes that disagree. karl-g, 2020, on the Portable: *"Sometimes a multi-bank ROM is not recognized if there is a lot of empty space in some of the banks. In this case, you can pad the babks by adding multiple data statements. Use a value other than $FF, though."* (`babks` is his), and for a SuperChip game, marked as his guess, *"you need to fill the first 256 bytes at the beginning of each bank with zeros. I haven't had to do this myself"*; Nukey Shay, two weeks later: *"I've had to fill the added Ram in Superchip roms with $FF's to get those to be detected by the portable. 100% reliable so far (if that was the only issue present)."* (AtariAge `topic/300586`). The same post of his has *"Multiple reads to SWCHA (during each frame) can cause problems occasionally...try to limit that to once per frame if possible."* `docs/techniques/input-budget.md` already puts the joystick read once per frame, for its cycle cost rather than for this. All Cited only, not verified. ★This engine also decides SuperChip from content: `hasSuperchip` in `Gopher2600/hardware/memory/cartridge/fingerprint.go` calls an image SuperChip when bytes `$00`-`$7F` of every 4K bank equal bytes `$80`-`$FF`, which a lowest page filled with one value in every bank satisfies — read from the source, Not verified. `internal/emu.TestCartridgeRAMIsRareAndNamed` fails if a technique or litmus ROM other than `litmus_superchip` starts mapping cartridge RAM. | When a ROM fails on a Flashback, suspect the device's tuning before the ROM, and name the device with the result. Where a SuperChip guess would hurt, keep code off the lowest page of each bank. On the Portable, read `SWCHA` once per frame. | Nothing here models a Flashback. | AtariAge `topic/260084` (billloguidice, keatah, kaeruyojimbo), `topic/296200` (Nukey Shay), `topic/300586` (karl-g, Nukey Shay) |
| **Emulators can agree with each other and disagree with the machine** | Cross-checking one emulator against others — what `cmd/oraclevote` does, and what "Stella 37/37" means — assumes the errors are independent. They are not always. stella-list `200211/msg00098`ff, on a PAL/NTSC auto-detect routine tested on real hardware and three emulators at once: *"It **does display NTSC on my hardware**. It **displays PAL on every emulator I've tried** (Of the emulators I've tried (StellaX, Pcaewin and z26) **only z26 displays Kool-Aid Man correctly. However z26 cheats!! It's not actually doing the emulation it's just detecting the presence of the Kool-Aid Man rom (presumably through checksum because changing even 1 byte in Kool-Aid Man makes z26 display incorrectly as per the other emus) and then compensates to the undocumented bahaviour.** Boy did I ever waste a bunch of time trying to extract the code and test it on z26 before realising this!! (smile))"* **Three emulators, one answer, and it was the wrong one.** A majority vote would have carried the error unanimously. **★And the fourth emulator is worse than wrong.** This passage was elided with `[…]` until 2026-09-06, and what it hides is the sharpest evidence the row has: z26 gave the right answer for that one ROM **because it recognised the ROM**, not because it modelled anything — *"it's just detecting the presence of the Kool-Aid Man rom … and then compensates"*. The author proved it by **changing one byte**, which made z26 fail like the others. That is a fourth way for an emulator to be wrong, and the one a majority vote handles worst: a right answer with no model behind it is indistinguishable from a right answer at the moment you take the vote, and it stops being right the moment your ROM differs by a byte. He fixed Kool-Aid Man's own trap-#67 violation four messages later 〔`200211/msg00102`〕 — the same person, the same month, the same ROM. ★**z26's side had said so in January 2002, and named the window.** Asked how PCAE or z26 handle a `RESxx` within 24 cycles after an `HMOVE`, Eckhard Stolberg: *"The results depend on what was in the corresponding HMPx register before the HMOVE. I think we have a table for handling that in z26, which John and I got from systematically trying out all possible combinations on a real VCS. We only experimented with accesses to one motion register during the 24 cycles after HMOVE though. So games that do more accesses, like Kool Aide Man still don't get emulated properly, and we have to cheat for them."* 〔`200201/msg00082`〕. The asker, Nicolás Olhaberry, had handled it in his own emulator *"with some kludges, like if the RESXX occurs 21 cycles after HMOVE I add 2 to the object expected position, or if it occurs at 20, I add 5"* 〔`200201/msg00079`〕. So the 24 cycles after `HMOVE` (§A) are one of those margins: in z26 a table taken from hardware for one access, and a cheat for the games that do more (Cited only, not verified). ★★**Two more shapes, 2017.** *Scattered*: stephena ran one ROM through five emulators — *"Stella 5 is correctly emulating the behaviour I see on my light-sixer with a Harmony cart"*, 6502.ts too *"(not surprising, since its TIA core was ported to Stella)"*, and *"MAME, Javatari and z26 are all incorrect (in slightly different ways) … Stella 4 and MAME give the same (incorrect) output. z26 seems to be doing something completely different. And Javatari is acting like 8bitworkshop"*. The majority was wrong, in three different ways; the two right answers were one TIA core; and one of the wrong ones is **MAME, the engine `cmd/oraclevote` counts as independent**. *Hardware agreeing with an emulator*: an emulator author, having fixed Battlezone's radar in his own code, *"tried Battlezone on an Atari Flashback Portable and...guess what...it has the same misplaced pixels I was getting"* — a product sold as the console is an implementation too, so "checked on hardware" has to name which. The Atari 2600+ sold today is one: *"The 2600+ is an emulator. You want to test your homebrew on real hardware"* (living-room-arcade), and, as verdant understood it, it *"doesn't run games directly from the cartridge, but rather dumps the ROM to internal storage and then emulates it from there"* (AtariAge `topic/360822`; Cited only, not verified). The Flashback Portable is another: nukey-shay, who ports games to it and calls it *"a flaky emulator"*, lists where it departs: *"Code existing on bankswitch hotspots do NOT trigger the bank...for example, the init code $FFF6: JMP Clean_Start existing in the 2nd bank of an F8 game will not flip to the first bank as the destination address is read"*, and *"VBLANK writes can cause the portable's display to be shifted downward a number of lines. This does not happen in every game...but fortunately, you can completely remove/ignore VBLANK writes for -any- game when running on the portable"* (AtariAge `topic/300586`; Cited only, not verified). And the quiet version: a beginner who got different results from 8bitworkshop, Stella and a PAL console wrote *"That's fine, I guess its to be expected"*; Thomas Jentzsch's first answer was that the frame was unstable in Stella. ★★**Sound is the plainest case, and the engine's own source says so.** Frank Palazzolo, 2001, announcing a change to Ron Fries' TIA sound code: *"currently used it every Atari 2600/7800 emulator around (I think)"* 〔stella-list `200108/msg00009`; his hedge, from one post〕. The audio package of the engine says of the code it runs now: *"completely superceded by the work of Chris Brenner. Chris's work has been used in both the 6502.ts and Stella projects. Source from both projects was used as reference. Both projects are exactly equivalent."* (`Gopher2600/hardware/tia/audio/doc.go`). Our reading: on sound, agreement with Stella is agreement with the lineage this engine's audio was built from, not a second opinion; Stella's source was not read here (Not verified). Ron Fries' TIASound, the code Palazzolo means, is not that code: the same `doc.go` says the engine replaced it. The "two independent emulator implementations" sentence in `docs/stella-oracle.md` is about the RAM results of CPU and timer tests, not about sound. | Treat emulator agreement as evidence that the emulators share a model, not that the model is right — especially where the thing under test is *how a real chip behaves at the margin*. Prefer a litmus whose claim can be checked against a picture or against documented silicon, and say "our engines agree" rather than "the hardware does" when that is all that was established. | Not detectable from inside. The 2002 thread found it because someone owned a console; nothing in this repository can substitute for that. | stella-list `200211/msg00098`–`00110` (Christopher Tumber, 2002-11-15); `200201/msg00079`, `200201/msg00082` (Nicolás Olhaberry, Eckhard Stolberg); found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-1), the elided passage recovered by helper-2 and re-read from the archive here — the quotation above is verbatim to the author's own spelling of *"bahaviour"*. The scattered case AtariAge `topic/271313`; the Flashback Portable `topic/260569` (dan-piponi) and `topic/300586` (nukey-shay); the beginner `topic/301514`; the 2600+ `topic/360822`; sound stella-list `200108/msg00009` (Frank Palazzolo), `Gopher2600/hardware/tia/audio/doc.go` |

| **Ten litmus ROMs measure a TIA that has no revision bugs, because ours is switched off** | The engine models **eight TIA-revision behaviours** — `LateVDELGRP0/1`, `LateRESPx`, `EarlyScancounter`, `LatePFx`, `LateColor`, `LostMOTCK`, `RESPxHBLANK` — and **all eight default to false**; nothing in this repository has ever set one. They are not hypothetical: `hardware/tia/revision/bugs.go` names shipped cartridges (`LostMOTCK` → *"Cosmic Ark (starfield)"*, `LateColor` → *"QuickStep"*, and *"some TIAs that are on the edge of tolerance can also exhibit this … such as an RGB MOD"*, `LatePFx` → *"Pesco"*), and `RESPxHBLANK` adds that the effect *"seems to be affected by operating temperature"*. **That last explanation outruns its own citation, checked 2026-09-04.** The comment points at stella-list `199901/msg00089`; that post contains **zero occurrences of "HBLANK" and zero of "temperature"** (counted). What it does contain is a console-to-console discrepancy — *"I get only two copies at Hit5 … **what version of the VCS are you using?**"* — and its author's own hedge, *"**maybe I need to revise my 5 pixel delay theory again**."* The citation is genuine (same phenomenon, same test ROM) and the mechanism is not in it: HBLANK and temperature must come from later work the code does not name. ★**Looked for, 2026-09-07, and it is not findable from here either.** The nearest candidate is AtariAge topic 316950 *tia-differences-models*, which carries the same pairing — a temperature dependence, HMOVE and multi-sprite position tolerance — and **cites `199901/msg00089` for it, exactly as the engine does**. So two modern sources attribute the mechanism to a 1999 post that contains neither word, and neither says where it actually came from. ★★**That is as far as this repository can take it**: `reference/`'s AtariAge material is distilled notes with **no primary text**, so the topic's own wording cannot be checked here the way a stella-list quotation can — the limit is recorded rather than papered over. **So the flag models a real effect on an unstated basis**, which is a second reason not to read our flag-off numbers as hardware. **Measured over all 145 litmus ROMs, ten render differently with a flag on, each under exactly one flag**: `LateColor` → `litmus_bpl_trip`, `litmus_dag_region`, `litmus_deadbranch`, `litmus_pagealign`, `litmus_pcm`; `LatePFx` → `litmus_pf0_reflect`, `pf_late`, `pf_wraps`; `LostMOTCK` → `litmus_hmove_mid`, `litmus_hmove_side`. | Not a defect to fix — a **scope** to state. Those ten facts are true of a TIA without those bugs, and a ROM that depends on them may behave differently on a console that has them. When a measurement lands in the ten, say which TIA it describes. **`litmus_respx_phase` was checked specifically and is NOT affected**: `RESPxHBLANK` applies only to a strobe at `hsync == 16 \|\| 18` on rising phi2, and `LateRESPx` only inside HBLANK during a starting HMOVE ripple, and that ROM strobes in the visible area — so the **+5/+4** is safe for the case it measures and unmeasured for the two those flags govern. | `internal/emu/tiarevision_test.go` renders each affected ROM with the flag on and off and fails if the dependency changes in either direction. Five flags move nothing here: the corpus has no fixture reaching their conditions. | `Gopher2600/hardware/tia/revision/bugs.go`, which cites stella-list `199901/msg00089` — *"maybe I need to revise my 5 pixel delay theory again"* — a post inside the archive being distilled; found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2) |
| **Two consoles of the same model can disagree by a cycle on a playfield write, and only a write at the edge of its window shows it** | Lee Seitz, 1997, running Erik Mooney's Invaders alpha 2 from a Supercharger: extra lines between certain columns of invaders on one 2600 Jr (serial #860746015) and none on another (#870014229) — *"The only thing I changed out was the console. Same Supercharger, WAV file, cords, etc."* Mooney's reading: column 25 is the first column of the second copy of PF1, and *"The lower serial number model responds to PF1 writes one cycle (possibly as low as one pixel) later than the other 2600jr and my 4-switch 2600. … It'll only mess up if the program updates the second copy of PF1 on the last possible cycle."* 〔stella-list `199704/msg00146`, `199704/msg00147`〕 A day later he counted it: the second copy's first pixel falls in the last third of cycle 55, the game's second PF1 write ended on cycle 54, *"a two-pixel (558.7 nanosecond) interval"*, and *"Apparently, the RAM inside the TIA in older 2600s and some 2600jrs does have a response time under 558.7 ns, but the RAM in the TIA in your weird 2600jr and the Coleco 2600 adapter does not respond that fast"* 〔`199704/msg00155`〕. Seitz had by then a report of the Coleco drawing the lines too 〔`199704/msg00151`〕, and Glenn Saunders saw the playfield update correctly on a first-revision 7800 with a Supercharger 〔`199704/msg00148`〕. This engine has one TIA, and a window edge measured here is that TIA's; `LatePFx` (the row above) is the engine's own switch for a late playfield write, off here, and whether it matches this case is not checked. The cycle counts are Mooney's, the chip's response time is his inference, and no console was run here (Cited only, not verified; the engine side Not verified). | Treat a playfield write that lands on the last cycle its window allows as depending on the console; where there is room, write it a cycle earlier. When hardware shows a defect the emulator does not, and the write sits at a window's edge, suspect the console as well as the code. | `pf_deadlines` is a pass or fail against one deadline (`docs/scenarios.md`), so a write on the last allowed cycle passes; on a pass it reports the room of only one write, the one with the least (`tightest is <reg> at clock <n> against a deadline of <d>`, `internal/cyclebound/pfdeadline.go`), and no other write's room. | stella-list `199704/msg00146`, `msg00147`, `msg00155` (Lee Seitz, Erik Mooney), `msg00148` (Glenn Saunders), `msg00151` (Lee Seitz) |

| **A shipped game DEPENDS on bus residue — the third direction** | The two rows above say *do not read an undriven address*. A 2001 report says a commercial cartridge cannot run unless you do. Someone writing their own emulator found Haunted House (4K PAL) looping forever on `SBC $0F / BCS`: *"Both PCAE and Z26 … leave the accumulator with `$F3`, so **the value subtracted was `$F`**. In my emu, since [it] is subtracting zero … the game **enters into an endless loop**. Since the address `$000F` **can't be read, the data bus isn't updated**, so the subtract is made with `$0F` which is **the last value loaded into the bus**."* Gopher2600 models it — `memory/memory.go:189`, `data \|= mem.LastCPUData & ^mem.DataBusDriven` — so the ROM runs here. ★**A second title, and a console that reads the residue as zero.** Eckhard Stolberg, 2002: *"On the 2600 the 6 lower bits will always read whatever data was on the data bus last. Usually this is the address of the register. On the 7800 these bits will always be zero. Mountain King for example starts to (accidentally ?) use the TIA read registers as level data pointers when you go too high. This is how the secret kingdom comes to be. On the 7800 you can't get there, since the accidental level data will be different."* His six bits are a collision register's, which drives D7 and D6; `INPT4`, in (a) of the next column, drives D7 only and leaves seven — our reading. Cited only, not verified — the 7800 is not modelled here. | Do not "fix" residue reads out of a corpus ROM you are studying: on some titles the residue **is** the value. Two separate facts follow. **(a) The residue is the last byte the CPU put on the bus**, which is why `INPT4`'s low seven bits read as the previous data rather than as anything about the trigger — `INPT4` drives D7 only. **(b) The pattern is not universal.** The engine's own comment: *"this pattern is good for replicating what we see on the **pluscart** … a **different bit pattern can be seen on the Harmony**"*, and `RandomPins` (default **off**, never set here) exists for the superchip case where the pins are *"more indeterminate"*. So a ROM that leans on residue is portable across a narrower set of hardware than its author knew. | `mining-digest.md:814` already points at a Haunted House disassembly, so the dependency can be confirmed from bytes without opening anyone's commented source. Nothing detects the pattern automatically. | stella-list `200107/msg00044`, `200211/msg00187` (Eckhard Stolberg); `Gopher2600/hardware/memory/memory.go`; found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2) |

| **`ROL`/`ROR` rotate THROUGH the carry, so two of them are not two of one** | `ROL` shifts bit 7 into C and C into bit 0. Do it once and the carry you started with lands in bit 0; do it twice and the *first* rotation's bit 7 comes back round. A 2004 report reads like a hardware fault and is not: *"1 time gives a good result. **2 times makes a total mess.** First line only → good. Second line only → good. **Both lines and it's a total mess!**"* — answered on the spot, *"is the carry bit messing you up? **The rotate instructions rotate data through the carry bit.**"* The symptom points away from the cause: each half works, so the pair looks like a timing or alignment problem. | `CLC` (or `SEC`) before the first rotate when you want a defined bit in, and remember the second rotate inherits the first's output. If you want a plain shift, `ASL`/`LSR` do not involve C on the way in. | **Do not make this a static check.** The chain is used deliberately here — `maze.md` turns four source bits into eight output bits precisely by rotating twice through carry — so a rule would fire on correct code. "Suspicious, read it" is the right strength. Third of a family this file already has: `cmp` clobbering Z between a load and a zero test, and an immediate `ld_` clobbering N/Z. | stella-list `200404/msg00370`; found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2) |

### The delay table, measured — and the row that has been wrong since 1998

"The shortest code that wastes exactly N cycles" is a daily 2600 tool, and the table everyone
quotes has a defect its own authors flagged and never fixed.

Andrew Davie posted it 〔`199805/msg00090`, 1998-05-09 11:07:03〕 under the constraint that makes it
useful — *"these delays are designed to be NON-destructive of memory. Ie: they have no effect other
than delay or the accumulator and/or flags"* — and corrected one row **eight minutes later**
〔`199805/msg00091`, 11:15:37〕: *"That should be 5@3. I can't figure a 2 byte non-destructive 5
cycle delay :("*. He fixed the row he was looking at. **`11@4` contains the same `STA $8000,X` and
was not fixed.** Paul Slocum carried the table into the 2600 Cookbook six years later
〔`200404/msg00246`〕 still reading `11@4`, under a heading that says so out loud:

    WASTING CYCLES
    Christopher Tumbler, Chris Wilkson, Andrew Davie
     Todo: Verify Andrew's

That Todo is discharged here. All 21 rows measured 2026-09-06 against the engine, cycles from the
CPU counter and bytes from DASM's own symbol table (`roms/litmus/litmus_delaytable.asm`,
`internal/emu/delaytable_test.go`). **Twenty rows match. One does not:**

| cycles | bytes | code | |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 1 | `nop` | |
| 3 | 2 | `lda $80` | |
| 3 | 1 | `pha` | 2004 |
| 3 | 2 | `dop $80` | 2004, **ILLEGAL opcode** |
| 4 | 1 | `pla` | 2004 |
| 4 | 2 | `nop / nop` | |
| 4 | 2 | `lda $80,x` | 2004 — **+1 cycle for ZERO extra bytes** over `lda $80` |
| 4 | 3 | `lda.w $80` | 2004 |
| 5 | 2 | `dec $2D` | 2004 — ★see the hazard below |
| 5 | 3 | `sta $8000,x` | Davie's own 8-minute correction |
| 6 | 2 | `lda ($80,x)` | |
| 7 | 2 | `pha / pla` | |
