| 7 | 3 | `rol $8000,x` | |
| 8 | 3 | `lda ($80,x) / nop` | |
| 9 | 3 | `pha / pla / nop` | |
| 9 | 4 | `lda ($80,x) / lda $80` | |
| 10 | 4 | `rol $80 / ror $80` | |
| 10 | 4 | `dec $2D / dec $2D` | 2004 |
| **11** | **5** | `sta $8000,x / lda ($80,x)` | ★**published as `11@4` for 26 years. It is 5.** |
| 12 | 3 | `jsr` + shared `rts` | 3 = the caller's bytes |
| 12 | 4 | `lda ($80,x) / lda ($80,x)` | |

**The correction is stronger than "the byte count is off by one."** Shortening that row to four bytes
means using `sta $80,x` instead of `sta $8000,x`, which also drops it to **ten** cycles — measured as
a negative control. There is no eleven-cycle four-byte form; the published row names something that
does not exist.

**And `STA $8000,X` is not a write to ROM.** Davie closed the post asking *"Any comments on the
danger of 'writing' to ROM?"* and the thread never answered. There is no ROM at `$8000` on a 2600:
the address bus is 13 bits and A12 selects the cartridge, so `$8000` folds to `$0000` — the TIA. The
litmus makes that visible rather than arguing it: with `x = $09` the "harmless" write sets **COLUBK**
and the background changes colour. Which register it hits is whatever `X` happens to hold, so this
idiom is non-destructive only by luck. `x = $02` folds to **WSYNC** and halts the CPU to the end of
the line — a delay of a completely different length, from an instruction whose whole purpose was to
have a known one.

**`dec $2D` is the 2004 answer to Davie's 1998 lament, and it carries the hazard in the row above.**
The trick is not a cleverer instruction but a better address: *"locations $2D-$3F do nothin and
aren't decoded"* 〔`200404/msg00246`〕 — the instruction is destructive, and there is nothing there to
destroy. Verified here for **writes** (a separate axis from the read-side folding): writing `$FF` to
seven addresses in that range moves no write-only TIA register, with a write to `$09` as the negative
control. But `$2D` sits inside `$00`-`$3F`, so on a **3F/X07 cart it is a bankswitch hotspot** by the
row above — and `scripts/check_traps.py` matches only `nop`/`bit`, so it will not flag `dec $2D`.
The same paragraph in the Cookbook says as much (*"The only case where this might be a problem is if
you're using an unusual bankswitching setup"*), and the engine's own mapper agrees from the other
side: *"tigervision cartridges use mirror addresses to write to the TIA"*
(`Gopher2600/hardware/memory/cartridge/mapper_tigervision.go`).


## E. TIA read / audio / emulator-fidelity

| trap | note | source |
|---|---|---|
| **an ODD immediate written to a colour register** | D0 of `COLUP0`/`COLUP1`/`COLUPF`/`COLUBK` does nothing — hue is D7..D4, luminance D3..D1 — so **`#$45` is the same colour as `#$44`, and `#$46` is the next one**. Measured 2026-09-06 against the palette this repository derives from the renderer: `$44` and `$45` both give RGB(204,33,33), `$46` gives (236,51,51). Writing an odd value never crashes, which is why nothing caught it; it is a sign the author believes in a colour that is not there. **The machine has 128 colours, not 256**, and an odd literal rounds DOWN — never towards the one the author was probably reaching for. `cmd/palette` names its swatches with the even code for the same reason, and a 256-entry palette file shows 128 colours the machine cannot make. ★Read the other way, the same fact is a hint for finding a colour table in a ROM: nukey-shay — *"the 2600 does not use the rightmost bit for color data (so in color data tables, you'll often see that bit is zero)"* (AtariAge `topic/218383`; Cited only, not verified — zero padding and many graphics bytes are even too, and the table he named in his own previous post shares its zeros with a bitmap). | static: `scripts/check_traps.py` warns on `lda #$xx` (odd) immediately followed by `sta COLUxx`. Positive and negative controls both run; **zero hits across the tree's 411 files**, so it is a guard against new code rather than a backlog | Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-3), who checked first — `rg -ni 'odd colour\|odd color' docs/ pkg/design/` returned nothing — and measured here |
| **an object drawn only as SCENERY still sets its collision latch** | The TIA compares geometry and has no notion of what a program MEANS by an object. Borrow a missile for an explosion, a shadow or a piece of the background and it keeps colliding at its first job — the latch is set by an overlap the program is no longer thinking about. This repository teaches the borrowing (`design-principles.md`: *"Multi-kernel = reuse one object per region"*, and the list's *"intelligent sprite flicker and reuse"*); until 2026-09-06 it did not teach the bill. **Measured** (`roms/litmus/litmus_decor_collides.asm`, `internal/emu/decorcollide_test.go`): a missile enabled over a quad-width player reads `CXM1P` = **$81, D7 set**; clear `ENAM1`, leave everything else identical, and it reads **$01, D7 clear**. The fix is not `CXCLR` after the fact — the latch is sticky, so clearing it only moves the problem to whoever reads next; stop TESTING the collision for the frames the object is on loan. ★And a note on measuring it: the first fixture strobed `RESP0` and what it called `RESM1` three CPU cycles apart and read no collision at all, which looks exactly like the trap being absent. `DecomposeRow` put the objects at clocks 3..10 and 25..32 — twenty-two apart, never touching (2026-09-29: the cause was the fixture's own `RESM1 = $12`, which is `RESM0`, so M1 was placed only by the init loop's write to `$13`; with `$13` in the equate the two overlap at normal width too). **A negative result from a fixture that never posed the question is not a negative result**, so the test now asserts that the player is drawn before it asserts anything about the latch (the overlap itself shows only through band A). ★**The other direction: a gap in the drawn shape is a gap in what can be hit**, when the hit is the latch — derived from the overlap rule, Not verified. Of Berzerk, a 2008 thread records that in the arcade game and the home version alike a horizontal enemy shot passes harmlessly through the one blank pixel between the player's head and body, told as behaviour a remake should keep (AtariAge `topic/132083`, held here only as distilled notes; Cited only, not verified). The thread does not say how the 2600 version tests the hit. ★**Hidden by colour is still drawn.** tokumaru, 2007, proposed an asymmetric-looking playfield made by giving it the background's colour over one side; Thomas Jentzsch's answer, held here only as distilled notes: it can be done but is inflexible, needs two TIA writes, saves almost no CPU time, and the hardware collision detection can no longer be used (AtariAge `topic/104777`; Cited only, not verified). The pixels coloured like the background still overlap whatever crosses them — the overlap rule above, derived, Not verified. | The bug report is from the author of the game it happened in: Piero Cavina on INV, *"This must happen because the program is checking missile/invaders collisions even when the missile is used for the explosion"* 〔`199801/msg00197`, 1998-01〕 — the symptom was that shooting one of two adjacent invaders in the top row exploded both. Found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-1), measured here; the Berzerk gap AtariAge `topic/132083`; the colour-hidden playfield AtariAge `topic/104777` (Thomas Jentzsch) |
| **collision latches are not set while `VBLANK` is on** | Thomas Jentzsch, 2025, building a lights-out effect: *"I just discovered something that I didn't know for 25 years (or had simply forgotten"* — *"During VBLANK, hardware collision detection does not work! Or am I wrong and it is my code?"*; JetSetIlly: *"If I'm reading the schematics correctly, then I think you're right. I didn't realise that either."* Dionoid then quoted the Stella Programmer's Guide — *"The collision registers could be read at any time but is usually done during vertical blank after all possible collisions have occurred"* — and JetSetIlly put it exactly: *"The collisions registers can be read at any time but they are only set when VBLANK is disabled."* (AtariAge `topic/385470`; Cited only, not verified). So an overlap that `VBLANK` hides — the mid-line mask in §A's last-picture-line row, or objects left on during vertical blank — sets no latch: derived, Not verified. ★**The engine has had it only since that thread.** Jentzsch: *"Fortunately Stella emulates this correctly"*; asked about Gopher2600, JetSetIlly: *"It does now"*. The vendored engine carries that commit (`2d6b6021`, 2025-11-26, *"TIA collisions do not take place when VBLANK is enabled"*), which gates the latches in `hardware/tia/video/video.go` with `if !vblank && (vd.Player0.pixelCollision \|\| vd.Player1.pixelCollision \|\|` — read from the source, Not verified: no litmus overlaps two objects under `VBLANK`. | AtariAge `topic/385470` (Thomas Jentzsch, JetSetIlly, Dionoid); `Gopher2600/hardware/tia/video/video.go` |
| **a software collision test against the playfield reads the bytes as stored, and the screen shows some of them reversed** | omegamatrix, 2016, to tschak909, who was fighting collision correction for a player moved by velocity, advising software collisions: *"Before you start remember that how you store the playfield blocks in rom are not always how they appear on the screen. Some will be reflected. You need to figure out which parts are reflected and which are not. (ie which parts of left side PF0,PF1,PF2, and right side PF0,PF1,PF2 are reflected when compared to how it's stored). When checking the date you have to flip the playfield bits which are reflected in rom, or write some convoluted code just to chose the right bit."* (*"date"* as written). His alternative: *"making a copy of your playfield data that is already correctly orientated so you simply don't have to deal with any of the flipping"* (AtariAge `topic/253865`; Cited only, not verified). The orders it depends on are measured here: `docs/techniques/maze.md` gives the playfield bit order as `read_row`-verified — *"PF1 MSB-first, PF2 LSB-first, reflect mirrors the left half"*. No test here checks a software collision lookup against what `read_row` shows. | AtariAge `topic/253865` (omegamatrix) |
| **a collision register stored whole keeps both of its latches — its D6 is another pair's collision** | Each `CXxx` read carries two latches, D7 and D6 (`docs/resources.md`), for two different pairs: in `CXM0P` D7 is M0–P1 and D6 is M0–P0 (the engine's `hardware/tia/video/collisions.go`, read from the source). Dennis Debro, 2004, on a Climber 5 bug in which a death could send the player to the next level: he stored the `CXM0P` read in a state flag and tested its D7 for the death, while D6 of the same flag meant the ball had been retrieved, which he took from a masked `CXP1FB`. *"What I didn't count on was the climber being hit by a girder and falling object at the same time. I wasn't masking the value from the CXM0P read so D6 could've been set from this collision. Which made it look like the player was hit (death animation and sound) but he retrieved the ball (go to the next level)."* He opened with *"I think I found the problem now. I'm having trouble reproducing it so it's hard to tell."* 〔stella-list `200402/msg00102`〕 (Cited only, not verified). It takes two overlaps in one frame, which a test that poses one collision at a time does not reach — our reading, Not verified. The fix is to mask a collision read down to the bit it is for before storing it beside other flags. | stella-list `200402/msg00058`, `200402/msg00102` (Dennis Debro) |
| **reading a write-only TIA register** | returns bus float; only **bits 6/7 are driven** (collision/INPT) — don't rely on the full byte | 319781, 328451, 63342 |
| **the undriven bits of a TIA read come back 0 on a 7800 — on later models, by its author's account two years on** | Eckhard Stolberg, 2002, asked whether a PAL 7800 plays a 2600 game as a PAL 2600 does: *"The only real difference between the two is in the handling of the undefined bits in the TIA read registers. On a 2600 the undefined bits will get the state of whatever value was on the data bus last, while on the 7800 these bits will always be 0. So if you are accidentally doing "LDA $07" instead of "LDA #$07", and then rely on the state of the six lower bits, it might work on the 2600, but not on the 7800."* His next paragraph names another, so "only" is about the bus: *"all (I think) PAL 7800 have the TIA chip that has problems with Kool-Aid Man. so if your game does some similarily wierd positioning sequence, it will not work on the PAL 7800. But it wouldn't work on some 2600 Jrs. either."* That one is a difference in the TIA, apart from the bus bits, and on his account not the 7800's alone; which of the engine's revision flags (the row "Ten litmus ROMs measure a TIA that has no revision bugs") it would be, if any, he does not say (Cited only, not verified). ★**Two years later he narrowed it himself.** Aaron, 2004, had found Stolberg's earlier statement in the archives and proposed `lda $0f` / `beq this_is_a_7800` to tell the consoles apart. Edwin Blink: *"it is possible for the 2600 to get rubbish or '0's floating on the databus too. It depends very much on the hardware pluged in and is not very reliable."* Stolberg: *"Also not all 7800s will zero out the undefined bits on the TIA read registers. This is just a side effect of a hardware fix that Atari build into later models of the 7800. This helped with some problems that the games with cartridge RAM had, but broke compatibility with Activision's FE bankswitching and the Supercharger. The origial 7800's would react just like a 2600 on a TIA read. Also some people disabled the extra hardware (only requires removing a certain capacitor) to make their 7800 compatible with the Supercharger and the Activision games again. So this is not a very reliable method of detecting 7800s."* 〔stella-list `200409/msg00112`, `msg00114`, `msg00115`〕. Earlier in the same thread Edwin Blink had proposed the other test: *"a signature check (or crc/checksum) can be done on ram. Because the 7800 copies 128 bytes code to ram with the 2600 swiching code"*; then, having found a 4K NTSC and a 16K PAL BIOS, *"The first 100 bytes are identical for both versions"*; and its limit, *"the test won't work when using a supercharger, cuttle cart or other cart that overwrites Ram before the test can be made"* 〔`200409/msg00086`, `msg00095`〕. The bytes such a test reads, `$D0` = `$2C` and `$D1` = `$A9`, and a menu that rewrites them, are in the row "A cartridge that runs a menu first hands the game a console that is not at power-up" in §D; a 2009 thread gives the same compare and runs it right after power-up only (AtariAge `topic/147537`, held here only as distilled notes). Cited only, not verified. Cited only, not verified — no 7800 here. Under the last-bus-byte model the byte before `LDA $07`'s read is its operand, `$07`, so the six low bits come back as `#$07`'s — derived, Not verified. This engine has the 2600 branch only: `memory.go` ORs in `mem.LastCPUData & ^mem.DataBusDriven`, or a random pattern under `RandomPins` (`Random.Rewindable(0xff)`), and has no setting in which those bits read 0 (read from the source). `litmus_floatbits` measures the first (the bus-residue table near the top of this file). So a ROM that passes here by reading those bits has met one of the two machines — derived. | stella-list `200211/msg00118` (Eckhard Stolberg); `200409/msg00112`, `msg00114`, `msg00115` (Aaron, Edwin Blink, Eckhard Stolberg); `200409/msg00086`, `msg00095` (Edwin Blink); AtariAge `topic/147537` (e1will's thread; distilled notes, the code's poster not recorded) |
| **`save_state`/`restore_state` is bit-exact for the picture and NOT for the sound** | Two causes, additive, and probing each in the vendored engine accounts for all of it (`tia_pcm.bin`, save at a frame boundary, 20-frame detour: 34 → 33 → 1 → **0**). **`Television.audioSignals`**: the TV batches one sample-set per scanline and flushes at `TotalScanlines` (`television.go:489-507`), but the field lives on `Television` and **not on `State`**, so the partial batch is neither saved nor restored. **`Audio.sampleSum`**: `audio.go`'s `Snapshot()` is `n := *au`, so the snapshot shares the averager's backing array; 20 of the 92 `Snapshot` functions under `hardware/` call `copy()`, so deep-copying is the convention and Audio is the exception — blast radius one averaging window, hence exactly one sample. **★How bad depends on WHERE you saved, and the first write-up of this got that wrong by measuring one ROM at one position.** Swept over 3 ROMs × 4 save positions: **at a frame boundary the damage is a short head** (6, 19 or 34 samples) **and heals**; **one scanline later it is not a head at all** and reaches the end of the capture — worst measured **530 of 786 samples, last at 780**. | **Save at a frame boundary and discard the head, or do not restore when measuring sound.** "Discard the first N" is a workaround only for a frame-boundary save. Both probes were applied, measured and reverted — the vendored engine has never been modified here, which is what lets "our engine does X" mean "upstream does X". `internal/emu/audiosnapshot_test.go` asserts the defect, asserts that a boundary save stays a head (so the advice above keeps earning its place), and asserts that a mid-frame save does not. ★**And a snapshot holds no inputs.** quohog, rewinding Stella to the frame before a rare bug: *"when I step forward the bug never appears because as I step through I'm not hitting any inputs, let alone the right input on the right frame"*; Thomas Jentzsch: *"Stella has no input recording. But you can use savestateif to save an extra state whenever you want (e.g. when a certain input happens)"* (AtariAge `topic/330559`; Cited only, not verified). Same here: nothing in `cmd/harness` records `set_input` calls (`rg -i 'log\|history\|record' cmd/harness/*.go` finds nothing about input), so a state reached by hand-driven input replays forward without it. A scenario's `inputs` timeline (`docs/scenarios.md`) is the replayable form. | engine read (mailing-list distillation, helper-2, who predicted the 1-sample half before it was run) + measured 2026-09-05; the missing inputs AtariAge `topic/330559` |
| **the audio clock is FREE-RUNNING, and it is not a picture coordinate** | `hardware/tia/audio/audio.go` keeps its own `clock228`, advances it by **3 per `Step()`** and wraps it at 228; measured 2026-09-05, the identifier appears **nowhere else in the engine** (`rg clock228 Gopher2600` → 6 hits, all in that one file), the `Audio` type has **no `Reset` and no HSYNC hook** (8 methods, none of them), and nothing outside ever writes it. It lines up with the scanline only because a line is also 228 colour clocks, so **anything that changes a line's length — `RSYNC`, a desynced frame — shifts the audio phase against the picture permanently**. Two events per line each: the waveform advances (`phase0`) at clock228 **9** and **81**, and the volume average closes (`phase1`) at **36** and **150** — 114 colour clocks = **38 CPU cycles** apart, which is the window `Vol0`/`Vol1` are averaged over. `Snapshot()` is `n := *au`, a value copy, so save/restore **does** carry the phase. Consequence for authoring a measurement: compare **two runs of the same instruction stream**, never an absolute position within a line. Same family as "a frame index is not a machine quantity" — a quantity that looks synchronised to the picture and is not. ★**The objects are the picture-side twin.** An emulator author working from the schematics, 2018, testing `RSYNC` mid-line: *"I forgot this only resets the Hclock and nothing else. That means it immediately skews the sprite clocks by whatever amount of visible area was in play. I was painting the ball down the middle of the screen and RSYNC in the middle of that which threw it all over the place on the next line"*; Stella's TIA author answered *"From the effects that you describe, your RSYNC implementation sounds correct"* (AtariAge `topic/279416`; Cited only, not verified) | engine read + measured 2026-09-05; found by the mailing-list distillation (helper-2) |
| **software-synthesised sound can be broken by the emulator's resampling, not by the ROM** | John Saeger (z26), 2001: *"On the 2600 you essentially have infinite resolution and choices of sample rate when outputting sound manually. Trying to remix a sound with an arbitrary sample rate with a sound card that is running at the standard TIA sample rate can sound very bad"* — in z26 *"Pitfall II took some serious \*cheating\*"*, because *"When I relied on the two sample per scanline standard output, it sounded terrible."* Our engine has that standard output: volume is averaged over 38-cycle windows, two per line (row above; `internal/emu/audvtwice_test.go`), so a ROM that writes `AUDV` faster than that is heard here through the same averaging. Whether that makes any given ROM sound wrong here is Not verified — but a software-synth ROM that sounds bad here is evidence about the instrument before it is evidence about the ROM. ★A 2017 case with a different cause — the emulator again, but its frame rather than its resampling: a beginner's digitised-sound ROM had *"2 or 3 little breaks each time it cycles through fom high to low"* in Stella 4.7.3, while SpiceWare's recording *"Sounds OK"* (*"That was on my 2600"*) and on the author's Harmony *"it worked correctly"*. stephena: *"in Stella 4.7.3, the scanline count is all over the place, which could account for the sound being off"*; 5.0.0-pre6 ran *"a consistent 342 scanlines"* with *"no 'gaps'"*, *"But it's still not perfect"* (AtariAge `topic/264918`; Cited only, not verified). | stella-list `200109/msg00335` (John Saeger); AtariAge `topic/264918` (SpiceWare, stephena) |
| **ARM code in a DPC+ cartridge takes time an emulator may not charge** | SpiceWare, in his DPC+ARM series: Stella at the time counted ARM code as taking no 6507 time, so a routine could run in emulation and jitter or crash on a console; his check was to save `INTIM` at the end of vertical blank and of overscan, show it in the score, and compare the two machines (Cited only, not verified). A 2025 report of a DPC+ game bouncing on a **7800+** through a Harmony Encore is not an example of it: the same thread says the + consoles run the ROM in Stella, and only the playfield moved while other parts of the picture held still, which a frame running long would not do. **Our engine is not in that position by default**: Gopher2600 counts ARM cycles (`hardware/preferences/arm_preferences.go`: `Immediate` false, `CycleRegulator` 1.0); whether its counts match a real Harmony is Not verified. | AtariAge blog `11964` (SpiceWare, DPC+ARM part 10); AtariAge `topic/384353` |
| **2-voice audio phase interference** | two voices can partially cancel to near-silence; Gopher2600's noise (old TIASound) differs from real HW. ★**The "old TIASound" is not what this engine runs now.** `Gopher2600/hardware/tia/audio/doc.go`: *"Originally, Gopher2600 used Ron Fries' audio implementation but this has now been completely superceded by the work of Chris Brenner."* The TIASound tables left in `polynomials.go` are read by nothing (`CHANGELOG.md`, the `golden_mix` entry; `internal/emu/audiomix.go`). One of them is the 9-bit polynomial, filled from `rand.Int()` under TIASound's own comment *"Rather than have a table with 511 entries, I use a random number generator"* — the shortcut Adam Wozniak met in 2003: *"the emulator uses random numbers for the 9 bit polynomial and I want to know the real polynomial for an actual TIA"* 〔stella-list `200311/msg00124`; which emulator he read is not named; Cited only, not verified〕. Whether the noise here now matches a console is not measured — Not verified. ★**The phase between two voices on one note stays put until something disturbs it, and it can be disturbed on purpose.** Paul Slocum, 2003, in his music programming guide: *"When you play the same note on both voices simultaneously you'll get interesting phasing effects. Once you start playing a simultaneous note on both voices, the phasing will stay the same until you switch notes. However, I've found that if you set the pitch on one voice to a random value for a few cycles and then set it back to the correct value, it will shift the phase."* The auto high-hat of his driver, put on the same channel, does the same, and *"Just be aware that phasing can sometimes briefly cancel out the sound completely."* 〔stella-list `200302/msg00208`〕 (Cited only, not verified: not run on this engine or a console) | 294766, 272769, 326549; stella-list `200311/msg00124` (Adam Wozniak), `200302/msg00208` (Paul Slocum); `Gopher2600/hardware/tia/audio/doc.go`, `polynomials.go` |
| **two voices at high volume squash each other — the output stage is compressive** | Thomas Jentzsch, 2017, from improving Stella's sound: *"the TIA volume is not linear (by far). E.g. a volume value of 5 6 is exactly 50% as loud as a value of 15"* (his `5 6`), and *"the non-linearity affects both channels combined. This means that the combined volume of both channels is compressed too. E.g. a combined volume value of 10 is exactly 50% as loud as a combined value of 30, a volume of 15 is exactly 2/3 as loud as 30"*, so *"If e.g. one wave is a full peak (volume = 15) the other wave at full peak would only add 50% of what it would add if played alone."* He names Ms. Pac-Man and E.T. as sounding *"pretty distorted on real hardware"*, both running both channels at 15, and gives the rule *"When you create sounds and especially music and want to avoid those distortions, reduce the volume"* — *"IMO the distortions are still quite noticeable with volume 10, but volume 6 sounds OK for me."* DirtyHairy posted the mixing equation in the same thread, with *"R_0 = 30, R_1 = 1"*; the equation itself is not in the copy held here. Our engine has that curve: `mix.Mono` builds its table as `int16(0x7fff * float32(vol) / float32(maxVolume) * (30 + 1*float32(maxVolume)) / (30 + 1*float32(vol)))`, with `maxVolume = 0x1e` and `vol` the two channels' sum, and `internal/emu/mixnonlinear_test.go` measures channel 0 at volume 15 contributing 10922 alone and 5461 with channel 1 also at 15 — the 50% Jentzsch states. The engine's table is that same curve (its header credits Jentzsch's optimisation of Chris Brenner's analysis), so the agreement is not an independent check of the hardware (AtariAge `topic/271920`; Cited only, not verified). Hear a two-voice mix with `golden_mix`; `golden_audio` hashes channel 0 alone. | AtariAge `topic/271920` (Thomas Jentzsch, DirtyHairy); `internal/emu/mixnonlinear_test.go`; `Gopher2600/hardware/tia/audio/mix/mix.go` |
| **AUDF-change propagation latency** | TIA tone comes from shift registers and dividers, not a table — Adam Wozniak's 2003 software model (sound.c) uses three LFSRs, 4-, 5- and 9-stage (taps `[4 3]`, `[5 3]`, `[9 5]`), and three dividers (÷2, ÷6, and ÷31 with a 13:18 duty), and each AUDC value picks a combination — AUDC 3 chains the 5- and the 4-stage, AUDC 8 is the 9-stage alone 〔stella-list `200312/msg00053`; Cited only, not verified; `pkg/audio` has AUDC 8 as a 511-bit poly〕; **lowering AUDF can take up to ~32 cycles (~1ms)** before the next output clock (the up-counter must wrap) — pitch changes lag, don't assume instant | blog 1116/1134/1140; stella-list `200312/msg00053` (Adam Wozniak) |
| **mid-line NUSIZ double/quad copy trick** | renders on real HW but **not in Gopher2600** → ROMs using it won't pixel-match our oracle | 181903 |
| **player-width change → 1-clk right shift** | changing NUSIZ player width shifts start by 1 color-clock (missile/ball unaffected) | 143781, 64251, 290654 |
| **the Cosmic Ark stars' double- and zero-width pixels are a clock glitch, and the engine reproduces them by rule** | crispy, 2017, building a TIA on an FPGA: the starfield's extra-clock half *"is well understood"*; the mystery was why it *"occasionally produces two clock wide and zero clock wide pixels"*. From the schematic, *"the clock for the missile logic is formed by ORing the motion clock with an inverted copy of the extra clocks"*. He timed the TIA's READY and XTAL pins on his scope, estimated the delay through one gate from that, and *"calculated that the motion clock line goes low 21.5 ns before the inverted extra clocks line goes high, and the resulting glitch has a pulse width long enough to create a valid clock edge for the missile logic"*. In his FPGA simulation the early edge gives a double-wide pixel one clock early when the missile was about to produce one, and no pixel when one was already at the latch input (AtariAge `topic/261596`; Cited only, not verified). The 21.5 ns is a calculation from an estimate, not a reading of the glitch itself. Gopher2600 models no gate delay: `missile.go` carries the effect as two conditions, `earlyStart` and `earlyEnd`, whose comment says they *"are fully explained in the AtariAge post "Cosmic Ark Star Field Revisited" by crispy"* (read from the source, Not verified). The 17-pixel shift from the same post is in `design-principles.md`; the revision difference is `LostMOTCK`, in §D's TIA-revision row. ★An earlier FPGA builder saw the starfield at the missile's clock too: ehenciak, 2005, before he had added the missile logic, *"when running Cosmic Ark, I see player 0's missile clock enable stay high for the starfield effect"* — a signal in his own model built from the schematics, not a console reading (AtariAge `topic/64309`; Cited only, not verified). | AtariAge `topic/261596` (crispy), `topic/64309` (ehenciak); `Gopher2600/hardware/tia/video/missile.go` |
| **The VSYNC "≥2 lines" threshold is an engine setting, and its default is the answer** | `television.go` decides a frame synced with `vsync.activeScanlineCount >= env.Prefs.TV.VSYNCscanlines`, and `preferences/television.go` sets that to **2** in `SetDefaults`. Nothing here changes it (`rg 'VSYNCscanlines' internal cmd pkg` → 0 hits). **A litmus that sweeps 0..4 VSYNC lines and reports "1 fails, 2 passes" has confirmed the number we fed it, not the hardware** — and this is worse than the SuperChip case above, because there the feature is off and the green looks odd, while here the default *matches the literature* so the green looks like corroboration. What is measurable is the shape (does a step exist, and only one?), not the threshold; a Go-side control that sets `VSYNCscanlines` to 3 and shows the step move is what makes the input visible. | engine source, measured 2026-09-03 |
| **SuperChip phantom reads are OFF by default in our engine** | Gopher2600 models the SARA phantom-read recovery (`mapper_atari.go`, `saraCycles = 2`, guarded by `cart.env.Prefs.Cartridge.EmulateSARA`), but `preferences/cartridge_preferences.go` sets **`EmulateSARA` to `false`** in `SetDefaults`, and nothing in this repo sets it (`rg 'EmulateSARA|SARA' internal cmd pkg` → 0 hits). **A litmus written for phantom reads today would come back green because the feature is off, not because the hardware behaves** — it would be measuring our own default. Pin the preference explicitly and say so, or record the behaviour as not modelled. The write/read port split ($F000-$F07F write-only, $F080-$F0FF read-only) needs no preference and *is* measurable as-is. | engine source, measured 2026-09-03 |
| **state isn't all in RAM** | sprite X positions live in TIA internal counters → full-state capture needs `read_tia_registers`, not just `read_ram` | 301766 |
| **With no program writing it, the TIA keeps drawing — and sounding — what its registers last held** | Nukey Shay, 2019, on whether the playfield must be cleared at the end of a kernel: *"If you are going to have an "empty loop" of WSYNCs to fill the rest, the uncleared registers will continue to spill whatever values were last written"* (`design-principles.md` carries that part), and *"Incidentally, this is why vertical bars appear onscreen whenever you turn a console on without a cartridge. There's no program to instruct the console what to do, so it just spills the garbage data existing in the registers over and over (usually with an annoying audio tone, because there's no program present changing those registers either."* (AtariAge `topic/295514`). tschak909, 2016, describing how the period's developers worked — his own words for the post are *"I tried to provide a contextual bridge"* — gives the same reason for what a crash looked like: *"a dev back in the day hitting pause on the dev box, would cause the whole screen to go black, and crashes were easy enough to see, because any crash would cause the screen to streak in interesting ways (because the TIA write registers are latched, they keep their last value, until changed)"* (AtariAge `topic/253565`, a copy holding 18 of its 20 posts). Both Cited only, not verified. So steady bars or streaks with a constant tone say first that the CPU stopped writing the TIA — it never started, or it hung — and only then anything about the kernel; a 2019 report of a ROM that would not start on a Flashback 9, *"a black screen with some random graphics at the top of the screen and a buzzing noise"* (AtariAge `topic/296200`), has that shape — derived, Not verified. | AtariAge `topic/295514` (Nukey Shay), `topic/253565` (tschak909), `topic/296200` (drgeek) |
| smooth horizontal PF scroll | **hardware-impossible** (RSYNC won't help); use ball/missile edge or delayed/tile scroll | 178574 |
| **the delayed scroll is Thrust's, and its counter-example is named** | The prescription in the row above has a source. Thomas Jentzsch, 2002, to Paul Slocum planning a side-by-side split-screen game: *"I'd suggest "delayed scrolling" like I did in Thrust. Else I think, the horizontal scrolling would look crappy (like in Vanguard) and it might be a problem to scroll both screens vertical independently"* 〔stella-list `200205/msg00034`〕; a reader who had played Vanguard as a child asked what was crappy about it 〔`msg00035`〕, and Slocum's fallback if the scroll looked too choppy was flip screens *"like Adventure"* 〔`msg00036`〕. Cited only, not verified | stella-list `200205/msg00034`-`00036` |
| **a playfield step finer than 4 colour clocks is a television question** | The "hardware-impossible" above holds for the playfield registers, whose pixel is 4 colour clocks wide; one report says a picture can still be moved by less, with a signal outside the standard, and that whether it shows depends on the set. iesposta, 2018, counting in colour clocks (*"a 1/2 pixel scroll or 2 color clocks, instead of the usual 4 Color clock wide playfield pixel"*): *"Fine 1-pixel horizontal play field scrolling was shown to work, however only on normal CRT televisions. Ones like SONY WEGA that processed the signal a lot \would only scroll 3 or 2 of the 4 pixels"*, and *"I think a 1/2 pixel scroll would work on most if not all displays"*. eshu's 1-clock demo worked on a Zenith and his later 2-clock version on the WEGA. iesposta did not add the half step to his own games because they *"are really pushing the display per scan line without having to worry about moving the screen a 1/2 playfield pixel by outputting an even more off-spec signal"*. The bus-stuffing demo the thread is about scrolled in whole playfield pixels, *"just like Scramble Arcade and Super Cobra Arcade"* (AtariAge `topic/273955`; Cited only, not verified). This harness does not model the television, so such a step cannot be checked here | AtariAge `topic/273955` (iesposta) |
| PAL: odd-scanline color / interlace | PAL color circuitry fails on odd scanlines; line-dropping kills color. ★An emulator that models the loss counts whole lines only. Eckhard Stolberg, 2002, after naming z26's `-o` and Stella's PAL mode as the two that simulate it: *"Unfortunately the emulators only count full scanlines, while on real VCSs the problem might occur on different spots of unfinished scanlines as well. So, if you have to use the emulator, you should have the jump to the start of a new frame happen right after a sta WSYNC at the end of the frame."* Cited only, not verified. ★**Read the other way, the rule sorts ROMs by region.** Albert Yarusso, 2002, asked whether the existing Air Raid (290 lines) and Great Escape (288) binaries were PAL or NTSC: *"At what point is a game considered PAL?"* John Saeger added counts of his own — zoofun 291, dumbo 286, curtiss 286, tps 285, galaga 282, tomboy 277 — and *"I draw the PAL line at 282 scanlines. I guess because of galaga."* Andrew Davie: *"We could, IMHO, safely assume all with an odd number of scanlines to be NTSC. IIRC, PAL will display a B&W image with an odd scanline count."*, asking to be confirmed or corrected; Thomas Jentzsch, of zoofun, tps and tomboy: *"All those games must be NTSC, because with an odd number of lines, PAL displays only shades of grey."* (his emphasis on *must* dropped). Eckhard Stolberg judged by colour where the count could not: *"Games in the 280 line range are a bit difficult to judge, as they could be for either format"*, and in Air Raid *"The sky is blue (purple with a PAL palette) and the Men-A-Vision logo is yellow, which is a colour that would show as grey in PAL"* 〔`200202/msg00046`, `200202/msg00047`, `200202/msg00048`, `200202/msg00049`, `200202/msg00052`〕. Cited only, not verified. An even count decides nothing by this rule — our reading. This engine does not model the loss at all (configuration surface, row 10) | 124445, 200197, 293881; stella-list `200206/msg00114`; `200202/msg00046`-`00052` (Albert Yarusso, John Saeger, Andrew Davie, Eckhard Stolberg, Thomas Jentzsch) |
| **a long first frame can make an emulator call an NTSC ROM PAL** | An emulator guesses the TV standard from the length of the first frames, and the guess overrides what the author set. A 2019 report of Stella 6.0 showing an NTSC game as PAL; Thomas Jentzsch: *"Probably your initial frames are closer to PAL than to NTSC. Then Stella auto detects PAL"*, and *"This is not a workaround, but a design feature. Else users would have to manually change the settings for all PAL games"*; SpiceWare read it as too much work before the first frame was drawn, and the author's cure was a playfield-clearing call — *"A simple PFCLEAR fixed it"* — which, jeffjetton confirmed, emits a `WSYNC`. No ROM was posted, so the cause is a guess in the thread (Cited only, not verified). **This engine has the same mechanism with a different window.** Given `"AUTO"`, `television.go` switches to PAL when a frame runs past `PALTrigger` = **302** lines (`specification/specifications.go`), but only while `stableFrames` is above `leadingFrames` (1) and below `stabilityThreshold` (6) — so the first synced frame is never judged, and a single long start-up frame may not trip it. `cmd/harness` defaults to `"NTSC"`; `cmd/dissect`, `cmd/rammap` and `cmd/fieldtest` pass `"AUTO"`. Engine read — whether a long start-up frame of ours reaches that window is Not verified. ★**Neither guess looks at the picture.** jeffjetton, reading Stella's source in the same thread with C++ he called *"a bit rusty"*: *"I think it does in fact count lines between VSYNCs"* (Cited only, not verified). In this engine both tests are on the line count alone — `tv.state.scanline > specification.PALTrigger` to switch to PAL and `<=` to switch back — and nothing drawn enters them, so two start-up frames of the same length get the same answer whatever they show — derived, Not verified. ★**The guess is made at start-up only, here as in the Stella of 2017.** JAC!, adding an NTSC toggle to a PAL game: *"Now I generate 262 scanlines, but Stella runs it as 60HZ PAL... Any ideas?"*; after a reply whose text the held copy has lost, stephena: *"Yes, that's true. Perhaps in a future release of Stella this will be monitored, and can happen automatically"*, and JAC!: *"wasn't aware it's not automatic already during execution"* — he made three batch files that pass Stella the setting on the command line, `-tv BW` in the one he showed (AtariAge `topic/266766`; Cited only, not verified). In this engine the window above has the same effect: a ROM past 302 lines in its first synced frames is switched to PAL under `"AUTO"` and the run goes on under the PAL specification, while an overrun later in a run switches nothing — derived from the engine read, Not verified | AtariAge `topic/286875` (winkdot, Thomas Jentzsch, SpiceWare, jeffjetton); `topic/266766` (JAC!, stephena); `Gopher2600/hardware/television/television.go` (engine read, including the two `PALTrigger` tests) |
| **on the 2600+, PAL60 is said to depend on a database keyed by the ROM's MD5** | PAL60 runs NTSC timing with PAL colours, so a guess from frame length (row above) sees NTSC — derived, Not verified. splendidnut, 2025: *"PAL60 is NTSC video timing (262 scanlines @ 60hz) using color values that target a PAL TIA chip."*, and *"On the 2600+, there is no 'set' TIA chip, so it's completely reliant on what Stella detects. Stella has a game database that includes information for TV type. That database is keyed off a MD5 hash of the ROM. For any project to be properly detected as PAL60, it would need an entry in that game database to specify that the TV type for the game is PAL60.... So the 2600+/7800+ firmware would need updates as new games come out."* — with his own warning: *"That is all based on my current understanding of how things work... so please take it with a grain of salt."* (AtariAge `topic/383581`; Cited only, not verified). | AtariAge `topic/383581` (splendidnut) |
| **on a 2600+, a cartridge has to answer a dumper, not a running game** | Philsan, testing with the UnoCart+, on a homebrew DPC+ game: *"at the moment DPC+/CDFJ games don't work"*, suggesting its author ask for support in a firmware update. al_nafuur: *"Since the cartridges of the 2600+ devices are only "storage devices", you don't really need to support the banking on the storage. You only need to support the dumper algorithm to get your ROM file to the Stella emulator."* So a pass on a 2600+ says the image was dumped and Stella ran it — derived, Not verified; the "Emulators can agree with each other" row quotes verdant on the same console (*"dumps the ROM to internal storage and then emulates it from there"*) (AtariAge `topic/384353`; Cited only, not verified; the posts' dates are not in the held copy). | AtariAge `topic/384353` (Philsan, al_nafuur) |
| **telling NTSC, PAL and SECAM consoles apart by timing a frame needs a clock four times finer than their difference** | A cartridge with its own processor can time a frame against its own clock. SpiceWare's Draconian splash screen does it for one frame with the Harmony ARM's timer, thresholds half-way between the counts: *"We had a number of people test it and while there were some variations the results the type of console was consistently detected."* TomSon, whose cartridge runs on an STM32F4 (his post survives here only as a quotation inside a reply): *"The clock rates I see are all within a 1% error margin (PAL/SECAM/NTSC). To put this into perspective: the internal clock accuracy of modern uProcs -the STM32F4 again- is 1%."* Thomas Jentzsch: *"The frequency difference between NTSC and PAL is about 0.9% and SECAM is in the middle. So the error margin should be lower than 0.225% (0.9%/4)"* — the thread does not say why four — and *"Probably the ARM used in Harmony has a higher accuracy."* TomSon: *"Nonetheless .2% isn't really low, remember that the precision of both the uProc and the 30+ year old clock generator have to be taken into account"*; *"'on average' this will work better than no detection whatsoever"*, *"Just not a 100% reliable one, until proven."* SpiceWare ran no call for testing *"because I felt the menu option as a fallback was good enough"* (AtariAge `topic/266200`; Cited only, not verified). ★**A console with no second clock was once offered a different difference.** Eckhard Stolberg, 2001, to someone trying to time a frame from the console itself: *"Detection routines based on the frame timing won't work, because both consoles should react the same to whatever frame you make them draw."* His candidate instead was the `HMOVE` window: *"In most cases the positions will be different from the normal positions, but the NTSC and PAL consoles will still give the same predictable results. But in Kool Aide Man the score display positioning routine accesses the registers in such a wierd way, that on all my PAL consoles the players will be positioned partly on top of each other … On the NTSC consoles the game seems to work normally though. So I think you could use this positioning trick to detect the console type."* He named its limits in the same post — *"I'm not sure if it really would work reliably with all of them"*, *"I'm not sure how SECAM consoles would react. And the emulators would also fail this test and react similar to the PAL consoles"* — and four days later put the difference down to Kool Aide Man touching at least two position registers where z26's tests had changed one (*"This is what I think causes the problem."*); the one-register limit, in his January 2002 words, is in §D's row on emulator agreement 〔`200107/msg00063`, `200107/msg00096`〕. Cited only, not verified: no PAL console here, and whether this engine reproduces the two-register case is not checked. ★**Three years later a test for the TIA itself.** Thomas Jentzsch, 2004: *"while working a little bit on my invaders demo, I wrote this little piece of code, which identifies if a console is "Kool-Aid-Man-compatible" or not"* — `P1` and `M0` enabled, `RESM0` then `RESP1` then `HMOVE` on one line (*"HMOVE during RESPx"*), `CXCLR`, one line's wait, and `CXM0P` read: bit 7 set means compatible. *"It works on my consoles (I have an incompatible Chinese Jr), maybe you can verify this. The attached program switches to a red background if your console is compatible."* No one else's result is in the thread as held here 〔stella-list `200410/msg00067`〕; Eckhard Stolberg's account of the mechanism, from the same thread, is in `design-principles.md` 〔`200410/msg00080`〕. Cited only, not verified: nothing here runs it, and this engine's TIA revision quirks are switched off (§D's row on the ten litmus ROMs). | AtariAge `topic/266200` (SpiceWare, TomSon, Thomas Jentzsch); stella-list `200107/msg00063`, `200107/msg00096` (Eckhard Stolberg); `200410/msg00067` (Thomas Jentzsch) |
| **A 7800 runs its own code on the TIA and RIOT before a 2600 cartridge starts, and one of its tests reads a skipped byte** | GroovyBee, 2011, posted the 7800 BIOS routine that runs once a 2600 cartridge is detected, *"which is copied into RIOT RAM"*: *"This code must all run out of 6532 RAM since the memory map will change as we change mode. The 6532 RAM exists in all memory maps."* It zeroes the TIA in a loop that *"counts up so that graphics are turned off before the collision registers are cleared"*, then places both players at fixed cycles over the playfield and reads the collisions to decide whether *"we must shift the clock once"* or twice. Its comments name states *case A*, *case C* and *case E* without defining them in the posted part. One test uses the CPU's own fetch: *"In some E cases, TWSYNCs do not work properly and this must be detected. In an E case failure, the LDA is prefetched and the 04 following it is skipped over and NOP ($EA) is loaded into A causing the branch on minus to be taken"*, and *"All E cases do not get trapped here and must be handled latter by collisions"* (`latter` as written). This harness models a 2600 with no BIOS, so none of it runs here (AtariAge `topic/184535`; Cited only, not verified — no 7800 here). ★**What that code leaves behind tells the consoles apart.** batari, 2005, answering nukey-shay's question whether a program can tell a 2600 from a 7800: *"on real hardware, the 7800 starts 2600 mode in a known, predictable state but the 2600 itself does not. On the 7800, memory contents are constant when booting 2600 mode"*, with a dump of `$80`-`$FF` that begins `A9 00 AA 85 01 95 03 E8 E0 2A D0 F9` and ends in a run of `$FF`. His advice: check *"2-3 bytes at the very beginning of your game before you do anything else"*, leave them unchanged so that a reset still finds them, and *"avoid the $FF's or $00's"*. Bruce Tomlin then posted the BIOS source, which moves the routine *"into RIOT RAM at $0480"*, the RAM that also answers at `$80`; its first instructions, `LDA #0 / TAX / STA 1 / STA 3,X / INX / CPX #$2A / BNE`, are those bytes, so what a 2600 game finds in RAM on a 7800 is that routine (bytes matched by hand here, Not verified on hardware). Tested in the thread: nukey-shay's routine was *"burned and tested … using a 6-switch and an original model 7800. It works"*, and batari's test binary was reported to detect correctly on a CC2 with a 7800 and, burned to a cartridge, on *"a 2600 six switch, 2600 jr, standard 7800, dev OS 7800 and standard PAL 7800"*. An emulator that never put the values there reads as a 2600, which nukey-shay took as the safe default. He had made the same point in 2007, to a beginner, as a check worth adding before the RAM-clearing loop (AtariAge `topic/109402`, held here only as distilled notes). The RAM test had been put on the stella list in 2004, the year before: Edwin Blink suggested a signature check on the 128 bytes the 7800 copies to RAM, found with a hex editor and a BIOS disassembly that *"The first 100 bytes are identical for both versions"* of the NTSC and PAL BIOS, and then that *"the test won't work when using a supercharger, cuttle cart or other cart that overwrites Ram before the test can be made"* 〔stella-list `200409/msg00086`, `msg00095`; Cited only, not verified〕. What ran before the game can change the answer — the menu-cartridge row in §D, and the pause-switch note in `design-principles.md`, where the Harmony's BIOS version decides it (AtariAge `topic/63710`; Cited only, not verified — no 7800 here). | AtariAge `topic/184535` (GroovyBee); `topic/63710` (batari, Bruce Tomlin, nukey-shay, mitch); `topic/109402` (nukey-shay); stella-list `200409/msg00086`, `msg00095` (Edwin Blink) |

---

**Use:** before shipping a kernel, walk this list; the static-detectable rows are the first targets for
`scripts/check_traps.py`. The runtime rows become `breakif`/scenario asserts. For Pong specifically, the
A-section (timing/sync) is the historical cause of every past abandonment.
